- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Grouping is engineered model data: an optional `group` field on `Signal`, following the same schema-optional convention as `unit`/`description`. `SignalIndex` carries the group through per point, the `dcs-model` CLI reports group counts, and the page groups its listing by it — ungrouped signals landing under the documented `"ungrouped"` default group.
- **Alternatives considered:** Deriving groups from device ids or `PointId` ranges (physical device topology is not the operator's plant-area structure); a page-side grouping configuration (a second authority for engineered data, off the single contract); name-prefix conventions like `AREA.SIGNAL` (unvalidated, and conflates a signal's identity with its organization).
- **Consequences:** UI organization becomes diffable, validated model data — the page needs no per-plant code, and future consumers get the same grouping for free. The field is display metadata only: it constrains nothing the controller does, keeping the schema addition cheap and backward compatible.
- **Affected tickets:** #89 (this record); #86 implemented it; relies on #23 (signal index) and #34 (metadata serving); decision 3 supplies the versioning rule.

### 24. Plant-side dynamics as declared data

- **Status:** Implemented (#81): `dcs-plant-server --dynamics FILE` merges a plant-side dynamics document — a JSON list of `ProcessElement` declarations in the `ChannelMap` vocabulary (`first_order_lag`, `second_order_lag`, `integrator`, `dead_time`, `noise`, `bool_flow`, `flow_sum`, `scaled_flow`, `threshold`) — into the resolved `sim_channel_map`, validating each element as it merges so a rejection names the element and the point it drives. The document deliberately carries no `version`: it is a plain declaration list beside the model, and widening the element vocabulary extends it the way `ChannelMap` itself evolves. Basis: the `ProcessElement` vocabulary — `FirstOrderLag` and `Integrator` (#8), `DeadTime` (#37), `SecondOrderLag` (#135), `Noise` (#156), `BoolFlow` and `FlowSum` (#200), `ScaledFlow` (#252), `Threshold` (#309) — stepped deterministically by `SimDriver::step` and checkpointed as element accumulators (#21); `dcs-assembly::sim_channel_map`'s open seam (#19); `PlantServer` serving a map's elements to remote drivers (#31). The Bool-gated and summed variants widen the vocabulary past SISO Float ends: a `bool_flow`'s gate is the one `Bool` element input, and `flow_sum`'s declared `inputs` list is the vocabulary's one multi-input shape — `ProcessElement::input` reports `Option<PointId>` for the single-input variants while `inputs` enumerates every variant, so actuator commands close a simulated loop without new stepping machinery. The second-order lag's recorded parameterization is `time_constant` (τ = 1/ωₙ) plus `damping_ratio` (ζ) — the standard `τ² y'' + 2ζτ y' + y = u` form, chosen over a two-time-constant cascade because ζ < 1 produces the overshoot the parameter-tuning path needs — stepped by the exact zero-order-hold discretization like the first-order lag's exact update. The noise element's recorded parameterization is `amplitude` plus `seed`: each `Good`-input step draws once from a splitmix64 generator — the `u64` state advanced by the golden-ratio increment, then the standard mix, the top 53 bits scaled into [0, 1) — and outputs `u + amplitude·(2x − 1)`, holding the output within `u ± amplitude`. A seeded generator was chosen over a declared disturbance table because it gives an unbounded, exactly replayable deviation sequence without authoring data: identical seeds replay identical sequences, distinct seeds produce distinct ones, and the carried `u64` state checkpoints and restores mid-sequence like every element accumulator. The scaled flow's recorded naming is `scaled_flow`/`ScaledFlow` (#252): `y = gain · u` re-evaluated each step, a `Float` command scaled into the signed rate downstream `flow_sum`/`integrator` elements consume — negative gains declaring draws as well as feeds — added so a document can express "drain proportional to the Float speed demand", which `bool_flow`'s fixed on/off rates and `flow_sum`'s verbatim sum cannot; stateless like them, so `dt` never scales the output and `initial` covers reads before the first `Good` step. The threshold element's recorded naming is `threshold`/`Threshold` (#309), the vocabulary's `Float`→`Bool` shape — `bool_flow`'s mirror: a `Float` input driving a `Bool` contact that asserts and releases on declared `on`/`off` bounds, the gap between them the hysteresis band. Explicit bounds were recorded over a central threshold plus a width because the comparison direction is then unambiguous: `on > off` is a high trip asserting at `u ≥ on` and releasing strictly below `off`; `on < off` is a low trip asserting at `u ≤ on` and releasing strictly above `off`; equal bounds are rejected (`NonPositiveBand`) as a zero-width band. Its documented non-`Good` rule holds the standing contact and stamps the input's quality on the driven `Bool` point — chosen over deasserting so a measurement dropout neither spuriously trips nor clears a standing protection state — and its `initial` is the vocabulary's one `Bool` initial, covering reads before the first `Good` step. The element exists for level-triggered protective behavior — the WW-ALM-005 enabler: the contact gating a `bool_flow` emergency draw, expressed entirely in a dynamics document — where previously only a scheduled script could assert a Bool on a Float crossing.
- **Problem:** The simulated plant's behavior — which elements drive which points, their time constants, delays, and initial values — is real engineered content, but it lived in Rust call sites: `dcs-demo` built its `FirstOrderLag` inside `assemble`, and a shared plant server could not serve a declared plant without code. The obvious alternative — process elements inside `PlantModel` — would couple the control contract to testbench physics: a real plant has no such declaration, and the model's job is to describe the controller's view, not the world's.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Plant-side dynamics are declared data in a document *beside* the model, not schema *inside* it: a JSON list of `ProcessElement` declarations naming the points each element drives. `dcs-assembly`'s open `sim_channel_map` seam is the join — `dcs-plant-server` loads the model for the control contract and the dynamics document for the physics, merging them into the `ChannelMap` it serves. The plant model stays the single contract for control; the dynamics document is the testbench's account of the process it stands in for — engineered and validated, never read by a controller.
- **Alternatives considered:** Elements as `PlantModel` schema (the contract would carry simulation-only content real deployments cannot produce, and every consumer would have to know which sections are testbench); keeping dynamics in Rust constructors (the pre-#81 status quo — works for `dcs-demo`, but the shared plant server and every integration rig then need code to describe the same plant); folding dynamics into device `kind` strings (opaque, unvalidated, and duplicating point identity the model already owns); a versioned document like the model's (decision 3's discipline is for a long-lived cross-consumer contract — the dynamics list is simulation tooling's own format, so a bare declaration list suffices until it grows one).
- **Consequences:** The simulated plant is declaratively reproducible — `dcs-plant-server` and test rigs load the same document instead of reimplementing physics, and the scripted-scenario device kind (#67) gets a sibling pattern for declared behavior. The boundary stays explicit: the model describes what the controller sees, the dynamics document describes what the world does. Checkpointing already treats element accumulators as driver state (decision 13), so the redundancy path needs no change.
- **Affected tickets:** #89 (this record); #81 implemented it; #67 follows the same declared-behavior pattern; #200 widened the vocabulary with `bool_flow` and `flow_sum`; #252 added `scaled_flow` (requirement `WW-CTL-003` enabler — the dosing loop's demand-proportional drawdown and discharge rate); #309 added `threshold` (requirement `WW-ALM-005` enabler — the Float-to-Bool contact a level-triggered protection function needs). Implemented basis: #8, #12, #21, #31, #37.

### 25. Rolling model-revision deployment

- **Status:** Implemented (#87). The standby's checkpoint pull is `Peer::transfer`, which routes on the fingerprint negotiation (decision 27): a matching fingerprint applies as ordinary convergence exactly like `Peer::apply`; a foreign fingerprint on a peer armed by `dcs-controller --revised` (`Peer::with_revision`) crosses the model boundary through `Executor::reinitialize`, while an unarmed peer still degrades on the mismatch — a revision is a declared intent, never an assumption. The crossing classifies every checkpoint section before applying anything, so a refused revision leaves the revised run untouched: carried are writable internal `In` points matched by declared point id and value kind, `Out` image samples the revision still declares, and the force set under the same writability and kind rule; named dropped in the `dcs_core::CarryoverReport` are checkpointed points the revision does not serve, synthesized link points whose allocated ids are not declared identity, checkpointed component state whose name the revision does not register, and driver state — component and driver state always reinitialize — the per-kind compatibility extension this contract named is decided for descriptor-declared parameters under #564: they stay reinitialized by rule (see the chosen approach), and the report's `reverted_tuning` section itemizes per component, in declaration order, every declared parameter whose checkpointed value differed from the revision's declared default, naming both the lost checkpointed value and the standing default; per-kind compat rules for other component state remain the future seam. The armed peer reports `StandbySync::Reinitialized` carrying the report — source and destination fingerprints, resumed tick, the carried and dropped and initialized and reverted-tuning sets — journaled once per transition as `JournalEvent::Reinitialized`; `promote` and `self_promote` accept it beside `Tracking`, so the documented demote-then-promote order (decision 15) moves the field writer to the revised model at a scan boundary with exactly-one-writer preserved. A carryover break — a retyped carried point, an unserved force, an unsupported checkpoint version — is a named `CarryoverError` the peer reports as `Degraded`, and `POST /promote` answers `not_converged` with that state. The scripted rig is `crates/dcs-controller/tests/model_revision.rs`: a `dcs-plant-server` tank loop with a v1 active and a `--revised` v2 standby runs the full revise-reassemble-promote path asserting the named carryover outcomes, the breaking-change refusal before promotion, and bit-identical repeats. Under #563 the same arm extends to the decision-35 resume seam — the unattended-station deployment class `docs/research/deployment-commissioning.md` names, where no redundant peer exists to carry the run: `--revised` with `--state-file` and no `--standby` rolls a revised model on a lone controller through the scheduled outage the restart already is, routing the persisted checkpoint's foreign fingerprint through `Executor::reinitialize` in place of `Executor::apply` under the same classify-then-apply rule, resuming at the checkpointed tick with the crossing's `CarryoverReport` printed at startup and journaled per decision 36. A rule-breaking checkpoint fails startup naming the `CarryoverError` and leaves the state file untouched, an unarmed mismatch still refuses, and a matching fingerprint still applies ordinarily. The revised document still arrives by operator-placed path per decision 46 — a mutating model-load endpoint stays rejected — and the scripted legs are `crates/dcs-controller/tests/lone_revision.rs`.
- **Problem:** Plant engineering changes in service — a retyped point, a new component instance, rewired connections — produce a revised model the running pair must adopt without interrupting the process. A checkpoint cannot carry a run across model revisions: decision 11's strictness makes the mismatch a named rejection, which is correct for same-model peers but leaves no path for intentional change.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Model revision is a deliberate rolling deployment through the redundant pair, not a synchronization. The standby loads the revised model, validates and reassembles against it, and enters a named `reinitialized` state — distinct from `converged`, since no checkpoint carried it there. A documented carryover rule decides what crosses the model boundary: operator-writable internal points (decisions 14, 18) matched by declared identity carry their last values; component state reinitializes by default unless a documented per-kind compatibility rule applies; and every element that cannot carry over is named in a carryover report rather than silently dropped. Promotion then moves the field writer to the new-model instance at a scan boundary with demotion of the old peer (decision 15), preserving exactly-one-writer throughout; a revision that breaks a carryover rule fails before promotion with named errors. The per-kind compatibility seam is decided for descriptor-declared parameters (#564): they stay reinitialized by rule rather than carrying by (component name, parameter name, value kind). The requirement's runtime-tuning-continuity clause binds same-model promotion and restart, where the checkpoint carries tuning intact; a revision crossing is a deliberate engineered change, and the checkpoint's component section cannot distinguish a tuned value from a constructed default — carrying by identity would silently defeat the revising engineer's prerogative to re-default a parameter (a checkpointed `kp = 1.0`, tuned or not, would override the revision's deliberately declared `kp = 2.0`). The report therefore itemizes the reversion instead — `reverted_tuning` names each declared parameter whose checkpointed value differed from the revision's declared default, with both values — the witnessed change-management record of exactly what tuning was lost, while deliberate re-tuning re-enters through the receipted, journaled `set_parameter` path. A checkpointed parameter field whose kind the revision retyped itemizes as reverted like any differing value: the named-refusal convention binds the sections that carry, and nothing component-side crosses to be reinterpreted.
- **Alternatives considered:** Restart-based redeploy (interrupts the process — the vision rules it out); a lenient restore mapping old state onto new components field-by-field (silent divergence — exactly what decision 11's strictness exists to prevent); dual-running old and new revisions with output comparison (doubles the field-writer question and defines no merge for differing control logic).
- **Consequences:** In-service model change becomes an engineered, auditable operation: the carryover report is the record of what survived the revision — including, per reinitialized component, the declared parameters whose tuned values reverted — and `reinitialized` is an honest state the UI can display instead of a false convergence. Runtime tuning never survives a revision boundary implicitly: the revised model's declared defaults always win, the report names what was lost, and any tuning the revision intends to keep is either declared in the revised document or re-applied through the receipted path after the roll. Component kinds that want other state to survive a revision still document per-kind compatibility rules — the same obligation decision 20 puts on runtime tuning. The mechanism composes existing pieces — load, validate, assemble, promote — rather than adding a second deployment path.
- **Affected tickets:** #89 (this record); #87 implements it; #114 adds `dcs-model diff`, the human-facing review surface reporting what a revision adds, removes, and changes before the pair rolls it; #564 records the tuning-carry decision and implements the `reverted_tuning` itemization; #563 extends the arm to the decision-35 resume seam — the lone controller's scheduled-outage roll; relies on #46 (promotion), #66 (fingerprinted checkpoints), and #32 (standby machinery); decisions 10–15.

### 26. Standby divergence detection

- **Status:** Implemented (#88): `dcs-runtime`'s `Peer` stashes each non-field-owning scan's staged field `Out` image (`Executor::staged_field_outputs`, the writes the scan would have issued through the closed `WriteGate`) and, when an applied checkpoint's tick equals the stashed tick, compares it point by point against the peer's own reads of the same `Out` points through its driver surface (`divergence::compare_staged_points`). A mismatch moves the peer to the named `StandbySync::Diverged` carrying the `Divergence` evidence — point, staged value, field value — and `promote` refuses it with `SwitchError::NotConverged` carrying that state; the gate stays closed throughout. Both transitions are journaled — detection as `JournalEvent::DivergenceDetected`, the resync as `JournalEvent::DivergenceResolved` carrying every compared point's staged and field values — each at the compared tick and rendered beside the convergence state on the monitoring surface. Under #541 the `Diverged` verdict clears only on positive evidence — a same-tick comparison whose field reads all succeeded and matched — so `compared` always names the full staged image it verified; an apply whose checkpoint tick pairs with no staged image, a comparison whose field reads failed, and a produced-nothing pull all observe nothing, so the verdict stands, no resolution journals, and promotion stays refused.
- **Problem:** A checkpoint-converged standby can still diverge — skewed parameters, a plant restart, a missed transfer — and nothing would notice until it promotes and writes wrong outputs to the shared field. As implemented, `StandbySync::Tracking` means "the last checkpoint applied cleanly", not "proven to track the run".
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** The standby proves tracking before it may promote. While quiesced it stages the `Out` writes its scan would issue; the field-side truth is the standby's **own reads of the same `Out` points** through its driver surface — the field-observing path it already uses for inputs, needing no plant-server record and seeing exactly the field the standby would write. The per-value-kind rule is documented in `dcs-runtime::divergence`: `Bool` and `Int` compare exactly, `Float` agrees within `FLOAT_TOLERANCE = 1e-9` relative to the larger magnitude (absolute floor 1.0, `NaN` agreeing only with `NaN`) — sized to absorb transport re-encoding without masking real divergence; a failed field read is skipped, a communication fault not being divergence evidence. The check pairs a staged image with the checkpoint of the *same* tick — under the pull-per-scan cadence that is the transfer after the staged scan, the stated detection bound — because the field then holds the active's write for the tick the staged image describes; an unmatched staged image is discarded rather than compared against a different tick's field. Mismatch lands the peer in `Diverged`, which `promote` refuses like any unconverged state; the transition — not each recurring mismatch — is journaled once with the compared tick, and a clean same-tick comparison is the resync that clears it.
- **Alternatives considered:** Promoting on checkpoint freshness alone (a checkpoint proves delivery, not that the standby's subsequent scans track — a skewed component diverges the tick after restore); comparing input images only (two peers reading the same field agree on inputs while computing different outputs — the divergence that matters is in what would be written); post-promotion detection (the wrong outputs are already in the field before anyone looks).
- **Consequences:** Promotion gains an evidence gate: the pair reports not just "holds a checkpoint" but "tracks the field". Divergence is a named, journaled, resync-clearing state — a redundancy fault the UI shows as pair health (decision 19), never a plant fault. The per-kind comparison rule keeps the verdict deterministic: same inputs, same comparison, same answer on every run.
- **Affected tickets:** #89 (this record); #88 implements it; relies on #46 and #64; decisions 13, 15, and 19.

### 27. Checkpoint versioning for rolling upgrades

- **Status:** Implemented (#66). The shape #66 was left to fix: `Checkpoint` carries `format_version` — written as `CHECKPOINT_FORMAT_VERSION` (1), absent on pre-versioning checkpoints where serde defaults it to the accepted version 0; `SUPPORTED_FORMAT_VERSIONS` lists every version a restore accepts — and `model_fingerprint`, an `Option<dcs_core::ModelFingerprint>` holding FNV-1a over the canonical model document (`PlantModel::fingerprint`: the hash covers the parsed model's own reserialization, so key order and whitespace cannot perturb it while any semantic difference can). The assembling layer supplies the fingerprint — `dcs-assembly::assemble` stamps it via `Executor::with_model_fingerprint`, and `Executor::restore` takes the restoring model's as an argument — because the executor stays model-agnostic. Restore negotiates before any state applies: an unsupported version is `RestoreError::UnsupportedVersion` carrying the found and supported versions, a fingerprint mismatch is `RestoreError::FingerprintMismatch` carrying both values, and only then do decision 11's structural checks run — the standby's existing rollback leaves the rejected apply a named, recoverable `Degraded`. Implemented basis: the checkpoint contract itself exists — `dcs-runtime::Checkpoint` (`tick`, `components`, `driver`, `outputs`, `internal`) carries a run between executor instances (#21, decision 11), `GET /checkpoint` serves it and a `--standby` instance pulls and applies one per scan (#32, decision 12), and strict structural restore makes every incompatibility a named, recoverable `Degraded` state rather than a silent divergence. The pair-level path is exercised under #133: `crates/dcs-controller/tests/cross_version.rs` runs the scripted two-peer rig with the standby's per-scan `POST /scan` pull pointed at a fixture `GET /checkpoint` endpoint that proxies the active's live document rewritten into the negotiated wire shapes — the version-0 `format_version`-absent shape, an out-of-set version, and a foreign fingerprint — the recorded crafted-checkpoint-through-the-pull-path serving choice (a JSON document, not a `Checkpoint` value, because only the document can express "the field absent"). The version-0 stream converges the standby to `tracking` and the decision-15 switchover completes bumplessly; each refusal surfaces its named `RestoreError` as `degraded` carrying the found and supported versions or both fingerprints, `POST /promote` answers `not_converged`, and the active's run and field writes stay undisturbed.
- **Problem:** The M3/M4 hot-swap replaces a controller with an identical build. M5's lifecycle goal is replacing it with a *different* build — a bug fix, a component kind whose captured `StateMap` vocabulary grew — while the process runs. As implemented, strict restore rejects any skewed vocabulary: correct, but it cannot distinguish "incompatible build" from "transferable evolution", and a new-build standby discovers incompatibility only when its first checkpoint apply fails — there is no negotiation, so the pair's compatibility is unknowable until the transfer is attempted.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** The checkpoint gains an explicit `version` field under decision 3's convention: a `CHECKPOINT_VERSION` constant, the loader accepting only known versions, a bump on any breaking format change — mirroring `MODEL_VERSION` on the model document. Beside it the checkpoint carries a fingerprint naming what it was captured under — the model identity, hash of the canonical serialized declarations — so compatibility is negotiated at fetch time: a standby rejects an unusable checkpoint before applying it, and the refusal reports through the existing `StandbySync`/`Degraded` surface (decision 19). The rolling-upgrade sequence then composes existing pieces: start the new build as a standby on the same model; if it accepts the checkpoint version and fingerprint it converges and decision-15 promotion/demotion completes the swap; if not, it reports the named incompatibility and the old build keeps running the process untouched.
- **Alternatives considered:** Keeping structural-only validation (every build change then forces a cold restart — the rolling upgrade M5 exists to provide); a negotiation handshake on the monitoring transport before any checkpoint moves (a second protocol beside the artifact it negotiates — versioning the checkpoint itself answers "can you consume this" directly); on-load migration of old checkpoint formats like the model's (premature for transient peer traffic — a version floor plus the strict per-element checks already name every genuine mismatch); loosening restore to tolerate field drift (rejected in decision 11 — quiet divergence).
- **Consequences:** Checkpoint format joins the versioned-contract surface alongside the model document, and the same optional-field discipline applies: additive changes ride the existing version, breaking changes bump it. Compatibility becomes a pre-flight question with a named answer instead of a failed apply. Per-kind `StateMap` vocabularies remain deploy-together wire contract; now the checkpoint says so explicitly.
- **Affected tickets:** #70 (this record); #66 implemented it; relies on #21 (the format) and #32 (the transfer); #87 consumes the fingerprint to distinguish a model revision from a build skew (decision 25); decisions 3, 11, and 12.

### 28. Automatic failover: heartbeat, self-promotion gating, and fencing

- **Status:** Implemented — #65. `Peer` counts consecutive failed checkpoint pulls (`misses`, reset by any produced checkpoint — the active served, so it is alive) against the budget `Peer::with_failover` arms; `failover_due` reports at the budget-th miss and `self_promote` promotes at that scan boundary exactly like a manual request — reporting `promoting` until the first scan under the lifted gate settles `active`. The convergence gate carries over as a proof that must still *stand*: the last applied verdict was `Tracking` — a diverged or rejected apply voids it — and the miss run has not exceeded the budget, so a single transient miss neither promotes nor re-arms, and an `Unsynchronized`, `Diverged`, or over-budget `Degraded` peer reports its named `StandbySync` state on `GET /role` instead of promoting. Fencing is the field's own arbitration: `dcs-sim-net`'s `PlantRequest::ClaimWriter` takes the plant's write-ownership under an opaque owner token — one token per controller instance covers all its attachments — granted unconditionally and held until preempted; once an owner is claimed, `write` answers a non-owner attachment with `IoError::Fenced` and `step` with `PlantError::Fenced`, while reads and plant tooling stay open. Every promotion — manual `promote` or `self_promote` — runs the claim before the gate lifts, refusing with `SwitchError::FieldClaimFailed` when the claim fails. Device kinds arbitrate through `DeviceBackend::claim`: `sim-tcp` installs the plant-server claim, `sim-bus` the device server's (#154 extends the rule to the register protocol — `claim_writer`/`release_writer` with the claim bound to its attachments, released on a holder's disconnect or explicit release so a dead owner frees the field for a promoted peer), and `FanoutDriver::unfenced_field_devices` names any field-facing device that cannot arbitrate — `--auto-promote N` refuses to start a model that has one, leaving manual promotion. `dcs-controller` wires both paced and `--driven` standby loops to count misses per scan cycle and self-promote when due. A field-owning peer whose write the field fenced is superseded: `Peer::scan` completes the boundary degraded — the `fenced` fault counted in `io_health` — and demotes the peer in place: the gate re-closes, the release hook drops the recorded claim token so a re-attaching link cannot re-assert it, the reported role walks `demoting` to `standby` on the first quiesced scan, and the `field_claim_lost` event and role changes journal — rather than the scan failure exiting the process, so a misordered promotion or a restarted superseded peer degrades instead of crash-looping (#508). Commands the fenced boundary applied settle `Rejected{superseded}` rather than `applied` — and because the image, force set, and component state they mutated are run state the demoted peer keeps serving in quiesced checkpoints, the supersede replays the boundary's recorded effects back out before the receipts rewrite (#793): a `Rejected`/`Superseded` receipt means the surviving line never made the change, and a field-side write the driver accepted gets a best-effort compensating write-back the claim arbitration may still refuse. The plant claim also tracks the live connections holding it (#515): a grant joining a token another live attachment already holds answers `claimed_shared` — still granted, since one owner's several attachments share a token by design and the token cannot tell that from a second field-owning process reusing it — so a duplicate `--owner-token` is flagged on the wire and warned by the claiming controller instead of silently defeating the fencing.
- **Problem:** Promotion is operator- or test-triggered only: an active that dies leaves the field unwritten until someone posts `/promote` to the standby — a human in the loop the vision's hot-swap does not assume. Automating takeover needs recorded rules: how active loss is detected and on what cadence, when a standby may promote itself rather than wait, and how a merely *partitioned* old active is prevented from writing the field beside the new one — the fencing or split-brain rule.
- **Chosen approach:** The checkpoint pull is the heartbeat: a successful `GET /checkpoint` is proof of liveness, so no second channel is introduced. Cadence stays the implemented one — one pull per scan cycle — and loss is declared only after a configured budget of consecutive failed transfers: the budget, not the transport, is the new knob, sizing detection time in scan periods. Self-promotion stacks on the existing convergence gate: only a standby whose convergence proof still stands within the budget may promote itself; `Unsynchronized`, `Diverged`, or over-budget `Degraded` verdicts all block. The fencing rule: exactly one peer writes the field (decision 15), and a heartbeat loss is not proof of death — a partitioned active still believes it owns the field. The field is therefore the arbiter: the plant-facing protocol gains a single-writer claim — the plant server grants write ownership to one owner token at a time, so the promoted standby takes it and a still-alive old active's writes fail as `IoError`s instead of doubling the field writes. Where a field kind cannot enforce single-writer, automatic self-promotion is not enabled: the write gate still lifts on the manual path, but unattended takeover stays off — the split-brain risk is a documented deployment constraint, not a silent hole.
- **Alternatives considered:** A separate heartbeat channel (duplicates the liveness signal the checkpoint pull already carries, and can disagree with it — a peer can heartbeat while serving stale checkpoints); promotion on the first failed pull (network jitter becomes a takeover — the miss budget exists to absorb it); unconditional self-promotion after a timeout (split-brain by design: a partitioned active keeps writing); a third arbiter process or quorum (another deployed component M5 does not otherwise need — the shared field is already the natural arbiter).
- **Consequences:** Loss detection, promotion gating, and fencing become recorded, testable semantics instead of operational convention. The heartbeat rides existing machinery — one new configured budget plus one new protocol element (the writer claim). Fencing honesty is the cost: deployments whose field cannot arbitrate single-writer get manual failover only, and the documentation says so.
- **Affected tickets:** #70 (this record); #65 implements it and #154 extends the fencing to the sim-bus register protocol; #515 adds the duplicate-owner `claimed_shared` flag; relies on #32 (checkpoint pull and named transfer failure) and #46 (roles, gate, switch errors); #88 supplies the divergence half of the gate; decisions 12, 13, 15, and 19.

### 29. Device integration as a registered seam

- **Status:** Implemented — the `DriverRegistry` seam exists (#47): model `Device.kind` strings resolve to factories through `resolve_drivers`, producing a `DriverPlan` whose `build` yields a `FanoutDriver` presenting one `IoDriver` over every backend. `DriverRegistry::standard` installs three registrations covering both contribution shapes: the `sim*` prefix (each device's points join the shared local simulated channel map — the `DeviceDriver::Sim` fragment shape), the exact `sim-tcp` kind (a `RemoteDriver` attached to a `dcs-sim-net` `PlantServer` — the `DeviceDriver::Backend` shape with a step hook and an inspect handle, #31), and the exact `sim-scripted` kind (a `ScriptedDriver` replaying a tick-indexed script declared in the device's `parameters` — `Backend` shape, #67). The integration guide documents the path end to end (#71).
- **Problem:** Per the vision, "adding a device" must be model data, not per-layer engineering: declare the device, bind its channels to logical I/O, and obtain driver resolution, wiring checks, diagnostics, checkpoint participation, and monitoring access from the platform. Before #47 the `sim` backend was hard-coded into assembly, so every new device family would have edited the platform itself.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** The seam is a registered factory `Fn(&DeviceSpec) -> Result<DeviceDriver, DeviceError>` keyed by the model's opaque `kind` string; exact registrations outrank prefix matches, which is how `sim-tcp` and `sim-scripted` route to their own backends while the rest of `sim*` stays local. `DeviceSpec` hands the factory everything the model declared — the device's id and actual kind string, its `parameters` as arbitrary JSON the factory alone validates, its channels, and the `DevicePoint`s model validation already guarantees agree with them. The factory returns `DeviceDriver::Sim(ChannelMap)` to merge into the shared local simulated backend, or `DeviceDriver::Backend { io, step, inspect }` for a self-contained kind — `step` an optional `StepHook` advancing a simulated backend per `FanoutDriver::step`, `inspect` an optional typed handle reaching beyond the `IoDriver` surface (the scripted driver's recorded-write log). Parameter and construction failures are `DeviceError::parameters`/`backend`, surfacing as `AssemblyError`s naming the device before any scan; an unregistered kind is `UnknownDeviceKind`. Cross-backend wiring becomes `FanoutDriver` routes applied at `step`, so one model can split a loop across device kinds.
- **Alternatives considered:** Hard-coded kind dispatch in assembly (the pre-#47 status quo — every device family edits platform code); per-kind fields in the model schema (splits device knowledge between schema and driver, and the schema would grow with every integration); link-time self-registration (rejected for components in decision 6 — kinds silently absent when unlinked — and the same reasoning applies to devices); driver kinds selected by `io_point` rather than by `Device` (points would carry transport detail — the device is the unit the model already declares).
- **Consequences:** "Adding a device" is: implement `IoDriver`, write the factory, register the kind — resolution, wiring checks, assembly-time error naming, `capture_state` participation (decision 13's per-kind choice), `step`/`inspect` hooks, and monitoring all arrive from the shared layers. The two contribution shapes are both proven: `sim*` merges, `sim-tcp`/`sim-scripted` stand alone. A real fieldbus kind follows the identical path — the seam does not distinguish simulated from physical backends, per decision 5.
- **Affected tickets:** #70 (this record); #47 implemented the registry, #31 the remote kind, #67 the scripted kind, #71 the guide; relies on #19 (the assembly seam) and decisions 5 and 6.

### 30. Plant-side simulation tooling outside the operator contract

- **Status:** Implemented — fault injection and explicit stepping live on the plant side, never on the operator surface: `SimDriver` carries `inject_fault`/`clear_fault`/`step` beside its `IoDriver` impl (#8); the `dcs-sim-net` plant protocol serves `step`, `inject_fault`, `clear_fault`, and `list_points` next to read/write (#31); `dcs-plant-ctl` exercises them as a command-line tool (#68); `dcs-plant-server` runs the shared plant as a process with `--dynamics` declarations merged beside the model (#81, decision 24); and `sim-scripted` replays declared tick-indexed field behavior from device parameters (#67).
- **Problem:** Developing, testing, and demonstrating the system requires perturbing the *world*, not the controller: inject a sensor fault, force a field input, advance plant time, replay a scenario. These are testbench actions on the simulated plant. Routing them through the operator `Command` path would corrupt the operator contract — a fault is the world misbehaving, not an operator action; it should not produce a command receipt, occupy the writability surface (decision 18), or read as an operator entry in the journal.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Plant-side tooling addresses the plant, not the controller. `dcs-plant-ctl` speaks the plant server's wire protocol directly — one subcommand per `PlantRequest` — and tests and rigs call the same `SimDriver` API in process. The controller observes only effects through the `IoDriver` boundary: an injected quality fault arrives as an `Uncertain`/`Bad` sample, an error fault as the point's `IoError` — indistinguishable from a real field fault, which is the point. Stepping is explicit and owned: the field-owning peer steps the shared plant once per scan and a standby never does, so plant time advances exactly once per scan regardless of pair membership (implemented in `dcs-controller`'s step closure consulting `owns_field`). Injected faults are part of the simulated plant's state: `SimDriver`'s checkpoint section carries them (decision 13), so a local-sim standby's tracking copy faults identically. Scripts and dynamics are declared device parameters and standalone documents — never `PlantModel` schema (decision 24's boundary).
- **Alternatives considered:** Fault injection through the `Command`/receipt path (corrupts the operator contract with testbench actions and would have to reach through the controller to a plant it does not own); scenario-driving only inside test code (no live demonstration or ad-hoc perturbation of a running pair — `dcs-plant-ctl` exists precisely for that); a private test-only protocol for the plant (blocks the standalone `dcs-plant-server` deployment shape and the second-controller-observes-same-plant property M3 needs); implicit plant time (a plant that advances on its own wall clock reintroduces the nondeterminism decision 4 excludes).
- **Consequences:** Two deliberately separate surfaces result: the operator contract (`Command`/`Receipt`, journal, role endpoints) stays clean of simulation concerns, while the plant protocol is a documented development surface — the monitoring page never offers fault injection because it is not an operator capability. The tooling composes with redundancy and determinism rather than bypassing them: faults checkpoint with driver state, stepping follows field ownership, and scripted scenarios replay identically on every run.
- **Affected tickets:** #70 (this record); #8, #31, #67, #68, and #81 implemented the surfaces; decisions 4, 13, and 24 supply the determinism, checkpoint, and declared-dynamics boundaries.

### 31. Typed model composition

- **Status:** Implemented (#112): the `dcs-build` crate carries the seam — `PlantBuilder` emitting exactly the versioned `PlantModel` document `dcs-model` loads, the per-kind specs in `dcs_build::specs` declaring each kind's string, typed port handles, and parameter set as data, `connect`/`bind` checking port direction and `ValueKind` at compile time through the typed handles, and `build()` reporting named errors for what types cannot carry; `dcs-blocks/tests/spec_drift.rs` pins the specs to the registered kinds' `describe()` output and `dcs-build/tests/tank_loop.rs` reproduces the checked-in tank-loop fixture. Implemented basis: the artifact being composed is already a plain serde document — `PlantModel`'s sections are public fields `PlantModel::validate` checks whether the model arrived as JSON or was built programmatically (#7, decision 3); every registered component kind already publishes its surface as a `ComponentDescriptor` — kind string, port names with direction and `ValueKind`, parameter names with kinds and ranges (#50, #61, decision 16) — and `dcs-assembly`'s `ComponentRegistry` resolves kind strings to constructors (#19, decision 6). Nothing emits a model from Rust today: composition is a hand-written or tool-written JSON document whose port names, directions, value kinds, required parameters, and channel references are all checked only at load.
- **Problem:** The vision asks that an engineer compose plant software in Rust with the contracts expressed through types and enforced at compile time as far as possible. As implemented, a typo'd port name, a `Float` port wired to a `Bool` point, or a missing required parameter is a `ValidationError`/`AssemblyError` discovered when the document loads — never a compile error — and every model edit round-trips through JSON even when the author is writing Rust anyway.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** A new workspace crate `dcs-build`, depending on `dcs-core` and `dcs-model` only — never `dcs-blocks` or `dcs-runtime`, so the seam stays a model producer with no runtime entanglement. Two halves: per-kind *specs* and a *model builder*. A spec is a data struct per registered kind — declared beside the kind's descriptor vocabulary — carrying its kind string, its named ports as typed handles (name, direction, `ValueKind`), and its parameter set; registering a component from its spec returns an instance whose ports are typed references, and `connect`/`bind` calls check the endpoints' direction and value kind at compile time where the kind is statically known. What types cannot express — resolvable ids, required parameters, channel references — `build()` checks and reports as named errors naming the offending element. `build()` emits the identical versioned `PlantModel` document `dcs-model` loads: the builder is a producer of the one contract, not a second schema, and `validate` remains the backstop for graph-level rules. Because specs are data kept in sync with the registered kinds' descriptors by convention, a checked drift test comparing specs against each kind's `describe()` output is part of the seam, and a worked example reproduces the checked-in tank-loop model.
- **Alternatives considered:** A builder API inside `dcs-model` (the schema crate is deliberately serialization-plus-validation only — decision 6 keeps `dcs-model` free of runtime knowledge, and per-kind spec data is kind knowledge; a separate crate also keeps `dcs-model` small for non-Rust tooling that only parses the document); having `dcs-blocks` kinds emit builders themselves (adds a `dcs-model` dependency to the component library and couples every kind crate to the document format — spec data in `dcs-build` keeps the dependency direction one-way); a proc-macro or external DSL (more machinery than the problem needs — ordinary generics and typed handles already carry the compile-time checks); building documents with `serde_json::json!` (the de facto route tests use today — no checks beyond load-time validation).
- **Consequences:** Two authoring surfaces produce one artifact: a Rust-composed plant serde-roundtrips, loads, validates, and assembles identically to a hand-written document, so versioning, diffs, and tooling are unchanged. Compile-time enforcement covers port existence, direction, and value kind — the checks types can carry; everything else stays a named build-time or validation error, and the boundary between the two is explicit in the spec's types. Spec-versus-descriptor drift is the standing cost: a kind that evolves without its spec is caught by the drift test, and a kind with no spec fails `build()` by name rather than silently compiling a weaker check.
- **Affected tickets:** #104 (this record); #112 implements it; relies on #7 (the document), #19 (the registry the emitted model feeds), and #50/#61 (descriptors as the spec's source of truth); decisions 3, 6, and 16.

### 32. A register-mapped fieldbus device kind

- **Status:** Implemented (#113): `dcs-sim-bus` serves a `RegisterBank` over a length-prefixed binary TCP protocol — per-register read and write, `list_registers`, and an explicit `step` advancing the bank's logical tick — `BusDriver` implements `IoDriver` translating each `PointId` through its channel's declared register and mapping the wire's error vocabulary onto `IoError` (`UnknownPoint`, `TypeMismatch`, `Disconnected`, `Timeout`), `dcs-sim-bus-device` is the device-server binary, `SIM_BUS_KIND` registers in `DriverRegistry::standard` with `DeviceParameters` declaring the server address and each channel's register index — the first kind where `DeviceSpec.channels`/`parameters` do real addressing work — and the `field_facing` backend contributes its `StepHook` and `None` capture under `FanoutDriver` beside `sim`, `sim-tcp`, and `sim-scripted` (the `mixed_bus.json` fixture proves the mixed-kind fan-out). #213 extends it with the declared-dynamics seam: the bank is a `SimDriver` whose point ids are the register addresses, `dcs-sim-bus-device --dynamics FILE` merges the same `ProcessElement` declaration list `dcs-plant-server --dynamics` serves (decision 24) with the element's point-valued fields carrying register addresses — the recorded binding encoding — and `step` carries the caller's `dt` on the wire, advancing the merged elements in lockstep under the same named `dt` validation the plant protocol applies. It was built on implemented machinery: the `IoDriver` boundary (#6, decision 5), the `DriverRegistry`/`DeviceSpec`/`DeviceBackend` seam (#47, decision 29), the one remote transport — `dcs-sim-net`'s `PlantServer`/`RemoteDriver` (#31) — `FanoutDriver` routing with `StepHook` and `field_facing` (#47, #64), and explicit, field-owned stepping (decision 30).
- **Problem:** The hardware-abstraction claim — the same component runs against any field interface unchanged — is so far exercised by exactly one remote transport, and it is a friendly one: the `dcs-sim-net` plant protocol addresses `PointId`s in the controller's own logical space and carries `IoError`s verbatim, so it validates the seam's mechanics without stress-testing the translation a real fieldbus needs. A real field interface is register-mapped: device-local numeric addresses, polled or batched exchanges, and a bus-level failure vocabulary. A second transport shaped that way is what proves the boundary is a boundary.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** A new workspace crate `dcs-sim-bus` carrying a documented register-mapped wire protocol over TCP — deliberately distinct from the line-JSON plant protocol — a `BusDriver` implementing `IoDriver` against a device server, the server binary for integration rigs, and a `sim-bus` device kind registered in `DriverRegistry` so one model mixes `sim`, `sim-tcp`, `sim-scripted`, and `sim-bus` channels under `FanoutDriver`. The three places it differs from `sim-tcp`, and what stays shared:
  - *Addressing.* `sim-tcp`'s wire speaks `PointId`s: the plant server is configured with the model's own point ids and the factory merely probes that each declared point is served — `DeviceSpec.channels` is effectively nominal there. A `sim-bus` device's `parameters` carry real addressing: the server endpoint plus each declared channel's register index — the first kind where `DeviceSpec.channels`/`parameters` do addressing work the model guarantees the shape of and the factory validates. The `IoDriver` surface stays `PointId`/`Value`; the driver translates point → channel → register internally, and a malformed register map is `DeviceError::Parameters` surfacing as `AssemblyError::InvalidDeviceParameters` naming the device.
  - *Error mapping.* The plant protocol returns the server's own `IoError` (`PlantError::Io`) because the remote is itself a `SimDriver`; transport failures collapse to `Disconnected`/`Timeout` at the requesting point. A register bus has its own failure vocabulary the driver owns the mapping for: no answer → `Timeout`, dead link → `Disconnected`, an unmapped register → `UnknownPoint`, a kind- or width-mismatched write → `TypeMismatch`. Polled register ranges mean one failed exchange faults every point the poll covered — each covered point reports the same named failure, and transport-level health reports through the optional `IoDriver::diagnostics` hook (#83), so a dead bus reads as link degradation, not scattered per-point faults.
  - *Step ownership.* Identical rule to `sim-tcp`: the register file is the shared field — server-side state, not the driver's — so the backend is `field_facing: true`, contributes a `StepHook` forwarding `step(dt)` to the device server, leaves `capture_state` at its `None` default (a standby observes the actual registers, per decision 13), and is stepped once per scan by the field-owning peer while `FanoutDriver::step_local` leaves its clock alone on a tracking peer (decision 30). What stays shared through the boundary is everything decision 5 promises: `read`/`write` over `PointId` with declared-kind checks, the `DeviceBackend` contribution shape, write-gate quiescing of field-facing points, fan-out routing, and assembly-time error naming.
  - *Declared dynamics.* `dcs-sim-bus-device --dynamics FILE` merges a JSON list of `ProcessElement` declarations — the identical serde vocabulary decision 24 records — over the bank's registers rather than logical points: the recorded binding encoding carries register addresses in the element's point-valued fields, so a document's `"input": 20` reads register 20. Each element validates as it merges, so a rejection names the element's position and the register it drives. Element behavior is `SimDriver`'s unchanged — caller-supplied `dt`, the documented non-`Good` input rule per variant (the #197 quality-injection path exercises it), named errors for non-finite or negative parameters and wrongly-kinded endpoints — because the bank literally is a `SimDriver` keyed by register. Element state lives in the bank for the bank's lifetime: it is shared field state exactly like the `dcs-sim-net` plant's, never checkpointed controller state — `BusDriver::capture_state` stays `None`, and a tracking standby observes the stepped registers rather than restoring a process copy.
- **Alternatives considered:** Extending the plant protocol with register operations (one protocol serving two address shapes proves nothing about foreign transports — the seam's value is that the second kind needs no contract change); encoding register addresses in channel names (loses the validated, kind-checked parameter surface `DeviceSpec` already provides); a controller-local register image stepped like a `sim` backend (`field_facing: false` — a loopback, not a transport; the field-observing shape is what exercises the redundancy and step-ownership semantics); a per-point request protocol like the plant's (misses the batch-failure semantics a polled bus actually has — the mapping must account for one failed poll faulting a whole range).
- **Consequences:** Device `parameters` become the documented addressing contract for real transports — opaque to the schema, validated by the kind's factory at assembly, naming the device on failure. Components and the executor cannot distinguish `sim-bus` from `sim-tcp` except through diagnostics — which is precisely the property the milestone exists to demonstrate. A second `field_facing` kind also exercises the write gate, `step_local`, and fan-out routing under a genuinely shared field rather than a single-remote shape.
- **Affected tickets:** #104 (this record); #113 implements it and #213 adds the declared-dynamics seam; #309's `threshold` merges through the same seam; relies on #31 (the first transport), #47 (the registry and fan-out), and #64 (field-ownership rules); #83 supplies the diagnostics hook it reports link health through; decisions 5, 13, 24, 29, and 30.

### 33. Alarm acknowledgment

- **Status:** Implemented (#109, filed under M6): the `latching-alarm` kind declares `in` (`Float`, `In`), `ack` (`Bool`, `In`), `alarm` (`Bool`, `Out` — the standing limit state under `AlarmMonitor`'s hysteresis rule exactly), and `unacknowledged` (`Bool`, `Out` — latched on a fresh trip, cleared on the scan `ack`'s rising edge lands, never clearing `alarm` itself); the model wires a writable channel-less `In` point to `ack` so acknowledgment rides the ordinary receipted `WriteValue` path and journals as `CommandSettled`, and the latch rides `capture_state` as `unacknowledged` so a tracking standby inherits it. Implemented basis: `AlarmMonitor` (#22) is strictly auto-clearing — its `alarm` output follows the documented hysteresis rule and carries no notion of operator awareness; channel-less internal `In` points hold operator values and accept `Command::WriteValue` at the scan boundary through the receipt-answered path (#30, #20, decisions 14 and 7); the `writable` flag narrowing that surface to model-declared `In` points is implemented (#49, decision 18); and the journal already records settled receipts and point quality transitions (#35, decision 17).
- **Problem:** Auto-clear alarms answer "is the process outside limits now" but not "did anyone see it": a transient excursion trips and clears before an operator looks, leaving no acknowledged record in history or journal. The missing vocabulary is the standard two-flag alarm model — a *standing* indication reporting process truth plus a *latched* unacknowledged flag reporting operator visibility — and a recorded answer to where the acknowledge action rides: a declared `ack` input the model wires, or a dedicated `Command` variant.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Latching is per-kind declared behavior, not a mode of the existing kind: `alarm-monitor` keeps its auto-clear semantics and a sibling latching kind (#109) declares `in` (`Float`, `In`), `ack` (`Bool`, `In`), `alarm` (`Bool`, `Out` — the standing limit state, following `AlarmMonitor`'s hysteresis rule exactly), and `unacknowledged` (`Bool`, `Out` — set when the limit state trips, held until acknowledged). The ack rides the path already built for operator values: the model wires a channel-less `In` point declared `writable` (decision 18, #49) to the `ack` port, and the operator acknowledges with `Command::WriteValue` on that point — a writable internal point holds the written value until the next command, which is what makes the consumed-edge ack below well-defined. No new `Command` variant and no new `Component` hook: an ack is an operator value like a setpoint (decision 14), so it inherits the receipt contract, the writability surface, telemetry visibility, and checkpoint carryover through the `internal` section for free. `ack` is consumed on its rising edge: the scan observing `false`→`true` releases the `unacknowledged` latch — a standing alarm then reads as acknowledged-but-active, which is the two-flag model's point — and a fresh trip after the latch cleared re-asserts it. The consumed edge is the QA-corrected contract (#781): a client holding `ack` permanently `true` must not silently auto-acknowledge every later trip — a withheld annunciation asserts a named managed state (decision 73), and a bare held level names none — so the component checkpoints the last-observed `ack` level, acknowledges once per `false`→`true` cycle, and lets a fresh trip win even on the edge's own scan; the kind documents its precise edge cases per #109's convention. Journaled: the ack write settles as `JournalEvent::CommandSettled` naming the ack point — the identical audit every operator action gets — while `unacknowledged` and `alarm` transitions appear in the points' history streams; no dedicated alarm event type is added. The latch is run state: `capture_state` carries it so a tracking standby inherits unacknowledged alarms — decision 20's checkpoint obligation applied to latched state.
- **Alternatives considered:** A dedicated `Command::Acknowledge { component }` variant with a `Component::acknowledge` hook (a component-targeted command carrying an empty payload — it duplicates what a point write expresses, needs a new hook plus a journal event to stay as auditable, and names only a component, so a kind raising several alarms could not say which was acked); auto-clear as the only behavior (loses the operator-visibility record entirely — a transient alarm leaves no trace it stood); making `alarm` itself latch until ack (conflates the two flags — the standing indication would report operator state, not process truth); level-observed ack releasing the latch on every scan it reads `true` (the shipped rule until #781's QA finding: a write left standing pre-acknowledged every later trip — functionally suppression with no named state — so the edge-consumed rule replaced it).
- **Consequences:** Acknowledgment needs no contract extension — a model-declared point, an ordinary command, a journaled receipt — and the page's ack affordance is a writable-point edit like any other, so #108's alarm summary pane consumes `alarm` and `unacknowledged` as ordinary status-role ports. The two-flag split keeps process truth and operator acknowledgment independently visible in telemetry, history, and checkpoints. The `writable` mark makes the ack surface explicit model data: an unmarked `In` point rejects `WriteValue` with `CommandError::NotWritable`, so the ack affordance exists only where the model declares it.
- **Affected tickets:** #104 (this record); #109 implements the latching kind; relies on #22 (the auto-clear baseline), #30 (internal points), and #20 (the command path); #49 supplies the writable surface; #108 consumes the flags; decisions 7, 14, 17, 18, and 20.

### 34. Faceplate parameter display

- **Status:** Implemented. `ParameterDescriptor` serves each tunable's name, `ValueKind`, and declared `ParameterRange` — the editing authority (#50, #61, decision 16) — and `Command::SetParameter` applies a tune at the scan boundary with named rejections and a receipt, tuned values riding the checkpoint (#82, decision 20). Under #105 (M4) the descriptor-driven faceplate renders edit controls typed and range-labeled from the descriptor and submits through the receipted path. Implemented under #132: `TelemetrySnapshot.parameters` carries one `ComponentParameters` per registered component in scan order — `name` plus a `values` map of exactly the descriptor-declared parameter names to their current `Value`s — fed by the `Component::report_parameters` hook whose default reports nothing so kinds without tunables are unaffected. Every `dcs-blocks` kind declaring parameters reports them from the same fields `capture_state` checkpoints, and the executor filters each report to the descriptor's declared names, keeping the descriptor the surface's sole authority.
- **Problem:** An editable faceplate without the current value is a blind edit: the operator cannot see what `kp` is before retuning it, cannot confirm a tune persisted except through downstream behavior, and cannot see a value a standby inherited or a checkpoint restored. Yet current values are run state, not static metadata — putting them on `ComponentDescriptor` would corrupt the descriptor's role (decision 16) and make the same instance's descriptor change between snapshots.
- **Chosen approach:** Record the division of authority: the descriptor owns the *editable surface* — name, kind, range — and current values are telemetry belonging on the snapshot. Implemented under #132: `TelemetrySnapshot` gained a per-component current-parameter section carrying `name → Value` for exactly the descriptor-declared parameters, fed by the `Component::report_parameters` hook whose default reports nothing so kinds without tunables are unaffected. The hook reports from the same fields `capture_state` checkpoints — one vocabulary feeds the standby and the faceplate — and the executor filters each report to declared names only, keeping the descriptor the surface's sole authority. The page then joins descriptor plus current value plus edit affordance exactly as it joins descriptor plus live points today — that rendering half remains follow-up work. The interim accepted for #105 — the faceplate rendering the declared metadata and submitting through the receipted path — was the first cut, not the end state.
- **Alternatives considered:** Display-only descriptors as the permanent answer (blind editing — the receipt confirms an apply but nothing shows what was there before or what a restored standby carries); declaring each tunable as an internal `In`/`Out` point pair (doubles the representation — every parameter needs a declared point, wiring, and component code reading it, duplicating the parameter map the kind already owns); serving the model document to the page (construction-time values only — it misses runtime tuning, the exact case that makes display matter); folding current values into `ParameterDescriptor` (breaks the descriptor's static-metadata contract — the type is kind-level metadata, not per-tick state).
- **Consequences:** Tuned state becomes first-class telemetry the page, tests, and tooling read identically. The reporting hook adds a per-kind obligation shaped like `apply_parameter`'s checkpoint obligation — report declared names only, from the same fields the checkpoint captures — keeping descriptor, live display, and standby coherent by construction. The snapshot grows by each component's declared parameter count: bounded, static in shape, and absent for kinds declaring none.
- **Affected tickets:** #104 (this record); #105 landed the declared-metadata edit surface; #132 implemented the current-value section; relies on #82 (tuning and checkpoint carryover) and #50/#61 (descriptors); decisions 16 and 20.

### 35. Checkpoint persistence for restart recovery

- **Status:** Implemented (#115): `dcs-controller --state-file PATH` writes the serde `Checkpoint` — the same versioned, fingerprinted contract `GET /checkpoint` serves (decisions 11, 27) — at the end of every completed scan cycle, after the scan and the plant step, by write-then-rename to a sibling `PATH.tmp`. When `PATH` exists at startup the run resumes: the checkpoint is applied in place to the freshly assembled executor (`Executor::apply`) before pacing begins; a missing file is a cold start. Every resume failure — unreadable or unparseable file, unsupported format version, fingerprint or structural mismatch — exits nonzero naming the file and the reason. Under #563 the fingerprint half of that gate gains the revision arm: `--revised` with `--state-file` and no `--standby` routes a foreign-fingerprint checkpoint through `Executor::reinitialize` — decision 25's carryover rule applied at the resume seam, the lone controller's scheduled-outage roll — with the crossing's `CarryoverReport` journaled per decision 36; matching fingerprints and every unarmed resume still negotiate exactly as recorded here.
- **Problem:** The checkpoint machinery moves a run between *peers* (decisions 11–13): a lone controller restart has no standby to converge from and would cold-start, interrupting the process the vision wants uninterrupted. The checkpoint format already carries everything a continuation needs — tick, component state, image, and driver-side state (decision 13) — so persisting it locally is the no-peer recovery story.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Reuse the one checkpoint contract rather than a second persistence format: the file holds exactly the JSON `Checkpoint` a standby pulls, so the negotiation a peer performs — `format_version` against `SUPPORTED_FORMAT_VERSIONS`, `model_fingerprint` equality, then decision 11's structural checks — is the same negotiation a restart performs, with the same named failures. The recorded boundary is the end of each scan cycle, after `step` has run: the checkpoint then captures the post-step driver state the next scan's input phase reads, so a resumed run re-enters the loop at the point the capture was taken — writing mid-cycle would resume with the driver one step behind. Writes are atomic (temporary sibling plus rename) so a crash mid-write cannot leave a torn file that a later resume must reject. Resume is automatic on the file's presence — no separate flag — because the flag's purpose is recovery: a restarted process given the same command line resumes, while a missing file is an unambiguous cold start. Restore is all-or-nothing per decision 11: a version or fingerprint mismatch, a corrupt file, or a changed model exits nonzero naming the reason rather than silently starting fresh — a state file that exists but cannot be resumed is a fault to surface, never a hint to ignore. The choice also extends to the `--driven` mode, where the write rides the per-requested-scan `after_scan` hook — now receiving the `Peer` — at the same end-of-cycle boundary.
- **Alternatives considered:** A separate `--resume` flag instead of resume-on-existence (a restarted process would need its command line changed to recover, and a stale `--resume` with no file is its own failure mode — existence is the honest signal); checkpointing every N scans or only on each checkpoint the monitor serves (both leave the persisted state behind the run by a configurable or request-driven amount — every cycle is the simplest boundary that loses at most the in-flight cycle); a bespoke run-state document instead of the `Checkpoint` (a second versioned contract to keep compatible with restore semantics forever).
- **Consequences:** Restart recovery needs no peer: the process dies, restarts with the same arguments, and continues at the last completed cycle — determinism makes the continuation identical to the uninterrupted run. The standby path is unchanged and remains the preferred recovery where redundancy exists: a tracking peer converges continuously and takes over without a restart, while the state file resumes only from the last persisted cycle after the process has died. What the checkpoint does not carry — receipt history, I/O-health counters, journal and history buffers — restarts fresh; the file recovers the run, not its diagnostics. A state file is bound to its model by fingerprint: a changed model fails resume by name, per the all-or-nothing rule.
- **Affected tickets:** #115 implemented it; #563 arms the resume seam for a deliberate model revision (decision 25); relies on #21 (the checkpoint contract and all-or-nothing restore), #66 (version and fingerprint negotiation), and #64 (the driven-mode scan boundary); decisions 11, 12, 13, and 27.

### 36. Journal persistence beyond restart

- **Status:** Implemented (#163): `MonitorConfig::journal_file` — wired on the controller as `--journal-file PATH`, requiring `--listen` since the recorder lives in the monitor — appends every journaled entry to `PATH` as a line-delimited `{"entry":{...JournalEntry...}}` record at the recorder's documented point, replays the file at bind to seed the ring and continue `seq` numbering, and opens each process lifetime with a `{"run_boundary":{"run":N,"tick":T}}` marker — `tick` recording the tick the run starts at, so a `--state-file` resume's restored tick domain is visible in the file. Under #536 the marker gains a served form: a restart's boundary journals once as a `JournalEvent::RunBoundary` entry — the run's first entry, attributed to its start tick — so a `GET /journal` consumer attributes the entries on either side of the seam to their process lifetime, and a `--state-file`-resumed run seeds its recorder's diff baselines from the restored executor (receipt log, image samples) plus the replayed record's last observed states, so a restart re-journals neither settled receipts nor the unchanged standing census as `from: None` first observations; a cold restart beside a kept journal still journals its census `from: None`, the designed first-observation convention the report tooling folds on. Under #623 the served marker is pinned: a `run_boundary` entry the bounded tail — or the pending publication delta, or the replay's own retained tail — evicts migrates to a pinned stream `GET /journal` still answers ahead of the retained window in `seq` order, since the marker is the only record a consumer has that a new process lifetime began; ordinary event volume can no longer merge two lifetimes invisibly. A record that cannot be replayed fails startup naming the file, the line, and the offending record; a missing file is a cold start; an append the file cannot take is fatal rather than a silent gap in the audit trail. Implemented basis: `dcs-monitor`'s `Recorder` already appends to the bounded per-point history rings and the transition journal once per completed scan — the documented recording point after the write phase, never mid-scan — with oldest-first eviction and never-reused `seq`s making eviction visible (#35, decision 17); `JournalEntry`/`JournalEvent`/`HistorySample` are serde contract types served over `GET /journal` and `GET /history`; and the checkpoint state file (#115, decision 35) gives a lone controller restart its resume story — its consequence section naming exactly this gap: "the file recovers the run, not its diagnostics". Point history remains volatile per decision 17; only the journal persists.
- **Problem:** The journal is the run's audit record — settled commands, quality transitions, step failures, role changes, divergence verdicts — and it lives only in the bounded in-memory ring. A controller restart drops it: the operator-action trail the receipts and journal exist to provide (decisions 7, 17) resets with the process, and a post-incident review of who commanded what cannot reach back across a restart. Decision 35's checkpoint file resumes the run's *state*; nothing preserves the run's *record*.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** A file-backed journal sink as monitor-local tooling, not a contract concern. When a path is configured on the controller or monitor, every entry the `Recorder` appends to the transition journal is also appended to a line-delimited JSON file at the same recording point — after the write phase, under the monitor lock, never mid-scan. On startup the monitor replays the file to seed the in-memory ring and continue `seq` numbering where it left off, so `GET /journal` answers continuously across a restart and a consumer still sees a retained gap as a numbering gap. The file reuses the contract's serde serialization but is the monitor's own format — its readers are tooling and the replaying monitor itself, not contract consumers — and a run-boundary marker record distinguishes process lifetimes in one file. Point *history* stays volatile per decision 17's original reasoning: it is high-volume diagnostics that re-accumulates after a restart, where the journal is the low-volume record that cannot be reconstructed — and under #886 the volatile ring's renumbering, when a restart begins a new tick domain, is made detectable by the `run` mark on each served `PointHistory`, counted on the same numbering as this file's `run_boundary` markers, rather than by persisting the samples. Interaction with decision 35's state file: the two are deliberately separate — the checkpoint file is state to resume a run, overwritten per save and consumed by restore; the journal file is the append-only record of what happened, consumed by review. A run resumed through `--state-file` continues appending to the same journal file in the restored tick domain, so the audit record spans the restart the state file healed.
- **Alternatives considered:** Persistence as contract — a `JournalSink` trait or mandated file schema in `dcs-core` (elevates an operations tool to wire contract every consumer must version, for a payload that is already contract-serialized); folding the journal into the checkpoint file (conflates resumable state with audit record — the checkpoint's overwrite-per-save shape fights append-only history, and a standby pulling checkpoints would drag a growing record); an embedded database or external log shipper (operational machinery the single-binary deployment does not otherwise need); persisting history samples too (volume and re-accumulation argue against it — the ring is a diagnostic window, not an archive).
- **Consequences:** The audit record survives restarts essentially without contract churn: endpoints and payloads are unchanged — #536's one additive exception is the `JournalEvent::RunBoundary` variant, which old files simply never carry and old consumers skip as an unknown event — and the ring stays memory-bounded while the file is the durable tail — file growth is a deployment-managed bound, not the contract's. Determinism is unaffected since appends ride the existing recording point. The choice is reversible: because the file serializes contract types, a later contract-level persistence story can adopt the same payloads.
- **Affected tickets:** #137 (this record); #163 implements it; interacts with #115/decision 35 (the sibling persistence surface, implemented); relies on #35 (the recorded streams) and decisions 7, 8, and 17.

### 37. Plant-wide supervision

- **Status:** Implemented (#164) — the page's overview mode is configured by repeated `?pair=<name>=<host:port>,<host:port>` URL parameters, the implementing ticket's recorded parameter shape: each parameter names one pair outright — every peer listed in the same `host:port` convention `?peer` uses, the serving origin not implied — with an optional display name before the `=`, the first peer's address standing in when absent. Implemented basis: the pair-as-one-controller view is implemented — `PairClient` polls both peers' `GET /role`, reads every data endpoint from the peer reporting settled `active`, routes commands active-only with mid-transition retry, and reports an unreachable peer as the named `PeerStatus::Unreachable` redundancy fault (#63, decision 19); the page is configured by `?peer=<host:port>` URL parameters beside its serving origin, and the JSON endpoints carry `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for cross-origin reads.
- **Problem:** A plant is more than one control loop: several controller pairs, each running its own model and serving its own monitor, operate one process. Today supervising a second pair means a second browser tab with its own `?peer` set — nothing presents the plant as a whole, and there is no recorded answer to whether plant-wide aggregation is page-side presentation or a served contract some process must own.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Aggregation stays page-side; the served contract remains per-controller. The page gains an overview mode configured by repeated pair-naming URL parameters in the same `?peer` convention, rendering one summary card per configured pair — the active peer's name, the reported roles and convergence, the unreachable-peer redundancy fault, and the I/O-health line — each card linking into the existing per-pair view for faceplates, trends, and commands. Plant topology — which pairs exist and what they are called — is deployment configuration carried in URLs and bookmarked links, not plant-model data: the model describes one controller's view, and nothing above it needs a new contract type. Cross-origin reads already enabled on the JSON endpoints let a page served by one monitor poll every configured pair.
- **Alternatives considered:** A served plant-aggregation contract — a `GET /plant` endpoint rolling up every pair (requires a process owning plant-wide state: either a new supervisor binary aggregating monitors, or monitors made aware of controllers beyond their pair — a second authority beside the per-pair contract for data already fetchable pair-wise); a dedicated supervisor process (a deployed component M8 does not otherwise need — the same reasoning that rejected a brokered bus in decision 12); stretching one `?peer` list across all controllers on the existing page (conflates pairs: the page would merge distinct tick domains and command targets into one faceplate set); plant-wide sections in the model schema (the model is per-pair engineered data; topology is deployment knowledge).
- **Consequences:** Plant-wide supervision is presentation, not contract: each pair's served surface stays the single authority for its loop, the overview degrades per pair — an unreachable pair is a card fault, not a page fault — and no new process or wire type enters the system. The cost is client-side polling per pair, bounded by the configured pair count and the existing cadence. A future served roll-up remains open: the per-pair contract types are already what it would serialize.
- **Affected tickets:** #137 (this record); the M8 overview-page ticket implements it; relies on #63 (the pair view) and #34/#51 (the page and its panes); decisions 8, 17, and 19.

### 38. Alarm lifecycle depth

- **Status:** Decided — the two-flag model stands as recorded in decision 33; no implementation ticket. Implemented basis: `latching-alarm` (#109) landed the two-flag semantics — `alarm` reporting standing limit state under `AlarmMonitor`'s hysteresis rule, `unacknowledged` latching on a fresh trip and clearing while the model-wired writable `ack` point reads `true` — acks journaled through the ordinary settled receipt and the latch carried in checkpoints. `AlarmMonitor` (#22) stays the auto-clearing sibling, and the alarm summary pane consuming the flags is filed as #108.
- **Problem:** Industrial alarm practice carries more lifecycle than acknowledgment: *shelving* (operator-temporary removal with automatic return), *suppression* (design- or state-based exclusion), and *alarm classes* (priority or severity grouping for display and response). M8 must decide whether the two-flag model grows these now or whether the recorded model is the lifecycle's extent.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The model stays as decided — `alarm` plus `unacknowledged` plus the writable `ack` point is the whole alarm lifecycle for M8. Each candidate extension is per-kind declared behavior needing no contract change, so nothing forces a decision now: a class would be a declared parameter or descriptor metadata on an alarm kind, shelving a stateful kind holding a shelved-for duration in ticks, and suppression-by-state is already expressible through existing interlock wiring gating the `in` port's observed value (decision 14 links). The platform's convention — new behavior lands as a new or extended kind beside descriptors and checkpoint vocabulary — covers each extension when a real plant's operations demand it, and #108's summary pane proceeds on the two flags alone.
