- **Alternatives considered:** Adding shelving now (a timer-bearing kind and a `shelve` writable point are buildable on the same machinery, but invent lifecycle depth no consuming surface yet renders); alarm classes as contract metadata — a `severity` field on the descriptor or signal (premature vocabulary for one alarm kind); suppression as a `Command` variant (suppression is engineered or derived state, not an ad-hoc command — and decision 30 keeps testbench-shaped controls off the operator path); dedicated alarm-lifecycle journal event types (rejected with decision 33 — transitions already appear in point history and the ack receipt).
- **Consequences:** The alarm contract stays minimal and every alarm kind speaks the same two-flag language the pane, history, and checkpoints already carry. The deferral is recorded rather than silent: when shelving or classes arrive they arrive as per-kind declarations — the same seam #109 used — so the extension path is proven, not speculative. Operators get one consistent acknowledgment semantic in the interim.
- **Affected tickets:** #137 (this record); #108 consumes the decided model; #109 implemented it; decision 33 records the semantics; decisions 16, 17, and 20 supply the descriptor, journal, and checkpoint machinery.

### 39. Command-path audit identity

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#165): `CommandReceipt` carries a serde-optional `actor` beside `command`/`outcome`; `POST /command` accepts the attributed envelope `{"command":…,"actor":…}` — the wire-shape choice this decision left to the ticket — beside the still-accepted bare `Command`; `Executor::submit_command_as`/`Peer::submit_command_as` are the attributed submit variants, and the monitor's `not_active` refusal stamps the actor identically. The page sources the identity from its `?operator=<name>` URL parameter — the page-side source choice — and submits the envelope on every command; `dcs-ctl` declares it through `--actor <name>` on the receipted subcommands with `DCS_ACTOR` as the configured default the flag overrides (#175) — the tool-side source choice — while `promote`/`demote` stay unattributed: the switch-request path carries no actor field. Implemented basis: every submitted `Command` settles into exactly one `CommandReceipt` — echoed command plus outcome (#20, decision 7) — journaled as `JournalEvent::CommandSettled` carrying that receipt (#35, decision 17).
- **Problem:** The vision names safety-related constraints among the platform's encapsulated concerns, and audit's first element is actor identity: the journal records that a setpoint moved at a tick but not who asked. `POST /command` is anonymous as implemented — a receipt cannot distinguish one console's write from another's, and the durable journal of decision 36 would preserve an unattributed record.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Operator commands carry a declared actor identity, journaled for audit. The `POST /command` submission accepts an optional `actor` — a free-form identity string the submitter supplies — carried through onto the `CommandReceipt` as a serde-optional field beside `command`/`outcome`, so existing payloads and receipts parse unchanged and the journaled `CommandSettled` entry gains the attribution for free by echoing the receipt it already carries. The implementing ticket recorded the envelope wire shape — `{"command": <Command>, "actor": "<identity>"}` beside the bare `Command` body, selected by either envelope key so a stray top-level `actor` on a bare command is a `400` rather than a silently dropped attribution. The page sources the identity from its `?operator=<name>` URL parameter and includes it on every submission; an absent actor journals as unattributed rather than rejected. Recorded honestly: this is *declared* identity — attestation, not authentication. The simulated transport has no auth layer; verifying an actor is deployment machinery (a fronting proxy or gateway) the contract deliberately does not prescribe. The field still earns its place: it makes attribution part of the receipt-answered contract, journaled at the scan boundary like every other audit entry, and a deployment that fronts the monitor can fill it from authenticated context without a contract change.
- **Alternatives considered:** An authenticated transport first (identity without verification is weaker — but authentication is a cross-cutting deployment concern: folding it into the command contract now couples the audit record to a mechanism the simulated system cannot exercise, while declared identity leaves the seam exactly where a deployment needs it); a separate audit API beside the journal (splits the run's ordered record — attribution belongs on the settled receipt the journal already carries); `actor` on `Command` itself (the enum's externally-tagged serde shape gains the field awkwardly, and identity is submission metadata — about who sent it, not part of the operation the executor applies); per-endpoint tokens mapped to identities (authentication by another name — the same deferral).
- **Consequences:** The audit trail gains attribution with no new event types, endpoints, or required fields — an old client's commands journal exactly as before, actorless. Checkpoint and peer traffic are unaffected: the actor lives on the submission and receipt path, not in executor state. The honest limit is recorded: until an authenticating front exists, actor strings are operator-declared and the journal proves *what was claimed*, not *who truly sent it* — sufficient for the simulated system and the documented extension point for a hardened deployment.
- **Affected tickets:** #137 (this record); #165 implemented it; relies on #20 (receipts), #35 (the journal), and #163 (the durable record it attributes); decisions 7, 8, and 17.

### 40. JSON Schema for the plant-model document

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#159): `dcs-model schema` prints the draft 2020-12 JSON Schema `PlantModel::json_schema` emits, and `crates/dcs-model/tests/schema.rs` sweeps the checked fixture corpus against it.
- **Problem:** Decision 3 chose JSON so non-Rust tooling can read the model, but nothing external could check a document without linking the Rust validator. The model is the shared contract, so its checkable surface should travel with the document format, not only with `PlantModel::load`.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** The schema is *emitted as data* — a hand-maintained document in `dcs-model`'s `schema` module, not generated from the serde types (e.g. via `schemars`). Hand authorship is deliberate: the schema intentionally diverges from deserialization in both directions, and deriving it from the types would lose the divergences that make it useful. The schema is *stricter* than serde where that serves engineering: `additionalProperties: false` everywhere (serde silently ignores unknown keys, so a misspelled field would evaporate), `version` as a `const` stamped from `MODEL_VERSION`, and `uniqueItems` on the four id-keyed collections (a sound approximation of id uniqueness — identical elements necessarily share an id, so nothing valid is rejected). It also expresses the intra-element rules `validate` owns but the type shape alone does not: an internal point's required `initial`, the bound-point ban on `initial`, `initial`'s variant matching `value_type`, and `writable` restricted to `In` points — plus the standard registry's known device-kind parameter shapes where conditional (`if`/`then`) subschemas can write them down: `sim-tcp`'s `address`/`timeout_ms`, `sim-bus`'s `registers` map, and `sim-scripted`'s `script` entries. `Option` fields admit `null`, matching serde. The drift check runs every checked-in `*.json` fixture through the emitted schema with a dev-dependency validator (the `jsonschema` crate): valid model documents — including their serde re-serializations — must pass, non-model documents must fail, and every `invalid/` fixture carries a pinned per-layer verdict so a schema or validator change flips a checked expectation instead of drifting silently.
- **Alternatives considered:** Generating the schema from the serde types (schema-on-derive cannot express the conditional intra-element rules, would mirror serde's silent unknown-key acceptance, and adds a derive dependency to `dcs-model` and `dcs-core` — rejected); a checked-in `.schema.json` file the CLI prints verbatim (equivalent, but the embedded document keeps `MODEL_VERSION` stamped from code and one canonical source; external tools fetch it through the subcommand); encoding the `sim*` prefix family's no-parameters rule (rejected — the open prefix cannot be pinned per-kind, and a checked-in valid document carries `sim*` parameters the standard factory would reject only at assembly, proving the constraint is not document-shaped).
- **Consequences:** What the schema cannot express is recorded rather than implied: cross-reference checks (channel bindings, signal `source`, connection endpoints naming declared elements), wiring checks (`from` produces / `to` consumes / kinds agree — they depend on the *referenced* element's declarations), general id uniqueness beyond identical elements, the semantic halves of the device-kind contracts (resolvable addresses, register-map coverage and uniqueness, script channel binding and tick ordering, script values matching channel kinds), and JSON's integer/float lexical distinction (schema compares numbers mathematically, so `1.0` passes an integer field serde rejects). Those stay with `load` and the assembly factories: the schema is a first screen for external tooling, not a replacement validator — a document passing it may still fail `PlantModel::load`, and the schema in turn rejects some documents `load` accepts (unknown keys, malformed known-kind parameters). The known-kind parameter sections encode the *standard* registry's contract; a deployment registering other device kinds extends or regenerates them.
- **Affected tickets:** #159 (this record and implementation); decision 3 (the serialization contract it describes); decision 29 (the device-kind seam whose parameter shapes it mirrors).

### 41. Duty/standby pump-group contract

- **Status:** Accepted — the contract below is proposed; the M9 implementation tickets (to be filed) land it. Implemented basis it builds on: `motor`'s per-equipment actuation verifies run feedback against the command and flags `fault` after `fault_ticks` disagreeing scans — fail-to-start and fail-to-stop detection in one mechanism (#52); `interlock`'s permissive-plus-trips gating and `bool-gate`/`sr-latch` cover interlock-chain wiring (#38, #52); `counter`, `timer`, and `totalizer` cover start counting and Float-rate integration (#85, #111); indexed per-element ports are an established convention — `interlock`'s `trip_1`…`trip_N` and `median-voter`'s `in_1`…`in_3` are discovered from the bound port names at construction (#38, #111); writable internal `In` points carry operator values through the receipted path (#30, #49, decisions 14, 18); and the descriptor and checkpoint conventions every kind follows are implemented (decisions 16, 11, 20).
- **Problem:** `docs/research/pumping-station.md` (#195) shows every owner specification treats the duty/standby group as one coordinated policy object: which of N pumps holds duty; rotation of that assignment by a declared policy — per-cycle alternation (sources [6], [9]), a timed or daily interval ([1] §42.4), or least accumulated run hours ([18], [19]); a lag pump staged on unmet demand at its own start level ([4], [9]) behind a simultaneous-start delay ([9], [10]); unavailable pumps excluded from staging ([5], [19]); and duty reassigned when a running pump's feedback proves failed ([5], [4], [18]). Whether those policies compose from the existing motor, latch, gate, counter, timer, manual-station, and interlock kinds — or need a new pump-group or process-assembly contract — is the M9 question, and if a contract, its declared I/O, parameter vocabulary, descriptor roles, and checkpoint obligations must be recorded.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** A new `pump-group` component kind in `dcs-blocks` — the duty/standby manager — plus a `dcs-build` builder-level station composition helper. The split answers the note's open composition question (its gap 7): the group's behavior is per-scan stateful policy — a rotation position and run-hours ranking are run state — so it belongs in an executor component, while the station as one typed artifact (N motors, level measurement, the alarm set, operator points) is construction-time composition, decision 31's seam. Composing the policy itself from `bool-gate`/`sr-latch`/`counter`/`timer` wiring was evaluated and rejected per the note's gap 1: it re-implements the policy per station with no named semantics, and two required pieces have no existing home — the rotation position, and per-pump Bool run-hours accumulation with least-run ranking (`totalizer` integrates a Float rate; Bool-feedback hours have no feed — gap 3).
  Declared I/O (proposed; per-pump ports indexed `_1`…`_N` under the `trip_N` convention, the pump count discovered from bound port names):
  - `demand` (`In`, `Int`) — the stage count the station requests: 0 all stopped, 1 duty only, 2 duty plus lag. One input carries whatever produces the station's pump request — the decision-42 threshold chain or staged continuous control — and covers duplex through lead/lag/standby shapes [12].
  - Per pump `i`: `cmd_i` (`Out`, `Bool` — the group's run request, wired to that pump's `motor.cmd`), `run_i` (`In`, `Bool` — run feedback, normally the same field point the motor verifies), `fault_i` (`In`, `Bool` — the motor's proven feedback-failure flag, so the group responds to verified failure rather than raw signal states), and `avail_i` (`In`, `Bool` — the aggregated availability: not faulted, not locally selected, not maintenance-inhibited, permissives satisfied — the note's gap 2 resolved as a declared group input; the aggregation itself composes upstream from `bool-gate`/`interlock` wiring because which conditions feed "available" is plant-specific data, not kind semantics).
  - `duty` (`Out`, `Int` — which pump holds duty), `staged` (`Out`, `Int` — how many are commanded), `none_available` (`Out`, `Bool`), and `all_faulted` (`Out`, `Bool`) — the group-raised station conditions the alarm set consumes (gap 6).
  Parameters (proposed — all within the `Value` vocabulary of `Bool`/`Int`/`Float`, so the rotation policy is a documented `Int` code): `rotation` (`Int` — 0 alternate each pump-down cycle [6, 9], the reference default and an unvalidated customer assumption per the note's assumptions; 1 timed interval [1 §42.4]; 2 least-run-hours first [18, 19]), `rotation_ticks` (`Int` — the timed policy's interval), `start_delay_ticks` (`Int` — minimum ticks between successive pump starts, the simultaneous-start delay [9, 10]), `restage_delay_ticks` (`Int` — a de-staged pump held out [19]), and `min_off_ticks` (`Int` — the tick-domain form of the engineered starts-per-hour bound [7, 8]).
  Behavior: `demand` ≥ 2 stages the lag at its own `start_delay_ticks`; on falling demand the lag de-stages first [4]. A running pump whose `fault_i` asserts or whose `avail_i` drops hands duty to the next available pump automatically at the scan — the evidence-supported response [5, 4, 18]; the alarm-and-wait alternative is the note's recorded unvalidated assumption. Latched lockout-until-reset [18] is not group machinery: it composes in wiring as an `sr-latch` on `fault_i` feeding `avail_i`, cleared by a writable internal reset point — the decision-33 writable-point pattern — so whether the reference station latches a failed pump out is likewise assumption-marked station wiring. Non-`Good` per-pump inputs take the fail-safe reading the library already uses (`avail_i`/`run_i`/`fault_i` non-`Good` reads as unavailable/failed, matching `motor`'s cannot-prove-agreement rule); a non-`Good` `demand` holds the last `Good` stage request — the implementing ticket documents the precise rule.
  Descriptor roles: the existing four-role `PortRole` vocabulary suffices, recorded as no extension — `demand` → `Setpoint`, `cmd_i` → `Output`, `run_i`/`avail_i`/`fault_i` → `ProcessValue` (measured equipment state the component acts on), `duty`/`staged`/`none_available`/`all_faulted` → `Status`.
  Checkpoint obligations: the rotation position, the per-pump run-hours accumulators, and the in-flight start/restage/min-off timers are run state the kind must capture and restore under decision 20's rule — tuned parameters folded into the same vocabulary — so a tracking standby inherits the duty assignment and the counters (`WW-LCM-001`'s station-facing reach; the note records that no source addresses controller redundancy — this carryover is our proposal, assumption-marked).
- **Alternatives considered:** Per-station composition from `bool-gate`/`sr-latch`/`counter`/`timer` (rejected — the note's gap 1: no named semantics, and rotation position and run-hours ranking have no home); extending `motor` into a group kind (couples the per-equipment actuation contract to group policy, and `motor` stays reusable alone for non-pump loads); a builder-level assembly spec only, no kind (the policy is per-scan stateful behavior — construction-time wiring cannot hold a rotation position); a general process-assembly contract (premature — the evidence supports the pump group specifically, and inventing assembly vocabulary no source names repeats what decision 31 already covers at the composition layer).
- **Consequences:** One new kind plus one builder-level helper carry the whole group semantics; the status outputs feed the station alarm set with no new alarm contract (decision 43). Availability aggregation stays declared station wiring — the descriptor's `avail_i` port gives it a named landing instead of an ad-hoc gate. The `Int`-coded policy vocabulary is recorded honestly: `Value` carries no enum, so the parameter's documented codes are the contract. The checkpoint vocabulary — rotation position, run-hours, delay timers — deploys to both peers together under decision 11's strictness, and every assumption-marked behavior above is flagged for customer validation before the acceptance scenario fixes its defaults.
- **Affected tickets:** #202 (this record); the M9 pump-group kind ticket and the M9 station-composition ticket (to be filed); requirements `WW-CTL-001` and `WW-LCM-001`; relies on #52, #38, #85, #111, #30, #49 and decisions 5, 11, 16, 20; evidence `docs/research/pumping-station.md` (#195).

### 42. Station level control and measurement failover

- **Status:** Accepted — proposed contracts on implemented seams. Implemented basis: `alarm-monitor` plus `sr-latch` can already hand-wire threshold behavior (#22, #52); `pid`, `analog-input`, and `signal-filter` implement the continuous path and `median-voter` the 2oo3 redundant-measurement path (#11, #110, #111); `manual-station` gives a bumpless operator/manual transfer on an analog demand and `override-select` an operator-driven source switch (#110); writable internal `In` points carry operator setpoints and mode switches through the receipted path, and `set_parameter` tunes descriptor-ranged parameters (#30, #49, #82, decisions 14, 18, 20); the quality model every input carries is decision 2's.
- **Problem:** The note's evidence gives the station two control semantics — the dominant ordered start/stop threshold chain (low-water cut-off, duty start/stop, lag start, high-level alarm — sources [4], [10]) with operator-adjustable setpoints ([5], [7]), and continuous constant-level control for variable-speed stations ([2], [8]) — plus an engineered bad-measurement rule (hardwired float backup [6], four-point float fallback [10], backup-float inputs [20], multi-transmitter master-level selection [5]) and a manual-takeover path that is a distinct mode with declared protections ([6], [5]). The decisions needed: which seam expresses each, and which behaviors are contract gaps.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** Four parts, each naming its seam.
  *Threshold control* is a new `threshold-chain` kind: one declared ordered setpoint table as parameters — `cutoff`, `stop`, `start`, `lag_start`, `high` — reading `level` (`In`, `Float`) and emitting `demand` (`Out`, `Int` — the stage count feeding `pump-group.demand`) plus per-threshold `Bool` flags the alarm set consumes. The start/stop pairs are the hysteresis. Setpoints are descriptor-declared parameters, so the operator-adjustable requirement [5] lands on the implemented `set_parameter` path with declared ranges — no writable-point wiring per setpoint. A non-`Good` `level` falls to the declared `on_bad_demand` (`Int` parameter) — the declared safe behavior when measurement cannot be trusted; which demand that is (hold, stop, or run) is the note's recorded all-measurement-bad assumption, customer-validation marked.
  *Continuous control* composes from existing kinds: `analog-input`/`signal-filter`/`pid` hold the wet well at a level setpoint [2], with owner bounds like minimum-speed cycling [8] expressed as declared parameters. The reference station ships threshold mode; which form is primary for the first client is an unvalidated assumption the note records (`WW-CTL-002` keeps both). A variable-speed group's per-pump analog commands are beyond this contract — `pump-group`'s `demand` is a discrete stage count.
  *Bad-measurement failover* is a new `failover-select` kind: `primary`/`backup` (`In`, `Float`), `out` (`Out`, `Float` — primary while `Good`, else backup), and `backup_active` (`Out`, `Bool` — the alarmed transition source; backup-mode engagement is alarmed as critical [5]). Issue #502's QA finding — a failed standby producing no indication while the primary served — added the optional `backup_unhealthy` (`Out`, `Bool`) port, bound only where the model wires it through the same `ComponentSpec::get` optional-port seam `flow-paced-ratio`'s `trim` uses, so documents emitted before the port existed assemble unchanged. Where bound it asserts while the backup's own sample is non-`Good` or non-finite — the same failed-measurement predicate the selection rule applies to the primary — independent of which source is serving, so the loss of the unused leg annunciates before it is needed. `override-select` cannot express this — its `select` is an operator input, not quality-driven — and `median-voter` is 2oo3 voting, not primary/backup failover (the note's gap 5). When every source is bad, `out` carries `Bad` quality and the threshold chain's `on_bad_demand` rule applies. The measurement architecture itself — single transmitter plus float backup [6, 10, 20] versus redundant transmitters with master selection [5] — is an unvalidated assumption; the kind covers the analog failover shape, and a float-direct backup is station wiring (`digital-input`-conditioned floats feeding the demand path), the simulated stand-in for [6]'s electrically separate circuit.
  *Manual takeover* composes from existing kinds: a writable internal mode point per pump — an operator value through the receipted path (decisions 14, 18) — selects between `cmd_i` and the operator's hand request through `bool-gate`/`override-select` wiring; `manual-station` covers the analog-demand case and reports `manual_active`. Which protections persist in manual — over-temperature does while the low-level cut-off may not [6] — is station wiring (which signals feed the `interlock`'s permissive and trips versus which gate only automatic mode), not contract, and its scope is assumption-marked.
- **Alternatives considered:** Hand-wiring the chain from `alarm-monitor` plus `sr-latch` per setpoint (the note's gap 4 — it works, but scatters one engineered table across many instances, each with its own quality handling, and nothing can check the ordered-setpoint invariant at build time); setpoints as `pump-group` parameters (couples station-level semantics to the equipment group — the same group serves demands that are not level-driven, and the table belongs to the measurement); quality-switching added to `override-select` (its `select` port is a `Bool` — failure-driven switching is a new semantic, not a parameter, and conflating operator and failure switching muddies the alarmed-transition distinction [5]); failover inside `pump-group` (measurement failover is station scope — it serves the threshold chain and any other level consumer, not the pump group); a `Bad`-quality default inside `threshold-chain` with no `on_bad_demand` (silences the all-bad case — the note records it as an engineered, owner-specified behavior, so it must be declared).
- **Consequences:** Two small kinds (`threshold-chain`, `failover-select`) plus wiring conventions carry the whole level-control contract; every station behavior names its seam. The assumption-marked behaviors — primary control form, measurement architecture, all-sources-bad demand, manual-protection scope — stay visible for customer validation rather than silently defaulting. Alarmed transitions (backup engaged, measurement fault) surface as ordinary status outputs feeding decision 43's set.
- **Affected tickets:** #202 (this record); the M9 `threshold-chain`, `failover-select`, and station-composition tickets (to be filed); #502 (`backup_unhealthy`); requirements `WW-CTL-002` and `WW-OPS-003`; relies on #22, #110, #111, #82, #49 and decisions 2, 14, 18, 20; evidence `docs/research/pumping-station.md` (#195).

### 43. Station alarm set on the two-flag lifecycle

- **Status:** Accepted — the lifecycle is implemented, the Bool-input sibling landed as the dedicated `bool-latching-alarm` kind (#219 — the new-small-kind option, since the Bool `in` and the parameterless signature are a distinct contract one kind string would hide), and the set's mapping is proposed. Implemented basis: `latching-alarm` (#109) carries the two-flag semantics — `alarm` reporting standing limit state under `AlarmMonitor`'s hysteresis rule, `unacknowledged` latching on a fresh trip and clearing while the model-wired writable `ack` point reads `true` — acks journaled through the ordinary settled receipt and the latch carried in checkpoints (decision 33); `alarm-monitor` stays the auto-clearing sibling (#22); the page's alarm pane consumes `alarm`/`unacknowledged` status-role ports and offers the ack affordance on writable `ack` points (#108); the durable journal survives restart (#163, decision 36); and decision 38 records that shelving, suppression, and classes stay deferred as per-kind extensions.
- **Problem:** The note fixes which alarms a representative station carries — the owner/regulator minimum (power failure, pump failure, dry-well sump and wet-well high water, unauthorized entry, or any other station malfunction, on a backed-up supply [1] §46), the owner lists' additions (per-pump failure-to-start and failure-to-pump, thermal and moisture faults, float/transducer failure, VFD failure, generator running/failed [10, 11]; backup-control-mode engagement alarmed as critical [5]) — and each must map onto the decision-38 lifecycle as either a wired `latching-alarm` instance or a kind behavior, without re-opening the lifecycle itself.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** The reference station's alarm set, each element named to its seam:
  - *High wet-well level* and *low level / dry-run* — wired `latching-alarm` instances on the level path (fed by `threshold-chain`'s flags or the selected level directly); the standing flag is process truth and the latch awaits the writable `ack` point, matching the latched high-level-alarm reset [10] requires. No contract work.
  - *Per-pump fail-to-start / fail-to-stop* — detection is `motor`'s implemented `fault` kind behavior (feedback disagreement after `fault_ticks` covers both directions [18]); the alarm view is a wired latching instance — which exposes the one contract gap: `latching-alarm`'s `in` is `Float` and the typed port contract never coerces, so Bool-sourced alarms need a Bool-input latching sibling — a new small kind, or a `point_kind`-dispatched variant through the seam `ComponentSpec::point_kind` already provides; the implementing ticket's documented choice, either way preserving the `in`/`ack`/`alarm`/`unacknowledged` vocabulary exactly.
  - *Measurement fault* and *backup-mode engaged* — `failover-select`'s `backup_active` plus the level point's non-`Good` quality, each wired into the same Bool latching behavior; the transition is alarmed per [5].
  - *No pump available* and *all pumps faulted* — `pump-group`'s `none_available`/`all_faulted` status outputs wired into the same Bool latching behavior ([1] §46's "any other station malfunction"; the note's gap 6: no new alarm contract — the group must expose the conditions, which decision 41 does).
  - *Station power failure* — a field `Bool` point into the same Bool latching behavior. *Communication failure* is served by decision 22's I/O-health surface — link state, per-direction counters, last `IoError` — rendered as health, distinct from process alarms (#83, #107); whether a discrete comm-fail alarm is additionally required is a customer assumption the note records.
  - *Equipment-fault conditions* (thermal, moisture, check-valve failure-to-pump [10, 11]) — field `Bool` points feeding the same Bool latching behavior, and simultaneously feeding `avail_i` wiring where they should exclude the pump.
  Which alarms latch until acknowledged versus auto-clear is per-alarm model wiring — `latching-alarm` versus `alarm-monitor` — and the required latch set is the note's recorded customer-validation question, assumption-marked.
- **Alternatives considered:** Extending `latching-alarm`'s `in` to accept `Bool` by loosening the declared port kind (the value-kind matching is strict and never-coercing by decision 2 — loosening it for one port undermines the wiring checks every kind relies on); `alarm-monitor` for the equipment alarms (auto-clear loses the acknowledged record the owner lists imply [10] and decision 33 chose the two-flag model to provide); a dedicated alarm `Command` variant or alarm-specific journal events (rejected with decision 33 — the writable `ack` point and the settled receipt already carry the semantics and the audit); promoting alarm classes or priorities for this set (decision 38's recorded deferral — per-kind declared extensions when a customer's operations demand them, not contract additions).
- **Consequences:** The station alarm set needs at most one small kind — the Bool-input latching sibling — plus wired instances of existing kinds; every alarm speaks the same two-flag language the pane, history, checkpoints, and journal already carry, and acknowledgment needs no new command path. Decision 38 deferred priority, shelving, and suppression at the time. The later IJmuiden incident evidence and accepted `WW-ALM-001`–`WW-ALM-005` requirements reopen that roadmap scope while preserving this two-flag acknowledgment axis as the compatible base; a new decision must define the additional contract before implementation. The latch-until-ack set and the comm-fail question remain customer policy rather than silently decided.
- **Affected tickets:** #202 (this record); the M9 Bool-latching-alarm and station-alarm-wiring tickets (to be filed); requirement `WW-OPS-002`; relies on #109, #108, #22, #163 and decisions 33, 38, 22, 17; evidence `docs/research/pumping-station.md` (#195).

### 44. Station dynamics document stays beside the model

- **Status:** Accepted — decided; no implementation beyond M9's checked-in documents. Implemented basis: `dcs-plant-server --dynamics FILE` merges a plain JSON list of `ProcessElement` declarations into the served `ChannelMap`, validating each element as it merges (#81, decision 24); the element vocabulary is `first_order_lag`, `second_order_lag`, `integrator`, `dead_time`, `noise` (#8, #37, #135, #156); and `dcs-build` emits exactly the versioned `PlantModel` document, depending on `dcs-core` and `dcs-model` only (#112, decision 31).
- **Problem:** The reference station needs two artifacts — the plant model (control contract) and the dynamics document (the simulated world's physics: wet-well inflow and level response). M9 must record whether the dynamics document also composes through the typed-build path, or stays a checked-in document beside the model.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The dynamics document stays a checked-in declaration list beside the model — decision 24's boundary applied unchanged. `dcs-build`'s product is the `PlantModel`; the dynamics document is `dcs-sim`'s `ProcessElement` vocabulary — testbench content no controller reads ("the model describes what the controller sees, the dynamics document describes what the world does"). A typed dynamics builder would put a `dcs-sim` dependency into the model producer — inverting decision 31's dependency direction — to gain compile-time checks for a document whose only consumer already validates it at merge. The station therefore checks in both documents: the model emitted through `dcs-build` (`WW-ENG-001`'s typed-composition evidence), the dynamics beside it, merged and validated through `dcs-plant-server --dynamics`; the acceptance scenario reproduces from the two checked-in artifacts.
- **Alternatives considered:** Extending `dcs-build` to emit dynamics too (a `dcs-sim` dependency in the model producer — the inversion decision 31's crate boundary exists to prevent); dynamics as `PlantModel` schema (rejected with decision 24 — the control contract would carry simulation-only content a real deployment cannot produce); a separate typed dynamics-builder crate with its own spec table pinned to the `ProcessElement` vocabulary under the drift-test convention (a real option, deferred — M9's acceptance needs the document reproducible and validated, which merge-time checking already gives; reopen if dynamics authoring grows error-prone).
- **Consequences:** `WW-ENG-001`'s typed-composition acceptance applies to the model document; the dynamics document's check is the plant server's validating merge plus the scripted scenario. The direction stays clean — testbench content never enters the control contract's authoring seam — and no new crate or vocabulary joins M9's scope.
- **Affected tickets:** #202 (this record); the M9 station-composition and acceptance-scenario tickets (to be filed); requirements `WW-ENG-001` and `WW-FND-001`; relies on #81 and decisions 24, 31.

### 45. Declared input freshness budgets

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#196; freshness judged in the run's own tick domain under #725): `io_point` declarations carry an optional `stale_after_ticks`, the point map rides it into the executor, and the input phase stamps `Uncertain(Stale)` once a field input's driver-returned report has gone unchanged for more than the budget in scan ticks.
- **Problem:** WW-OPS-003 asks operators and control components to distinguish stale data. `QualityReason::Stale` has existed in the contract since the signal model — every quality-propagating kind honors it — but no runtime path produced it: only scripted playback or fault injection could name the reason. A field source that stops refreshing looked exactly like a healthy one, because the executor re-stamps every input read with the scan tick and the last value reads `Good` forever.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Freshness is *declared*, not inferred. `stale_after_ticks` is an optional non-negative integer on field `in` `io_point` declarations, following the optional-field convention (`writable`, `channel`): documents predating it load and serialize unchanged, the model version stays 1, and validation confines it to channel-bound `in` points (`StaleOut`, `StaleInternal` elsewhere) — an `out` point is never read and an internal point never reaches a driver, so a budget there could only mislead. Assembly carries the budget onto the field point's `PointSpec`; the executor's input phase tracks each budgeted point's last-seen driver report — an unchanged report ages one scan tick per scan in the run's own tick domain, and once that age exceeds the budget the landed quality merges `Uncertain(Stale)` by the worst-of rule, so a driver-reported `Bad` or worse-named `Uncertain` is never improved, while the first changed report — value, quality, or stamp — returns the driver's own quality on that scan. Judging the report's age in the run domain rather than subtracting the driver stamp is what keeps the contract honest across drivers stamping in a foreign domain (#725): a remote driver's `Sample::tick` lives in the field owner's step domain, which a paused or demoted owner leaves frozen while the run ticks on, so a cross-domain lag never closes once it opens — a resumed field would serve fresh data as stale forever, and a run resumed behind the driver's domain would saturate the lag at zero and never mark stale. The executor remains the only timestamp authority: the image stamp is the scan tick either way, and the driver report is evidence for the check only — so drivers need no new contract beyond the `Sample` the existing sim, remote, and bus protocols already carry. The transition reaches operators through the existing machinery: the recorder's quality diff journals `QualityChanged` at the scan tick and the page renders the named `stale` reason — no new event, endpoint, or wire type. A driver whose samples carry no usable freshness signal — one that returns a changed report every read, or one whose report never varies — makes the declaration inert or always-stale; `docs/model-reference.md` records the rule to declare the field only where the source distinguishes fresh samples from held ones.
- **Alternatives considered:** An executor-side refresh detector watching every field input (no declared budget — a uniform timeout would guess at per-source rates the model knows better, and an undeclared point could never opt out; the declaration also travels with the document reviewers sign off); deriving staleness from wall-clock or scan-period time (ticks are the run's only deterministic clock — wall-clock would break the replayable, identical-runs guarantee the scripted tests pin); a new journal event or wire type for staleness (rejected — the quality-transition path already attributes and renders named reasons, and `Stale` was already a first-class `QualityReason`); drivers marking their own samples stale (the field does not know the consumer's scan cadence — freshness is a contract between the declared budget and the scan tick, which only the executor holds); comparing the driver-stamped tick against the scan tick directly (the original #196 mechanism, retired under #725 — it silently assumes driver stamps share the run's tick domain, which a remote driver's does not).
- **Consequences:** Field `in` points gain an opt-in freshness contract with no change for undeclared points, failed reads, forced points, or internal points; `PointSpec` carries the budget beside `writable` as point metadata; `dcs-build` composes it through `field_input_stale_after`; and a stale input's transition is journaled, rendered, and checkpoint-neutral like any other quality change — staleness is recomputed from field evidence every scan, never latched.
- **Affected tickets:** #196 (this record and implementation); #725 (freshness judged in the run's own tick domain — the remote driver's stamp lives in the field owner's step domain, which an unstepped window leaves permanently offset from the run's); relies on #5 (the signal model supplying `QualityReason::Stale`), #9 (the executor's input phase and timestamp authority), #83 (the I/O-health surface staleness sits beside), #35 (the quality-transition journal the transition rides), and WW-OPS-003.

### 46. Model and dynamics artifact distribution

- **Status:** Decided — the implemented mechanism stands: every process loads its documents from paths the deployment supplies, and no served or pushed distribution enters the contract. Implemented basis: `dcs-controller <model.json>` loads and validates the model from a local path (#19); a `--revised` instance loads the revised document as its own positional model path — the flag requires `--standby` or `--state-file` (#87, #563; decisions 25, 35); `dcs-plant-server` takes the model positionally plus `--dynamics FILE` (#81, decision 24); the restart state file and the journal file are likewise operator-placed paths (#115, #163); the container images mount their documents read-only and take their container paths — the model is the shared contract each container mounts (#39, #157, `docs/packaging.md`); and the engineering surface — `dcs-model diff`/`lint`/`schema` and `dcs-controller --check` (#114, #136, #159, #160) — operates on checked-in documents. What crosses the wire is identity, not content: the checkpoint's `model_fingerprint` names the document it was captured under (#66, decision 27), which is what makes #87's crossing deliberate.
- **Problem:** A revision roll needs the revised model at the new peer, and a plant container needs its model and dynamics at startup — and the pair must provably agree on which document runs. The open question is who carries content: a contract mechanism — a monitor serving the model for a peer or a rig to pull, or a pushed document — or the deployment itself, with the wire carrying only identity.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Distribution stays operator-supplied paths: each process loads the document its deployment hands it, and the pair's agreement on which revision runs is negotiated by fingerprint rather than by moving content over the wire. The documents are engineered artifacts — produced, diffed, linted, and checked against the emitted schema before they reach a rig — so the deployment mechanism (read-only bind mounts today) places exactly what engineering approved. A revision roll is then: start a standby whose own model path is the revised document, armed `--revised`; the fingerprint crossing carries the intent, and the carryover rule names what transfers. No endpoint serves the model — none exists — and none is added.
- **Alternatives considered:** A `GET /model` endpoint a standby or a rig pulls from a running peer (makes a running controller a distribution authority — a revision would depend on the active's availability at the moment a new peer starts, and the document's home is engineering tooling, not a process); a pushed `POST /model` deployment path (the same content-over-wire coupling, plus a mutating endpoint the contract deliberately lacks — the monitor's POST surface is commands, promotion, and scans); artifacts baked into each image (couples the image to one plant — the image is the binary, the mount is the contract, per the packaging discipline); a fetched manifest or artifact-registry service (another deployed component answering a question the fingerprint already answers).
- **Consequences:** Every consumer of a document gets it from the deployment, never from a peer — distribution is uniform across the revision roll, plant startup, the state file, and the journal file. What the contract must guarantee — that the peers agree which document runs — the fingerprint and its named negotiation errors already provide: skewed mounts surface as `FingerprintMismatch`/`Degraded`, not silent divergence. The recorded limit: nothing checks that two mounted documents are the *intended* pair beyond the fingerprint — which is exactly its job. A later served distribution would still be only a transport choice beneath the same identity contract.
- **Affected tickets:** #179 (this record); relies on #87 (the revision roll), #81 (the dynamics document), #66 (the fingerprint), #115 and #163 (the sibling file artifacts), and #39/#157 (the container packaging); decisions 3, 24, 25, and 27.

### 47. Plant-level topology stays deployment configuration

- **Status:** Decided — URL configuration stands; no plant-level document or index enters the served contract. Implemented basis: decision 37's overview mode is implemented — repeated `?pair=<name>=<host:port>,<host:port>` parameters each name one pair outright, one card per pair polls that pair's `/role` and `/snapshot`, and an unreachable pair degrades to a named card fault while the others keep updating (#164). The deferred plant index has since earned its place on the side the decision assigned it — deployment configuration, not the plant model or the wire: the consumer deployment manifest's optional `topology` section declares the deployment's named pairs, each pair's standby wiring closing inside it, validated against the rig definition (#856) — the declaration a deployment generates its `?pair=` overview URL from, carrying no runtime, wire, or persisted-format change.
- **Problem:** With the overview landed, plant topology — which pairs exist, what they are called, where they are reachable — lives only in the page's URL. Decision 37 deferred the contract question; the open issue is whether a plant-level document or index now earns a contract place — a schema section, a served topology endpoint, or a checked-in topology artifact — or whether deployment configuration remains the answer.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** URL configuration stands. Topology is deployment knowledge, not engineered plant data: the same station model deploys as a test pair or a production pair, and which pairs exist is a fact about the deployment, not about any one model. Each candidate contract home was weighed: a `PlantModel` section is the scope violation decision 37 recorded (the model is per-pair engineered data); a served roll-up endpoint needs a process owning plant-wide state (decision 37's supervisor alternative); a checked-in topology document would be an artifact with no consumer — the page takes URLs, and no tooling exists to generate them from a document. The recorded mechanism is the `?pair=` convention plus bookmarks and deployment templates; a deployment that automates it generates the query from its own inventory.
- **Alternatives considered:** A served `GET /topology` (a monitor cannot serve a topology it is part of without self-knowledge — the data would be configured into it anyway, moving the problem rather than removing it); a `PlantModel` topology section (rejected with decision 37); a checked-in plant index — a document naming the pairs and their roles that a generator or page tooling consumes (the real candidate, deferred: inventing it now defines a schema nothing validates against a consumer; it earns its place when a deployment's pair count makes hand-authored URLs the error source); deriving topology from `Signal.group` areas (display metadata, not deployment reachability).
- **Consequences:** The contract stays per-pair; supervision stays page-side presentation per decision 37. Plant topology has exactly one recorded interface — the overview's `?pair=` parameters — and the honest limit is recorded with it: there is no authoritative plant index, so a deployment's pair set lives in its own tooling. The decision is reopenable the moment a consumer exists that the URL cannot serve.
- **Affected tickets:** #179 (this record); #164 implemented the URL convention; #856 landed the deferred plant index as the deployment manifest's optional `topology` section; decision 37 records the per-pair boundary.

### 48. Audit depth: the declared-actor seam stands

- **Status:** Decided — declared identity plus the recorded fronting-proxy seam is the audit answer; no next contract step is warranted now. Implemented basis: `POST /command` accepts the attributed envelope beside the bare `Command`, `CommandReceipt` carries `actor` serde-optionally, `CommandSettled` journals the attribution, and the page stamps its `?operator=` identity on every submission (#165, decision 39); the journal persists across restart through `--journal-file` (#163, decision 36).
- **Problem:** Decision 39 recorded the honest limit — `actor` is attestation, not authentication; the journal proves what was claimed, not who sent it — and deferred verification to deployment machinery. With M8 landed the question is whether a next contract step — transport authentication, signed identity, authorization — earns a place now, or whether the recorded seam is the answer.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The seam stands. The contract's obligations are met in full: every settled command carries its attribution into a durable, tick-ordered record. Verification is deployment machinery by design — an authenticating proxy fronts the monitor and supplies `actor` from authenticated context with no contract change; that is the seam working as recorded, not a gap in it. Adding authentication to `dcs-monitor` itself would couple the audit contract to machinery the simulated system cannot exercise — no identity provider, credential store, or threat model exists inside the no-live-deployment bound for it to be honest against. A next contract step is reopened by a concrete requirement — a customer access-control or audit-integrity specification — not invented ahead of one.
- **Alternatives considered:** TLS plus authentication inside the embedded monitor server (duplicates what a standard fronting proxy does at the cost of certificate and identity machinery in a crate whose discipline is a small, dependency-light surface — and unexercisable within the bound); signed actor assertions (key distribution and verification are deployment infrastructure the contract would have to carry — a PKI concern with no simulated counterpart); command authorization roles on the contract (no requirement names roles; the writable flag already bounds *what* is commandable — who may command is the proxy's question); retracting `actor` until verification exists (loses the journaled attribution the seam exists to carry — an unattributed durable record is worse than a declared one).
- **Consequences:** The journal remains a faithful record of declared attribution; the documented limit stands until a deployment fronts the monitor — at which point `actor` is the field the proxy fills, with no contract or client change. The decision keeps the safety-related-constraints surface honest: what the platform guarantees — a complete, durable, attributed record — is separated from what the deployment guarantees — that the record's claims are verified.
- **Affected tickets:** #179 (this record); #165 implemented the attribution; #163 the durable record; decisions 7, 8, 17, 36, and 39.

### 49. The demonstration rig as a checked-in definition

- **Status:** Accepted — decided; a rig-definition ticket (to be filed) lands the checked-in compose file; everything below the file is implemented. Implemented basis: the controller image (#39) and the plant-server image (#157) are checked-in packaging artifacts; `docs/packaging.md` documents the full rig — network, the plant container with mounted model and dynamics, the redundant controller pair on `--remote`/`--standby`/`--listen`, promotion by `POST /promote` or `--auto-promote` — including the cross-host form with published addresses; and the scripted tests prove the semantics end to end with real processes (`hot_swap.rs` #64, `failover.rs` #65, `model_revision.rs` #87, `lifecycle.rs` #161, `full_stack.rs` #116).
- **Problem:** The vision's demonstration is the two-machine hot-swap: a redundant instance on another machine taking over without interrupting the process. The packaged form of that demonstration — the plant-plus-pair container rig — has its images and its documented run commands, but the rig's topology exists only as prose: which containers exist, what they mount, how the standby is wired to the active's monitor, which ports the UI reaches. The question is whether a checked-in multi-container rig definition — a compose file or a documented equivalent — completes the demonstration within the no-live-deployment bound, and what beyond the two Dockerfiles the packaged demo requires.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The rig definition earns a checked-in place as a compose file — the artifact the two Dockerfiles presuppose. The packaged demo requires, beyond the images: the rig's topology as data (services, network, mounts, flags, peer wiring, published monitor ports), the mounted artifacts (the checked-in model and dynamics documents, per decision 46), and the switchover procedure — already documented in `docs/packaging.md`. A compose file is the standard, statically inspectable declaration of the first item: `docker compose config` can check it where prose commands cannot be checked, and `docker compose up` reproduces the rig without transcription. The recorded boundaries keep it honest: it is a single-host orchestration declaration — the two-machine shape remains the documented cross-host form (published `host:port` in place of network names) — and within the no-live-deployment bound nothing executes either; like the Dockerfiles, it is a checked-in packaging artifact that defines no deployment. The scripted tests remain the behavioral proof — the rig definition makes the packaged demonstration reproducible, it does not re-prove the semantics.
- **Alternatives considered:** The documented command sequence as the permanent rig definition (works — `docs/packaging.md` is already the documented equivalent — but prose cannot be statically checked and drifts silently from the binary's flags; the compose file becomes the declaration the document references); a bespoke rig-runner script (non-standard tooling duplicating what compose already is); Kubernetes or other orchestrator manifests (machinery the demonstration does not need); treating the scripted tests as the whole demonstration (they prove behavior with real processes — they do not exercise the packaged artifact set, which is what this decision covers).
- **Consequences:** The packaged demonstration is three checked-in artifacts plus a procedure: two Dockerfiles, the rig definition, and the documented switchover steps. The file must stay in lockstep with `docs/packaging.md` — one is the declaration, the other the explanation. The two-machine demonstration within the bound is the same declaration plus the documented published-port variant; an actual second machine stays outside the workflow, recorded rather than silently absent.
- **Affected tickets:** #179 (this record); the M9 rig-definition ticket (to be filed); relies on #39 and #157 (the images) and #116/#161 (the scripted proof); decisions 9, 10, and 15.

### 50. Flow-paced ratio demand

- **Status:** Accepted — the contract below is proposed; the M10 implementation tickets (to be filed) land it. Implemented basis it builds on: every input carries decision 2's quality, so a bad pacing-flow signal is already visible to components; `pid`, `analog-input`, and `signal-filter` implement the residual-analyzer loop a trim rides on (#11, #110); `failover-select` covers a declared backup pacing source (#211, decision 42); `rate-limiter` bounds demand slew (#85); writable internal `In` points carry operator values through the receipted path (#30, #49, decisions 14, 18); `set_parameter` tunes descriptor-declared parameters against declared ranges (#82, decision 20); optional ports are an established seam — `ComponentSpec::get` binds a port the model may leave unwired; and the descriptor and checkpoint conventions every kind follows are implemented (decisions 16, 11, 20).
- **Problem:** The note's gap 1: no kind computes "dose per flow unit × measured flow → bounded actuator demand" with declared dose and rate limits, a clamped indication, and a declared response to a bad flow signal. Composing it from `pid` misuses the controller — pacing is feed-forward scaling with no error term — and `bool-gate` wiring has no arithmetic semantics. The mode is the owner-specified norm: feed proportional to measured flow with the dose per flow unit operator-adjustable [1 §5.1.2, 2 §2.2.4.1.1, 7 §3.5, 9, 10]. The contract must fix the kind's declared I/O, where the operator dose setpoint lives, the parameter vocabulary, descriptor roles, checkpoint obligations, and the `dcs-build` spec obligation.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** A new `flow-paced-ratio` kind in `dcs-blocks`. Each scan it computes `dose × flow`, scaled by `trim` while that port is bound and `Good`, clamps the dose term to `[min_dose, max_dose]` and the resulting demand to `[min_rate, max_rate]`, and flags the saturation — the demand-saturates-at-the-bound behavior the evidence records [7, 14].
  Declared I/O (proposed): `flow` (`In`, `Float` — the measured process flow); `dose` (`In`, `Float` — the operator dose setpoint per flow unit, e.g. mg/L [2 §2.2.4.1.1, 7]); `trim` (`In`, `Float` — the optional analyzer-`pid` correction scaling the paced demand, the note's gap-2 port-wired cascade, bound through `ComponentSpec::get` so an unwired `trim` means unity); `demand` (`Out`, `Float` — the bounded actuator demand feeding decision 52's actuation path); `clamped` (`Out`, `Bool` — asserts while either bound engages); `fallback_active` (`Out`, `Bool` — asserts while a declared bad-signal response is engaged, the alarmed fallback-transition output decision 55's alarm set consumes).
  The operator dose setpoint is a port wired to a writable internal `In` point — the `pid.sp`/`manual-station.manual` convention — not a descriptor-declared parameter: the dose is a standing operator value whose writes ride the journaled, receipted `WriteValue` path and whose held value crosses checkpoints through the `internal` section (decision 14), and a port keeps the setpoint composable from a non-operator source — the deferred schedule kind of decision 54 — without a kind change. The declared dose bounds still constrain the outcome: an out-of-range entry saturates the computed demand at the bound with `clamped` asserted.
  Parameters (proposed — descriptor-declared, tuned through `set_parameter` where the descriptor declares a range, under the decision-41/42 conventions; `Int` codes where `Value`'s vocabulary has no enum): `min_dose`/`max_dose` (`Float` — the engineered bounds on the dose term; the feed-range floor and mid-range guidance make them engineering data, not conventions [1 §5.0.3(g), 3, 5]); `min_rate`/`max_rate` (`Float` — the bounds on the actuator demand, the engineering-adjustable minimum and maximum pump running speed [7] and the vendor speed limit [14]); `on_bad_flow` (`Int` code — `0` stop, `demand` falls to zero, the conservative reading of the no-flow interlocks [3, 6]; `1` hold the last `Good` demand; `2` drive the declared `fallback_rate`); `fallback_rate` (`Float` — the fixed demand code `2` drives); `on_bad_trim` (`Int` code — `0` pace untrimmed on flow alone, the note's proposed response; `1` hold the last `Good` trim). A non-`Good` `dose` holds the last `Good` dose — the implementing ticket documents the precise rule — and `demand` carries the merged worst-of input qualities so a bad flow marks the demand untrusted even under a hold or fallback response. The default `on_bad_flow`, whether the response must be operator-selectable, and whether analyzer trim is in scope at all are the note's recorded customer-validation assumptions, not defaults this decision fixes.
  Descriptor roles: the four-role `PortRole` vocabulary suffices — `flow`/`trim` → `ProcessValue` (measured state the kind acts on), `dose` → `Setpoint`, `demand` → `Output`, `clamped`/`fallback_active` → `Status`.
  Checkpoint obligations: the held last-good demand, dose, and trim values the hold responses need are run state captured and restored under decision 20's rule — tuned parameters folded into the same vocabulary — so a tracking standby computes identically.
  The `dcs-build` obligation under #151's drift-guard convention: the kind ships a spec in `dcs_build::specs` declaring its kind string, typed port handles, and parameter set as data, joins `dcs_blocks::KINDS`, and takes its `check` call in `dcs-blocks/tests/spec_drift.rs` — a kind added without its spec fails the drift test.
  Fixed-rate service is no kind mode: where the treated flow barely varies [3, 6] the operator's fixed demand rides the `manual-station` path of decision 52, or `on_bad_flow = 2` makes the declared fallback rate the fixed rate. Flow pacing is the mode owner specs require to exist; which mode the first client calls primary is the recorded assumption.
- **Alternatives considered:** `pid` as the ratio computer (feed-forward scaling has no error term — the integral accumulates a meaningless quantity and the clamped and bad-signal semantics hide inside tuning); arithmetic composition from `bool-gate`/`rate-limiter` (no multiplication seam exists, and per-skid rewiring re-implements policy — the note's gap 6); the dose setpoint as a descriptor-declared parameter (range-enforced at submission, but parameters are the tuning surface — the dose is operator data the point path already carries, journals, and checkpoints, and a port keeps the schedule-source composition open); bad-flow handling delegated to downstream `interlock` wiring (silences the declared response — the note's gap 6 puts it on the kind the way `threshold-chain.on_bad_demand` carries the station's all-bad answer, decision 42); permissive inputs on the kind itself (decision 51 records why the chain stays wiring).
- **Consequences:** One kind carries the whole ratio contract — declared bounds, clamped indication, and named bad-signal responses — and every assumption-marked behavior (primary mode, default `on_bad_flow`, trim scope) stays flagged for customer validation rather than silently defaulting. The `Int`-coded parameter vocabulary is recorded honestly, as decision 41 records its `rotation` codes. The kind's checkpoint vocabulary deploys to both peers under decision 11's strictness.
- **Affected tickets:** #240 (this record); the M10 `flow-paced-ratio` kind ticket — the kind plus its `dcs-build` spec under the drift guard (to be filed); the M10 dosing-composition fixture ticket and the scripted acceptance-slice ticket (to be filed); requirement `WW-CTL-003`; relies on #30, #49, #82, #211 and decisions 2, 11, 14, 16, 18, 20, 42; evidence `docs/research/chemical-dosing.md` (#232).

### 51. Dosing permissive chain

- **Status:** Accepted — recorded as composition on implemented kinds; no new kind. Implemented basis: `interlock` gates an analog value behind a `permissive` and `trip_1`…`trip_N` inputs to a declared `safe_value`, fail-safe on non-`Good` inputs and auto-resetting (#38, #52); `bool-gate` and `sr-latch` compose Bool conditions (#38); `sequencer` walks a declared ordered step table (#134); debounced `digital-input` conditions field contacts (#11); `failover-select` switches the pacing signal to a declared backup source on quality (#211, decision 42).
- **Problem:** The owner-specified permissives — process flow proven [1 §5.1.2(b), 2 §2.2.4.2, 3, 6], metering pump available, chemical tank above the low-level inhibit [7, 12, 13], external inhibits such as wet weather [7] — are the load-bearing safety surface: no flow means no feed, and a flow switch in series with the metering pump is required over a pump interlock alone [6]. The chain can also be sequenced — the storage-tank valve opens and proves on its limit switches before the duty pump starts [7]. The decision: which seams express the chain, whether the ratio kind takes declared permissive inputs, and the recorded loss-of-permissive rule.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** The chain is declared model wiring on implemented kinds, not ports on `flow-paced-ratio`. The demand passes through an `interlock` instance whose `permissive` input carries the aggregated "dosing permitted" condition and whose `trip_N` inputs carry the individual inhibits — flow not proven, tank at or below the low-level inhibit, bund flood, external inhibit, pump unavailable — the Bool aggregation composed upstream through `bool-gate`/`sr-latch` wiring, because which conditions feed "permitted" is plant-specific data, not kind semantics — the same division decision 41 records for `avail_i`. The sequenced start composes through `sequencer` driving the valve-open-proven-then-pump-start order [7] where the skid carries an actuated valve; simpler skids wire the permissive directly. The recorded loss-of-permissive rule — demand drops to the safe value and restart stays inhibited until permissives return — is exactly `interlock`'s implemented semantics: `out` drives `safe_value` while any condition fails and pass-through resumes the first scan every condition holds again, with no latch; a skid whose owner requires a manual reset declares an `sr-latch` in the permissive wiring rather than changing the kind. Two flow concerns stay separate: `flow-paced-ratio.on_bad_flow` answers *untrusted* flow — the signal cannot be believed — while the interlock's permissive answers *unproven* flow — the process is not running: flow below its declared threshold, the service pump stopped, or the flow switch open [2 §2.2.4.2, 3, 6]. Where the pacing signal has a declared backup flowmeter, `failover-select` feeds `flow` the same way it feeds the station's level path (decision 42). Anti-siphon and overfeed protection is physical design the logic does not replace [1 §5.1.5, 2 §2.2.3, 3] — the contract's part is the no-feed-without-flow permissive above.
- **Alternatives considered:** Declared permissive inputs on `flow-paced-ratio` (couples kind semantics to a plant-specific condition set and duplicates the fail-safe, non-`Good`-cannot-vouch machinery `interlock` already owns — keeping the kind permissive-free leaves it reusable for non-dosing ratio service); aggregating availability inside the kind (rejected with decision 41 — which conditions feed "available" is declared wiring); a dedicated dosing-permissive kind (no named semantics beyond the and/or/latch the existing kinds already carry); latching restart-inhibit as the default (the recorded rule is inhibit-*until*-permissives-return — auto-reset — where a latched lockout is an owner option the `sr-latch` wiring covers, assumption-marked like the station's, decision 41).
- **Consequences:** The permissive chain is engineered model data — declared, checkable, and per-skid — needing no contract work. The untrusted-versus-unproven flow split is explicit: the ratio kind owns the declared bad-signal response, the interlock owns the safe-value gate. A latched inhibit is a declared wiring choice, not a kind variant.
- **Affected tickets:** #240 (this record); the M10 dosing-composition fixture ticket carries the permissive wiring (to be filed); the scripted acceptance-slice ticket exercises permissive loss and return (to be filed); requirement `WW-CTL-003`; relies on #38, #52, #134, #211 and decisions 2, 5, 14, 41, 42; evidence `docs/research/chemical-dosing.md` (#232) sources [1, 2, 3, 6, 7].

### 52. Metering-pump actuation

- **Status:** Accepted — decided: the motor-plus-analog-output composition stands; no `metering-pump` kind. Implemented basis: `motor` echoes the run command to the field and flags `fault` after `fault_ticks` of disagreeing feedback — fail-to-start and fail-to-stop in one mechanism (#52); `analog-output` carries the Float speed demand and `digital-input`/`digital-output` the status and command bits (#11); `manual-station` gives bumpless operator takeover of the demand and `override-select` an operator-driven source switch (#110); `pump-group` manages a duty/standby pair where feed is essential (#210, decision 41); and decision 35's state file plus the redundant pair cover the remote-restart-after-power-loss expectation [8, 14].
- **Problem:** The note's gap 4: the hardwired-interface evidence is run/stop/reset Booleans plus an analog dose-rate setpoint plus running, fault, isolator, and local/remote-selected indications [8 §6.6.3]; the 4–20 mA speed command is the dominant actuation [9, 10, 12–15]; run-only on/off skids exist beside it [8]; and two-axis command — speed plus stroke-servo adjustment — exists where a servo is fitted [8, 13, 16]. Whether the library models a metering-pump contract (run Bool plus speed-demand Float plus fault in) or stays at the `motor` + `analog-output` composition must be recorded against that evidence, with stroke length's place fixed.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Stay at the composition: `motor` — run command out, run feedback in, the proven `fault` flag — plus `analog-output` carrying the ratio kind's `demand` through the decision-51 permissive interlock. The hardwired contract [8] is exactly run command plus analog setpoint plus status bits, each half already exists with its diagnostics, and no per-scan policy couples the halves that a kind would own. Stroke length is recorded as an engineering/operator setting, never a control output: sizing guidance wants the pump mid-range on both axes [3, 5], stroke-servo adjustment is equipment configuration where fitted [8, 13], and the manual practice pairs operator-set speed with operator-set stroke at the pump [10] — the model represents a remote stroke setpoint, where the skid has one, as an ordinary field point, not a component output. A run-only skid simply omits the `analog-output`: the demand path then ends at the operator's fixed rate through `manual-station`. The pump's own proven-fault path — no-discharge detection, diaphragm or drive fault [13, 16] — is a declared field `Bool` input feeding the decision-55 alarm set and the availability wiring beside `motor`'s computed `fault`, because pump stroke alone does not prove delivery [13]. Duty/standby dosing pairs [1 §5.1.1, 7, 9] compose through `pump-group`, the fault and availability signals landing on its `fault_i`/`avail_i` inputs exactly as the station records. Manual and local takeover stays `manual-station` on the demand plus the equipment's local/remote-selected field indication [8, 15], matching the station pattern (decision 42); remote-manual-versus-pump-local scope and which permissives persist in manual [1 §5.1.2(a), 6] are the note's recorded customer-validation assumption. The per-pump actuation contract — speed-only, speed-plus-stroke-servo, or run-only — and whether pulse-per-stroke contact pacing must be an input form [12, 13] are likewise recorded assumptions.
- **Alternatives considered:** A `metering-pump` kind bundling run, speed, and fault (the kind would be a wiring bundle — no per-scan policy joins the halves — and would freeze one actuation shape where the evidence shows three; the builder composition already assembles the bundle per skid); extending `motor` with an analog demand port (couples discrete actuation to analog pacing, and `motor` stays reusable alone for non-pump loads — decision 41's reasoning); stroke length as a component output (the hardwired interface does not give the controller that authority in the common case — an output would promise control the contract cannot honor [8, 10]); pulse-per-stroke pacing as a second demand form on the ratio kind (the contact-meter form [12, 13] is equipment-side pacing — where required it lands upstream of `flow`, and its necessity is assumption-marked).
- **Consequences:** Actuation needs no contract work: every form the evidence shows — speed-demand, run-only, two-axis — is expressible by including or omitting composition parts, and the demand path ends at a typed field point like every other output. The assumption-marked choices stay open for the customer answer; a `metering-pump` kind remains available to a later decision if a real skid couples the halves with policy wiring cannot express.
- **Affected tickets:** #240 (this record); the M10 dosing-composition fixture ticket wires the `motor` + `analog-output` actuation (to be filed); the scripted acceptance-slice ticket exercises the proven-fault, takeover, and standby-handover paths (to be filed); requirement `WW-CTL-003`; relies on #52, #11, #110, #210 and decisions 5, 35, 41, 42; evidence `docs/research/chemical-dosing.md` (#232) sources [8–16].

### 53. Dose confirmation and consumption accounting

- **Status:** Accepted — totalization composes on implemented kinds; the expected-versus-measured check is a proposed small kind; the M10 tickets (to be filed) land both. Implemented basis: `totalizer` integrates a Float `rate` with `reset`, `rollover`, and frozen-on-bad-quality semantics (#111); `counter` accumulates Bool edges — the pump stroke count (#85); `alarm-monitor`, `latching-alarm`, and `bool-latching-alarm` cover threshold and latched alarming (#22, #109, #219); per-point history and the journal supply the trend a tank-drawdown cross-check reads (#35, decision 17).
- **Problem:** Owner specs require measuring the quantities of chemical used [1 §5.1.2(e)] and confirm dosing against delivered water — the master meter read daily [3], the storage-tank level trend monitored [7], the calibration standpipe timing a measured drawdown [2 §2.2.6]. The note's gap 3: expected-versus-measured verification compares the commanded demand integrated over time against a measured consumption — tank drawdown, discharge flow, or stroke count — and `totalizer` + `alarm-monitor` can compare totals only against static bounds. The decision: whether verification needs a small deviation kind or composes, and what totalization alone must deliver where no measured-consumption signal exists.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** Two layers, recorded separately.
  *Commanded totalization* is unconditional: a `totalizer` integrates the ratio kind's `demand` — the commanded chemical rate — satisfying the measure-quantities-used requirement [1 §5.1.2(e)] with its semantics recorded honestly: the commanded total proves what was requested, not what was delivered — a ruptured diaphragm still strokes [13]. Where the pump reports a stroke pulse, a `counter` accumulates it beside the total [14, 16].
  *Expected-versus-measured verification* is a small new kind where a measured-consumption signal exists — proposed as `deviation-monitor`: `expected`/`measured` (`In`, `Float` — the commanded and measured chemical rates or totals), `deviation` (`Out`, `Float` — the current relative deviation), `deviating` (`Out`, `Bool` — the dose-not-confirmed condition decision 55 alarms), with declared `deviation_limit` and `window_ticks` parameters under the decision-41/42 descriptor conventions. The composition alternative cannot express the check: `alarm-monitor` bounds a value against static limits while the expected side here moves with the command — that is the gap. Where the measured signal is a tank drawdown trend rather than a flow, `expected`/`measured` take the two rate signals and the windowed comparison covers the slow-trend case [7]. Where no measured-consumption signal exists, totalization alone delivers the commanded total plus the observable surfaces for the operator cross-check the evidence describes — the tank-level trend in history [3, 7] — and no automated dose-confirmed verdict is claimed; that limit is recorded, not silently absent. Whether the first client requires a measured-consumption signal is the note's recorded assumption. Tank-inventory ownership — day-tank limits, fill interlocks, delivery logging [1 §5.1.11, 7] — is recorded as outside the dosing contract: a tank is its own plant element whose level signals feed the dosing permissives and alarms, pending the customer answer the note marks. `deviation-monitor`'s windowed accumulators are run state under decision 20's checkpoint rule, and the kind ships its `dcs-build` spec under #151's drift guard.
- **Alternatives considered:** `totalizer` + `alarm-monitor` as the whole answer (an absolute bound on a measured total cannot express "measured tracks commanded within a declared bound over a declared window" — the note's gap 3); verification inside `flow-paced-ratio` (couples pacing to a measurement many skids lack — the kind stays honest about what it observes); a per-stroke discharge-verification kind like the vendor flow monitors [13, 16] (equipment-level function the field device owns — the contract consumes its fault contact as a field Bool, it does not re-implement stroke verification); claiming dose-confirmed from commanded integration alone (silences the delivered-versus-requested distinction the diaphragm-rupture evidence makes load-bearing [13]).
- **Consequences:** One more small kind where measured consumption exists; where it does not, the contract delivers commanded totalization plus the telemetry cross-check, and the limit is recorded. The deviation kind follows every kind convention — descriptor, spec under the drift guard, checkpointed accumulators — and feeds the alarm set as an ordinary status source.
- **Affected tickets:** #240 (this record); the M10 `deviation-monitor` kind ticket — the kind plus its `dcs-build` spec under the drift guard (to be filed); the M10 dosing-composition fixture ticket carrying the `totalizer`/`counter` wiring (to be filed); the scripted acceptance-slice ticket (to be filed); requirement `WW-CTL-003`; relies on #111, #85, #22, #109, #219 and decisions 16, 17, 20; evidence `docs/research/chemical-dosing.md` (#232) sources [1, 2, 3, 7, 12, 13, 14, 16].

### 54. Time-profiled (diurnal) dosing

- **Status:** Accepted — deferred out of scope with a recorded revisit condition; no kind now. Implemented basis relevant to the recorded shape: components own no wall clock — all timing is the executor's virtual tick (decision 4); `sequencer` walks a declared tick-timed step table (#134); writable internal points carry operator multiplier and offset values (#30, #49); declared `stale_after_ticks` budgets mark a held external input stale (#196, decision 45).
- **Problem:** Sydney Water's Mode 1 runs a 24-setpoint hourly diurnal profile — separate weekday/weekend tables, an operator multiplier and offset, bounded by min/max pump speed [7 §3.5]. A diurnal mode needs time-of-day, which decision 4's no-wall-clock rule forbids inside components: the choice is a schedule kind fed by an external tick-mapped time input, or an explicit out-of-scope deferral — the note's gap 5.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Deferred. The revisit condition is concrete: the customer answer to the primary-mode assumption (decision 50) naming diurnal profiling a required mode — or any first-client specification carrying a time-of-day demand — reopens this decision. The recorded shape when it reopens, so the deferral costs no contract churn: a `schedule` kind holds the declared profile table — 24 hourly setpoints per day-kind — and reads an externally supplied tick-mapped time, a field `In` `Int` point carrying e.g. minutes-since-midnight plus a day-kind flag, driven by a device kind whose source is the deployment's clock. The kind emits the profiled dose onto the decision-50 `dose` port's composition point — which is why `dose` is a port — with the operator multiplier and offset as writable internal points and the result bounded by the same declared limits downstream. The external input's freshness composes through a declared `stale_after_ticks` budget, and a non-`Good` time input takes a declared `on_bad_time` response under the decision-41/42 convention. `sequencer` was evaluated as the substrate and rejected for this use: its steps count ticks, so a restart or a missed scan shifts the table's phase — wall-clock alignment is exactly what the tick domain excludes.
