But whether per-zone and per-train airflow must be *totalized* in the contract, and where the derived metrics — specific power, kWh per kg of COD removed [18], the oxygen-uptake and αF(KLa) fouling diagnostics [4 §6.3.3.1–6.3.3.2] — live, is thin in the sources: no aeration-specific totalization or reporting mandate was found, and no arithmetic derived-value kind exists.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The control contract carries the measured quantities and the totalization: `totalizer` instances on each zone's airflow and on the header's aggregate airflow — the delivered-air accounting `WW-CTL-005`'s acceptance names — with blower motor power landing as ordinary field `Float` inputs where the machines report it [16]. The derived metrics defer to `WW-REP-001` reporting, which consumes the totalized values and point history rather than the contract computing them — the same division decision 53 records for dose confirmation's reporting-facing surfaces. The revisit is concrete: a first-client specification requiring specific-power or kWh-per-load as *control-domain* values reopens this decision, and the named seam is a small derived-ratio kind in the `deviation-monitor` shape — not built on spec. Whether per-zone and per-train totalization is itself required — versus measurement for control alone — is the note's recorded open assumption: the `totalizer` wiring lands where the plant declares it, and the ownership deferral stays flagged for the customer answer rather than silently settled.
- **Alternatives considered:** A derived-value arithmetic kind now (no source mandates in-controller specific power — inventing contract ahead of the reporting answer repeats what decision 54's deferral avoids); totalization deferred too (the acceptance evidence already names "airflow measured per zone and totalized at the header" — totalization is the control contract's share, and `totalizer` exists); the airflow signal's diagnostic reuse computed in-contract (oxygen-uptake and fouling analytics are derived values — the same deferral, recorded rather than silently absent).
- **Consequences:** `WW-CTL-005`'s acceptance covers measured-and-totalized airflow; the derived-metric ownership question is answered as a deferral with a named revisit, staying flagged for the customer. No new kind enters this decision's scope; the history and journal surfaces reporting reads are already implemented.
- **Affected tickets:** #254 (this record); the M10 aeration-train composition ticket wires the `totalizer` instances (to be filed); the scripted acceptance-slice ticket asserts the totals (to be filed); requirements `WW-CTL-005` and `WW-REP-001` (candidate); relies on #111, #85, #35, #163 and decisions 17, 36, 53; evidence `docs/research/aeration-do-control.md` (#241) sources [3(a), 4, 5, 7, 10, 16, 18].

### 69. The aeration alarm set

- **Status:** Accepted — the lifecycle is implemented; the set's mapping is proposed. Implemented basis: `latching-alarm` (#109) and `bool-latching-alarm` (#219) carry the two-flag lifecycle — `alarm` standing, `unacknowledged` latched until the model-wired writable `ack` point reads true, acks journaled through the ordinary settled receipt, the latch carried in checkpoints (decisions 33, 38, 43); `alarm-monitor` stays the auto-clearing sibling (#22); the alarm pane consumes `alarm`/`unacknowledged` status-role ports (#108); decision 22's I/O health surface reports link and communication faults separately from process alarms (#83); decision 38 holds shelving, suppression, and classes deferred as per-kind extensions.
- **Problem:** The note's proposed alarm set — per-zone, per-blower, and coordinator conditions [4, 6, 7, 11] — must each map onto the decision-38/43 two-flag lifecycle as wired `latching-alarm`/`bool-latching-alarm` instances without reopening the lifecycle; the alarm-and-I/O list is a specified deliverable [7 §1.3], so the set is declared per plant, not emergent.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** The set, each element named to its seam:
  - *Per-zone low/high DO* — `latching-alarm` instances on the zone's selected DO measurement against declared limits; low DO is the permit-relevant direction [3(e), 19].
  - *Airflow below the mixing floor* — `latching-alarm` on the zone airflow against the declared floor [4 §6.3.3.1, 7 §3.4]; a pulse cycle whose timer exceeds its set-point flags through the per-grid mixing timer's composed output into `bool-latching-alarm` where the plant declares the pulse scheme.
  - *Measurement fault and discrepancy* — `median-voter`'s `discrepancy`, `failover-select`'s `backup_active`, and the DO, airflow-meter, and pressure-transmitter points' fault conditions into `bool-latching-alarm` [4 §6.4.1, 7 §3.4, 19]; whether discrepancy flags alarm, latch, or merely flag is the note's recorded assumption.
  - *Fallback engaged* — `demand-fallback`'s `fallback_active` (decision 65) into `bool-latching-alarm`; the alarmed transition is part of the declared fallback contract.
  - *Per-blower protection trips* — the hardwired devices' reported status: bearing over-temperature with the affected bearing identified by per-bearing inputs, surge shutdown with reason indication (`surge-guard`'s `tripped` beside the field status), vibration level, motor overload/fault (`motor`'s computed `fault` beside the field reports), oil-system status, and the startup/shutdown override state [6 §2.2.1.11.x] — each a field `Bool` or kind flag into `bool-latching-alarm`; the per-equipment-class minimum tables cover blowers [11].
  - *Header pressure outside bounds* — `latching-alarm` on the header `pressure` against the declared bounds [4 §6.5.1.4]; *coordinator at bound* — `header-coordinator`'s `at_bound` into `bool-latching-alarm`, the can-no-longer-optimize indication.
  - *Staging pending approval* — `blower-group`'s `staging_pending` into `bool-latching-alarm` where the declared authority requires approval [4 §6.5.1.2]; *no blower available / all faulted* — `none_available`/`all_faulted` into `bool-latching-alarm`.
  - *Backup/manual mode engagement* — `manual-station`'s `manual_active` per demand into `bool-latching-alarm` where the plant alarms it; *communication/PLC faults* — served by decision 22's I/O health surface, reported separately from process alarms (#83); whether a discrete comm-fail alarm is additionally required is assumption-marked, as the station records (decision 43).
  Which alarms latch until acknowledged versus auto-clear is per-alarm model wiring — `latching-alarm`/`bool-latching-alarm` versus `alarm-monitor` — and the required latch set is the note's recorded customer-validation question, assumption-marked.
- **Alternatives considered:** An aeration-specific alarm kind or vocabulary (rejected per decisions 43, 55, and 61 — every element is a standing condition plus a latch the existing kinds already speak, and a train-specific kind would fork the two-flag language the pane, history, and checkpoints share); alarming every status flag including `at_bound`/`staging_pending` by default (invents alarm load the owner specs do not state — the note records the set as a declared deliverable, so each flag's alarm-versus-status choice stays model wiring); dedicated journal event types for aeration events (rejected with decision 33 — status-output transitions already journal).
- **Consequences:** The aeration alarm set needs no contract work beyond the proposed kinds' status outputs it consumes: every alarm speaks the two-flag language the pane, history, checkpoints, and journal already carry, and acknowledgment needs no new command path. The latch set, the discrepancy alarm-versus-flag question, and the comm-fail question stay flagged for customer validation.
- **Affected tickets:** #254 (this record); the M10 aeration-train composition ticket wires the alarm set (to be filed); the scripted acceptance-slice ticket asserts each raised alarm (to be filed); requirements `WW-CTL-005` and `WW-OPS-002`; relies on #109, #219, #22, #108, #83 and decisions 22, 33, 38, 43, 55, 61; evidence `docs/research/aeration-do-control.md` (#241) sources [3(e), 4, 6, 7, 11, 19].

### 70. The alarm rationalization record — carriage and enforced fields

- **Status:** Accepted — the carriage below is proposed; the M9 implementation tickets (to be filed) land it. The pane slice (#295) records the serving-seam choice: `SignalIndex` carries a serde-defaulted `components` section — one derived `ComponentRecord` per instance carrying `name`, `kind`, and the `rationalization` block — which the page joins to the snapshot's descriptors by the shared `ComponentInstance::name()` diagnostic-name convention, adding no endpoint or command machinery. Implemented basis it builds on: the two-flag alarm kinds `latching-alarm`/`bool-latching-alarm` and the auto-clearing `alarm-monitor` supply the instances the record attaches to (#22, #109, #219, decisions 33, 43); an alarm's identity already lands as the component instance plus the `Signal` naming its standing point, served through the signal index (#23, #34, decision 3); descriptor-declared parameters are served live through `TelemetrySnapshot.parameters` (#132, decision 34) and tuned through `set_parameter` under declared `ParameterRange`s (#82, decision 20); `Signal`'s `unit`/`description`/`group` establish the optional-field convention that extends the version-1 schema without a bump (#86, decision 3); the engineering-validation surfaces are `PlantModel::load`/`validate`, the advisory `lint`, the emitted JSON schema, and `dcs-controller --check`'s full assembly through the registries (#136, #159, #160, decision 40); and `dcs-build`'s per-kind specs check declared parameters at composition under the spec-drift guard (#112, #151, decision 31).
- **Problem:** `WW-ALM-001` makes the rationalization record contract data: stable identity and source, priority, class, consequence of inaction, required action, allowable response time, and a display or procedure reference — the master-alarm-database content [4; 2 §6.2; 13] — and requires engineering validation to reject an alarm with no required response or priority basis, the point at which rationalization kills a candidate alarm [4]. The note's seam analysis names three candidate homes — descriptor/parameter metadata, declared model fields, or an external master alarm database — and records that today nothing carries it: component `parameters` are `Value`-typed (Bool/Int/Float — no strings), descriptors are kind-level metadata rather than per-instance data, and the schema has no rationalization field.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** The plant model is the master alarm database — the note's recorded direction, keeping the alarm declaration, its rationalization metadata, the acknowledgment audit, and the performance-metrics source in one versioned artifact [4, 13]. The record splits by who consumes each element:
  - *Identity and source* — implemented: the instance name plus the `Signal` on its `alarm` point; no contract work.
  - *Priority and class* — descriptor-declared parameters on the alarm kinds: `priority` (`Int` code — the site vocabulary the philosophy's consequence-severity × allowable-response-time matrix produces [4 Table 1; 8]; owner specs attach criticality per alarm [9]) and `class` (`Int` code — the administrative grouping orthogonal to priority, e.g. highly-managed membership [4; 2 §§13–15]). Parameters because they are per-instance engineering data in the existing `Value` vocabulary, served live through the implemented parameter report (#132) so the pane renders the priority indication [2 §11.4], and range-declared on the descriptor. Whether priority is static or state-driven [5 §6.4] is the recorded revisit that would promote it to a port or served field — the note's recorded proposal.
  - *Allowable response time* — `response_ticks` (`Int` parameter — the tick-domain form of the allowable response time [2 §5.4; 4], declared beside `priority` so tooling and any later escalation machinery read it as data rather than parsing prose).
  - *The prose record* — consequence of inaction, required action, and the display/procedure reference — is declared model data: an optional `rationalization` block on `ComponentInstance` under the `Signal::unit` optional-field convention — serde-optional, so documents predating it load unchanged and the model version stays 1. `Value`-typed parameters cannot carry prose and a descriptor cannot hold per-instance data, so the block rides the document where the emitted schema, the diff, and the lint surfaces already see it (decisions 3, 40).
  - *Enforcement* — lands where kind identity is known: `dcs-model` treats `kind` as an opaque string, so the rejection — an alarm with no required response or priority basis — is an assembly-level rule: the alarm kinds' construction fails an instance missing `priority`, `consequence`, or `required_action`, so `dcs-controller --check` and any real load reject it before a scan; `dcs-build`'s alarm specs take the rationalization fields as required typed arguments so a composed plant cannot omit them; and the hand-maintained schema can pin the obligation for the known kind strings through its `if`/`then` conditional convention (decision 40). A candidate with no consequence, no required action, or no time to act dies exactly where rationalization kills it [4] — at engineering time.
- **Alternatives considered:** An external master alarm database beside the model (rejected — splits the single contract; the note records model-plus-descriptors-plus-journal as the system of record, and a second store would need its own versioning and cross-checking against the declared alarm set); the whole record as parameters (`Value` has no strings — prose cannot ride the parameter map); descriptor-level metadata (kind-level by contract — two instances of one kind would share one record); the record inside `Signal.description` (unverifiable prose — the required-response rejection needs named fields, not text); enforcement inside `PlantModel::validate` (the model layer is kind-agnostic by design — a kind-conditional obligation belongs to assembly, where the registry resolves `kind`).
- **Consequences:** One optional schema block plus three declared parameters carry the whole record, and the enforced core — required action, consequence, priority — becomes checkable data rather than review convention. The site's priority vocabulary — level count, names, colour and indication conventions [2 §11.4; 16] — the class rules, and the response-time values stay open customer assumptions rather than silently defaulted; state-driven priority is the named revisit.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 alarm-rationalization ticket — the `rationalization` block, the `priority`/`class`/`response_ticks` parameters on the alarm kinds, the assembly/`--check` enforcement, and the `dcs-build` spec obligations under the drift guard (to be filed); the alarm-pane priority-coding slice (to be filed); requirement `WW-ALM-001`; relies on #22, #109, #219, #132, #82, #160, #159, #112, #151 and decisions 3, 16, 20, 31, 33, 40; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [2, 4, 8, 9, 13, 16].

### 71. The managed-state axis — a uniform status-port vocabulary

- **Status:** Accepted — decided: the per-kind `Out` status-port convention with a recorded uniform vocabulary; a first-class served field is deferred with a revisit condition. The pane slice (#295) renders the three named managed lists with counts over the vocabulary — managed rows counting toward the totals while `alarm`/`unacknowledged` truth stays on every row — and the state filter the operator navigation requires. Implemented basis: the pane's alarm summary already lists every status-role `Out` port whose live sample asserts, joined through the descriptor's bound-point annotation, drawing non-good quality degraded (#108, #62); `Status` is one of the four `PortRole`s every descriptor declares (decision 16); and status outputs ride telemetry, history, and checkpoints like every other port (decisions 11, 17, 20).
- **Problem:** The standards require shelved, suppressed-by-design, and out-of-service alarms to be visibly distinct — never simply absent from the summary [1 §5.3.2.6–5.3.2.8; 2 §§11.7.4, 11.8.3, 11.9.4; 7]. The note's seam analysis names two candidate homes: per-kind `Out` status ports, which the existing pane machinery already consumes, or a first-class served field — a managed-state element on the telemetry contract. Either way the pane's managed-state lists need the same names on every kind, which makes the vocabulary itself a contract question even where the carriage mechanism is implemented.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Managed state lands as per-kind `Out` status ports under a recorded uniform vocabulary — `shelved`, `suppressed`, `out_of_service` (`Bool`, `Out`, `Status` role) — the same names on every managed alarm kind, so the summary pane renders the managed-state lists uniformly across kinds by joining the flags, and the ports ride the existing snapshot, history, journal, and checkpoint surfaces with no new wire type. The two-flag axis stays untouched: `alarm` keeps reporting process truth and `unacknowledged` operator awareness while a managed flag reroutes the row's presentation — so a shelved or suppressed condition still counts, `WW-ALM-002`'s "still shows counts when annunciation is suppressed", and no managed state erases history. Elevating managed state to a first-class served field — a `ManagedState` element on the snapshot or per-alarm rows — is deferred contract work: the revisit condition is a consumer the ports cannot serve, such as a cross-kind managed-state roll-up that must not depend on per-kind port binding.
- **Alternatives considered:** A first-class served field now (premature — one axis of three flags with no consumer beyond the pane, and it would duplicate onto a second surface what the ports already carry); unmanaged per-kind flag names (the pane cannot list "shelved alarms" across kinds without shared names — the uniform naming is precisely the contract the standards' managed-list displays require); managed state encoded into `Quality` (the wrong axis — quality reports data trust, and a shelved alarm's signal may be perfectly `Good`; conflating them would misstate both); a dedicated managed-state command/telemetry pair (vocabulary without semantics — the port convention carries the same content on the established surface).
- **Consequences:** The pane's managed-state slice is a rendering join over declared ports — no new endpoints or wire types; every managed kind speaks the same three names so the lists, counts, and history are uniform; and the served-field deferral is recorded with its reopen condition rather than silently absent.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 managed-alarm-kind ticket carries the three ports (to be filed); the alarm-pane managed-state-lists slice (to be filed); requirement `WW-ALM-002`; relies on #108, #62 and decisions 16, 17, 20; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [1 §5.3.2.6–5.3.2.8, 2 §§11.7.4/11.8.3/11.9.4, 7].

### 72. Shelving and out-of-service — the managed sibling kinds

- **Status:** Accepted — the contract below is proposed; the M9 implementation tickets (to be filed) land it. Amended under #900: the shelving-reason record's in-contract form is decided below, replacing the open assumption this entry previously carried. Implemented basis it builds on: `latching-alarm`/`bool-latching-alarm` carry the `in`/`ack`/`alarm`/`unacknowledged` vocabulary with the edge-consumed `ack` (#109, #219, #781, decision 33); writable internal `In` points carry operator values through the receipted, actor-attributed `WriteValue` path — journaled `CommandSettled` receipts and checkpoint-carried held values (#30, #49, #165, decisions 7, 14, 18, 39); kind-internal timers and captured state are the established convention (`sequencer`'s step timers, `latching-alarm`'s latch — decision 20); and the managed-state port vocabulary is decision 71's.
- **Problem:** Shelving is operator-initiated temporary removal for a limited period — the mechanism for nuisance alarms and temporarily invalid ones [1 §11.7; 12; 15] — bounded by a per-alarm pre-assigned maximum shelving time including zero for never-shelvable alarms, returning automatically on expiry or manually on unshelve, with the shelved status tracked, listed, and reviewed at shift transition, and the shelving action recorded with its reason [15; 17; 2 §14.3]. Out-of-service is the maintenance-associated non-functioning state — applied and removed manually with no automatic return, accountability sitting with maintenance rather than the operator [1 §11.8; 15; 2 §§11.8, 15.3]. Neither is expressible today; decision 38 recorded them as per-kind extensions and the note proposes per-kind siblings — this decision fixes the vocabulary.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** One managed sibling per existing latching kind — `managed-latching-alarm` (Float `in`) and `managed-bool-latching-alarm` (Bool `in`) — preserving `in`/`ack`/`alarm`/`unacknowledged` exactly and adding the managed vocabulary. A single managed kind per input arity, not one kind per state: the standards make the three managed states orthogonal, enterable from and returning to any alarm state [1 §5.3.2.6–5.3.2.8; 2 §5.3], and separate shelve-only and oos-only kinds could not express an alarm that admits both; the model selects each alarm's managed surface by which points it binds and marks writable and which bounds it declares.
  Declared I/O (proposed): `shelve` (`In`, `Bool`) — bound to a writable internal point, level-observed: `true` requests shelving, `false` is the manual unshelve — the request riding the receipted, actor-attributed path exactly like `ack`, the who/when audit the shelving records require [2 §14.3; 17]; `oos` (`In`, `Bool`) — writable point, level-observed: `true` takes the alarm out of service, `false` returns it — manual in both directions, no automatic return [1 §11.8; 15]; `suppress` (`In`, `Bool`) — the designed-suppression condition, decision 73's; and `shelved`, `out_of_service`, `suppressed` (`Out`, `Bool`, `Status`) — the decision-71 vocabulary. While a managed flag stands, `alarm` keeps reporting process truth and `unacknowledged` its latch — the flag reroutes presentation, never erases the record; the kind's precise edge rules — shelving an active alarm, an ack arriving mid-shelve, a trip arriving mid-OOS, re-shelving after expiry — are the implementing ticket's documented contract under the #109 edge-case convention.
  Parameters (proposed): `max_shelve_ticks` (`Int` — the pre-assigned per-alarm maximum shelving time [15]; `0` declares never-shelvable). Automatic expiry is kind run state — the shelve timer dropping `shelved` at the declared bound even while the request still reads true, so a re-shelve requires the request to cycle; a non-shelvable alarm's strongest form leaves the `shelve` point unbound or unwritable so the command path answers `NotWritable` at submission, with `max_shelve_ticks = 0` making any bound request inert beneath it. An operator-chosen period shorter than the declared maximum — the defined-period adjustment [17] documents — is recorded as an open assumption with its named seam: a `shelve_for` `Int` input carrying requested ticks clamped to `max_shelve_ticks`, added if the customer answer requires it; the level-observed `shelve` covers the bounded-shelving contract meanwhile.
  Descriptor roles: `shelve`/`oos`/`suppress` → `Status` following the `ack` convention; `shelved`/`out_of_service`/`suppressed` → `Status`.
  Checkpoint obligations: the shelve-expiry timer, the out-of-service state, and the suppression state are run state under decision 20's rule — a promotion mid-shelve preserves the remaining duration rather than silently releasing or double-counting; the kind ships its `dcs-build` spec under the drift guard.
  Shelving authority — who may shelve — is the declared-actor/writable surface's existing boundary: the contract bounds *what* is commandable and *who* is the fronting-proxy seam (decisions 39, 48). The shift-review obligation [15; 2 §14.3] is served data — the shelved list and the durable record — not machinery.
  The shelving-reason record (decided, #900): the reason rides the receipted path itself, not a second record. A `Value`-typed home does not exist — points and parameters carry Bool/Int/Float only, decision 70's recorded bound — so an optional declared `reason` text joins `actor` on the attributed `POST /command` envelope, is stamped onto the `CommandReceipt` beside `actor` under the same serde-optional convention, and echoes into the journaled `CommandSettled` entry — one durable record carrying actor, action, outcome, and reason, which the page and `dcs-ctl` stamp exactly as they stamp `actor`: declared, never authenticated (decisions 39, 48). The field is generic — any command's envelope may declare a reason — but the record obligation attaches to the operator-driven managed writes: `shelve` in both directions (the shelve reason [2 §14.3; 17], and the release's) and `oos` in both (maintenance accountability [1 §11.8; 15; 2 §15.3]); designed `suppress` is declared wiring issuing no command — its reason is the declared gating itself (decision 73) — and automatic expiry issues no command either, its record the `shelved` `PointChanged` (decision 74). Whether a reason is mandatory is a per-alarm declaration — a flag the managed kinds declare, a required-but-absent reason answering a named admission rejection beside `NotWritable`, the precise surface the implementing ticket's — while which alarms declare it stays customer policy; an absent optional reason journals as none, never a rejection, the `actor` convention. Consumers read the record they already read: the pane's managed rows render standing state from the decision-71 port join and pair each `shelved`/`out_of_service` `PointChanged` with the settled `shelve`/`oos` receipt at its applied tick — the `seq`-ordered alarm journal decision 75 serves — so actor and reason stand beside the transition, and `dcs-alarm-report` matches `CommandSettled` `WriteValue` receipts on the managed request points exactly as it pairs `ack` receipts today, carrying `actor` and `reason` into the managed-state accounting. A refused shelve — `NotWritable` on a never-shelvable alarm — journals its receipt with whatever reason the operator declared, so the refused attempt is as auditable as the applied one. What reopens the question: a customer policy requiring coded reasons — an enumerated site vocabulary rather than free text — pushes toward a typed payload and reopens this carriage; a consumer needing the reason as live served state without journal reach — a managed row's reason after the bounded journal evicted the receipt — reopens the first-class-served-field deferral on decision 71's terms; batch-scope reason carriage stays out of scope.
- **Alternatives considered:** Shelving inside `latching-alarm` as a mode (the base kinds stay the un-managed vocabulary — a station declaring no shelving carries no dead ports; the sibling shape is the #219 precedent); a dedicated `Command::Shelve`/`Unshelve` variant (rejected with decisions 7, 33, and 38 — the writable-point path already carries receipt, attribution, and audit; a new variant adds vocabulary without semantics); one kind per managed state (the states are orthogonal and combinable — three kinds could not express an alarm that is both shelvable and out-of-service-able); automatic return from out-of-service after a declared period (the standards deliberately deny it — out-of-service is maintenance accountability, not a timer [1 §11.8; 15]); one `managed` enum port instead of three flags (hides orthogonal states behind a code — the standards let them combine, and three named Bools are the honest status surface the pane lists); a separate journaled reason record beside the shelve receipt — a dedicated `JournalEvent` variant or a kind-emitted `EventEmitted` carrying `Text` (splits one action's audit into two records a consumer must re-join across `seq`, while the receipt already carries actor, action, and outcome — the reason is an attribute of the settled command, the same seam-splitting decision 74 recorded against dedicated alarm event types); a writable `shelve_reason` point carrying the text (`Value` has no strings — the point cannot carry it, and a second point would decouple the reason from the request's receipt anyway).
- **Consequences:** Shelving and out-of-service need two sibling kinds and no new command, transport, or journal machinery — every operator action is an attributed receipted write and every state a named status port. The open customer choices — which alarms are shelvable and their maximum times, who may shelve, which alarms must declare a shelving reason, whether out-of-service is the expected alarm state or the equipment maintenance-inhibit covers the intent — stay flagged rather than defaulted; the reason's in-contract form is decided above as one serde-optional field on the envelope and receipt under the convention `actor` landed, so envelopes, receipts, and journaled entries predating it load unchanged. Managed states land on the two-flag family; whether the auto-clearing `alarm-monitor` ever needs them stays open with the same sibling seam.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); #900 (the shelving-reason carriage record); the receipt `reason` field's implementation ticket — envelope, receipt, page and `dcs-ctl` stamping, report read (to be filed); the M9 managed-alarm-kind ticket — the two kinds plus their `dcs-build` specs under the drift guard (to be filed); the alarm-pane managed-state slice (to be filed); requirement `WW-ALM-002`; relies on #109, #219, #30, #49, #165, #108 and decisions 7, 14, 18, 20, 33, 38, 39, 48, 71; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [1 §11.7–11.8, 2 §§11.7/11.8/14.3/15.3, 7, 12, 15, 17].

### 73. Designed and state-based suppression — declared wiring with a named state

- **Status:** Accepted — decided: suppression stays declared wiring per decision 38, landing on a declared kind input so the state is named; the M9 managed-kind ticket carries it. Implemented basis: the gating sources already exist as declared plant state — `pump-group`'s `avail_i`/`fault_i`/`none_available`, `failover-select`'s `backup_active`, mode and maintenance-inhibit points, `motor`'s `fault` (#210, #211, #52, decisions 41–43); `bool-gate`/`interlock`/`sr-latch` compose the conditions (#38, #52, #152); and the `suppressed` status port is decision 71's vocabulary.
- **Problem:** Suppressed-by-design is intentional non-annunciation under a designed condition — a category spanning simple logic-based gating through state-based alarming [1 §11.9; 7] — and the suppressed condition must still be visibly presented, not absent [2 §11.9.4]. Decision 38 recorded that gating an alarm's `in` port through `interlock`/`bool-gate` driven by equipment state expresses the function; the note's gap is that such wiring suppresses *silently* — nothing names the state to the operator. This decision confirms the wiring answer and fixes the naming seam.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The suppressing *condition* stays declared wiring — which alarms are state-dependent is plant data, not kind semantics — but it lands on a declared `suppress` (`In`, `Bool`) input on the managed alarm kinds rather than on `in` itself. While `suppress` reads true the kind asserts `suppressed` and withholds annunciation — `alarm`/`unacknowledged`'s precise suppressed behavior is the implementing ticket's documented contract under the #109 convention — and on release the alarm evaluates fresh, so a condition that outlasted its suppression arrives as a new transition rather than a stale standing flag. The pane's distinction is thereby uniform: a suppressed alarm is a `suppressed`-flagged row with its standing truth intact — `WW-ALM-002`'s suppressed-count visibility — exactly parallel to `shelved`. State-based alarming's other members stay wiring and presentation: state-driven priority is decision 70's recorded revisit, first-out ordering is decision 74's ordered record rendered by the pane, and engineered flood suppression around an equipment trip [5 §6.7] is the same `suppress` input driven by the trip state — declared per plant. Gating `in` directly remains legal wiring only where the suppressed state needs no operator visibility; the standards' display obligation [2 §11.9.4] is what routes visible designed suppression onto `suppress`.
- **Alternatives considered:** Keeping decision 38's `in`-port gating as the whole answer (suppresses silently — the note's recorded gap, and the pane cannot count or list suppressed alarms it cannot distinguish from absent ones); a suppression component kind computing the condition (the condition is plant wiring — a kind would freeze a condition vocabulary `bool-gate`/`interlock` already compose); suppression as a `Command` variant (rejected with decision 38 — designed suppression is engineered state, not an operator action, and decision 30 keeps testbench-shaped controls off the operator path); a per-plant ad-hoc suppression flag (no uniform name — defeats the managed-list rendering the standards require).
- **Consequences:** Designed and state-based suppression is one declared input plus one named output — the policy stays per-plant wiring (which alarms are state-dependent is the note's recorded open assumption), while the state itself is uniformly visible and countable. No new command or event machinery; the transition journals per decision 74.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 managed-alarm-kind ticket carries `suppress`/`suppressed` (to be filed); the station suppression wiring and the alarm-pane slice (to be filed); requirement `WW-ALM-002`; relies on #210, #211, #52, #38, #152 and decisions 30, 38, 41, 71, 72, 74; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [1 §11.9, 2 §§11.9/11.9.4, 5 §6, 7].

### 74. Lifecycle audit — the durable transition record

- **Status:** Accepted — proposed; this narrows decision 33's recorded journal rejection in one declared respect, stated explicitly below. Implemented basis: every operator action on the managed surface — `ack`, `shelve`, `oos` — is a `WriteValue` on a writable internal point settling as an actor-attributed `CommandSettled` receipt in the durable journal (#20, #35, #163, #165, decisions 7, 17, 36, 39); quality transitions journal as `QualityChanged`; alarm and managed-state *value* transitions land in the per-point history rings, which stay volatile by deliberate choice (decisions 17, 36); and component run state — the `unacknowledged` latch, the shelve timer — rides checkpoints and the `--state-file` resume (decisions 20, 35).
- **Problem:** Do shelve, suppression, and out-of-service transitions journal adequately as status-port transitions plus settled receipts, or do they need named `JournalEvent` variants — which would reopen decision 33's recorded rejection? The audit halves differ. The *actions* are already durable, ordered, and attributed — a shelve is its receipt. But the *state transitions* — activation, return-to-normal, shelved assertion, automatic expiry, suppression, out-of-service entry and return — land only in the volatile history ring, while `WW-ALM-001` requires every activation, return, and acknowledgment ordered and attributable across restart, and `WW-ALM-004` requires durable alarm data its metrics compute over — the alarm and event log and the historian are inside the alarm system's scope [2 scope; 6; 13]. A restart today erases the transition record while keeping the receipts — an honest gap, not an adequate one.
- **Chosen approach (proposed):** The record reopens decision 33 in one narrowed respect — explicitly. Decision 33 rejected *dedicated alarm-lifecycle event types*; that rejection stands — no `AlarmShelved`/`AlarmSuppressed`/`AlarmActivated` variants enter the journal. What the durability gap needs instead is one generic mechanism: a declared `journaled` flag on `io_point` under the `writable`/`stale_after_ticks` optional-field convention — serde-optional, model-declared, validation-bounded — whose point's value transitions the recorder journals as a single new `JournalEvent::PointChanged { point, from, to }` beside `QualityChanged`. The landed validation bound: `journaled` marks `bool`/`int` points of either direction — the lifecycle status points are `out` — while a `float` point is `JournaledFloat`, keeping per-scan measurement churn in the history ring and out of the durable record. Alarm kinds' status points — `alarm`, `unacknowledged`, `shelved`, `suppressed`, `out_of_service` — are declared `journaled` by the model, so every lifecycle transition lands in the durable record in `seq` order beside the receipts that caused it; the same flag carries non-alarm record points — mode changes, protection-layer states — without alarm-specific vocabulary. The flag is opt-in per point, so the journal stays the low-volume record decision 36 describes rather than every point's stream.
- **Alternatives considered:** Status-port transitions and receipts alone as adequate (they are not — the activation and expiry record dies with the volatile ring, falsifying `WW-ALM-001`'s across-restart clause and starving `WW-ALM-004`'s durable-data metrics; recording "adequate" would silently drop both); dedicated alarm-lifecycle `JournalEvent` variants (the decision-33 rejection, retained — per-event vocabulary names kinds of transitions, while the durable gap is *any* declared point's transition, and generic coverage avoids a variant per lifecycle concept); journaling every point's transitions (unbounded — reproduces history inside the journal, the bounded-record reasoning decision 36 rejected); persisting the history rings for alarm points (decision 36's division — the ring is a diagnostic window, the journal the record — holds; a declared subset's transitions belong in the record, not the whole ring in the file); deriving transitions from receipts alone (covers only operator actions — activation, expiry, and return have no receipt).
- **Consequences:** `WW-ALM-001`'s ordered-and-attributable-across-restart clause and `WW-ALM-004`'s durable metrics get their data; the `journaled` flag is contract-visible model data — the emitted schema and `dcs-build` carry it like `writable`; and the decision-33 narrowing is on the record: generic declared-point transition journaling, dedicated alarm event types still rejected. The revisit is named: a consumer needing structured alarm-event payloads beyond point/from/to — a severity-classified event feed — reopens the dedicated-variant question.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 journaled-points ticket — the `journaled` flag, the `PointChanged` event, and recorder coverage (to be filed); the alarm-pane history and first-out slice and the metrics surface consume it (to be filed); requirements `WW-ALM-001`, `WW-ALM-002`, `WW-ALM-004`; relies on #20, #35, #163, #165 and decisions 7, 17, 33, 36, 39; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [2 scope, 6, 13, 15, 17].

### 75. Consequential annunciation — the IJmuiden contract surface

- **Status:** Accepted — the seam map below is recorded against implemented surfaces plus decisions 70–74; the scripted vertical acceptance ticket (to be filed) proves it. The pane slice (#295) implements the redundant priority coding, the state filter, and the first-out ordering — the alarm journal listing in the durable journal's `seq` order, not tick order, so an equal-tick burst's initiating receipt stands first. Implemented basis: equipment mode and authority are declared points whose transitions are observable and, per decision 74, durably journaled; `valve`/`motor` prove confirmed-state/demand discrepancy — the `discrepancy`/`fault` flags after declared disagreeing scans (#52); `latching-alarm`/`bool-latching-alarm`/`threshold-chain` annunciate worsening process conditions (#109, #219, #211); signal quality and declared staleness mark bad and frozen data — `Uncertain(Stale)` under `stale_after_ticks`, the journaled `QualityChanged`, degraded pane rows (#196, #35, #108, decisions 2, 45); the pane joins a component's flags, mode, trend, and quality in one view (#62, #108, #51); and the I/O-health surface reports link and communication faults beside process alarms (#83, decision 22).
- **Problem:** The IJmuiden near miss is the defining evidence for consequential annunciation [18, 19]: a fault switched the discharge gates to manual and they stayed open, and the remote operating position could not see the fault messages, the gate positions, or the rising canal level in one workflow. `WW-ALM-003` turns that into contract requirements: a mode or authority change, a command/confirmed-position mismatch, a worsening high-consequence condition, or bad/stale data cannot stay hidden in separate screens or present as a healthy last-known value; new actionable alarms get redundant visual coding and site-policy audible annunciation, remain visible while active, and give the operator the related equipment state, trend, quality, and control mode. Each element must name its seam and the gaps must be recorded, so the scenario becomes the vertical acceptance case the implementation slices test against.
- **Chosen approach (recorded seam map):**
  - *Mode/authority change* — the automatic-to-manual transition is a declared status point (`manual-station`'s `manual_active`, a mode point's transition) wired to `bool-latching-alarm` — backup-control-mode engagement alarmed as critical in the owner evidence [9] — and declared `journaled` so the transition is durable (decision 74).
  - *Confirmed-state/demand discrepancy* — implemented: `valve`/`motor`'s `discrepancy`/`fault` flags into `bool-latching-alarm` — the gate-demanded-but-confirmed-open mismatch is exactly the proven-feedback mechanism [19].
  - *Worsening process condition* — the rising level rides `latching-alarm`/`threshold-chain` on the measured level, with the trend and rate visible through the point's history; whether a dedicated rate-of-rise measurement kind is needed beyond the threshold path is the implementing ticket's documented choice, not assumed contract.
  - *Bad or stale data* — implemented: decision 2's quality, `stale_after_ticks` freshness budgets (decision 45), and the journaled `QualityChanged` — a frozen or lost signal presents degraded, never as healthy last-known [21].
  - *Priority with redundant coding* — decision 70's declared `priority` parameter served in the snapshot, rendered by the pane as colour plus the textual level — the redundant indication [2 §11.4] — the site vocabulary and colours an open customer assumption. Audible annunciation is site policy: the `unacknowledged` flag is the annunciation-demand signal the pane marks, and audio/delivery machinery is outside the simulated transport — the delivery and escalation assumption carried from the note.
  - *First-out ordering* — decision 74's durable ordered transitions give the causal order; the pane orders its alarm journal by the durable record's `seq` — `point_changed` transitions and settled receipts alike — so the initiating cause stands first even when a burst shares one tick (tick order alone cannot order it); engineered first-out alarming — suppressing the consequential burst on the initiating condition [5 §6.2] — composes through decision 73's `suppress` wiring where declared.
  - *Related equipment state* — implemented: the pane's per-component join supplies the alarm's sibling flags, the equipment mode, quality, and the trend panes — the fault, the position, and the rising level in one workflow [19].
  - *Acknowledgment* — the implemented two-flag semantics: ack silences the annunciation demand (`unacknowledged` clears) while the hazard (`alarm`) stands — acknowledgment records awareness; it cannot clear the condition [7].
  - *The protection boundary* — the scenario's independent high-high action is decision 77's: declared, reported, and audited, never credited to the alarm path.
- **Alternatives considered:** A dedicated consequential-annunciation kind or annunciation contract (nothing new is needed — every element is an implemented seam or a decision-70–74 extension; a wrapper kind would fork the two-flag vocabulary); presenting mode changes only through faceplate mode indicators (the IJmuiden failure was precisely a transition the operator could not see — a state display is not an annunciated event; the alarmed, journaled transition is the contract's answer); treating audible annunciation as contract (no audio machinery exists in the simulated transport — the honest seam is the `unacknowledged` annunciation-demand flag plus site delivery policy, recorded as an open assumption rather than simulated); a first-out *kind* computing causality (the ordered record plus pane ordering gives the visibility; causal suppression stays declared wiring — inventing a causality engine exceeds the evidence).
- **Consequences:** The scenario is expressible as a scripted test over the recorded seams — it becomes the vertical acceptance case the alarm implementation slices prove — with the genuinely new contract limited to decisions 70–74. The customer-policy items — priority names and colours, the audible scheme, delivery and escalation for the unattended station — stay flagged.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 IJmuiden vertical acceptance ticket — the scripted scenario over the redundant rig and pane (to be filed); the alarm-pane slices it depends on (to be filed); requirements `WW-ALM-003` and `WW-OPS-002`; relies on #52, #109, #219, #211, #196, #83, #108, #62 and decisions 2, 22, 33, 45, 70, 71, 72, 73, 74; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [2 §11.4, 5 §6.2, 7, 9, 18, 19, 20, 21].

### 76. Flood and performance metrics — computed data over the durable record

- **Status:** Accepted — decided: metrics are computed over the durable journal and point history, aggregation staying page-side or tooling-side per the decision-37 precedent; the only contract the metrics require is decision 74's durable transition record. Implemented basis: every activation, return, managed-state transition, and operator action lands in the ordered durable record (decisions 17, 36, 74); point history supplies standing durations and trends (#35); the journal file persists across restart (#163); and plant-wide aggregation is already page-side over the JSON endpoints (decision 37, #164).
- **Problem:** `WW-ALM-004` requires deterministic metrics — alarm and peak rate, flood periods, standing and stale alarms, chattering/fleeting alarms, most-frequent alarms, shelving duration, suppression state, acknowledgment/response time — the monitoring-and-assessment machinery the standards put inside the alarm system's scope [1 §16; 2 §16; 6; 13] — plus first-out visibility through a burst with the original event sequence preserved [5 §6.2]. The questions: whether the metrics need served-contract form or stay computed presentation, what the flood thresholds are, and which contract the computation stands on.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Metrics are computed data, not contract — derived over `GET /journal` and `GET /history`, and the durable journal file across restarts, by the page or standalone tooling — exactly the decision-37 precedent that kept plant-wide aggregation page-side: no metrics endpoint, no in-controller computation, no new wire type. The one contract obligation is decision 74's: the durable, ordered, declared transition record is the dataset — activation and return ticks, managed-state durations, and attributed ack receipts are all there to compute over. The recommended thresholds — flood at more than 10 alarms per 10 minutes per operator position, roughly 150 annunciated alarms per day acceptable against 300 maximum manageable, under 1% of 10-minute periods above 10, zero chattering/fleeting alarms, few stale alarms standing past 24 hours, the ~80/15/5 annunciated priority distribution [1 §16; 16] — are declared report configuration in the computing surface, site policy rather than kind data. Chattering elimination is upstream design — declared deadbands and on/off delays on the alarm kinds' parameters [12, 15] — and engineered flood suppression around an equipment trip composes through decision 73's `suppress` keyed on the trip state [5 §6.7], declared per plant; the burst's underlying transitions all journal in order either way, so suppressed alarms still count and the original sequence is preserved. The revisit condition is named: a client requiring the metrics as served contract data — a computed surface other processes consume — reopens the aggregation question exactly as decision 37 recorded its future.
- **Alternatives considered:** A served metrics endpoint or an in-controller metrics kind (premature — the data is computable from the durable record, and a served roll-up needs a process owning the aggregate, the same reasoning that kept the plant overview page-side in decision 37); metrics over the volatile history alone (a restart silently truncates the dataset — the durable journal is the record the standards scope into the alarm system [2 scope; 6]); flood *detection* as contract state (detection is assessment-stage work [6] — a computed verdict over the record, not a runtime mode the controller executes); the conventional thresholds as contract constants (site policy differs — the thresholds belong to the site's alarm philosophy [8], carried as declared report configuration, not compiled in).
- **Consequences:** The metrics surface is a to-be-filed computing slice over existing endpoints plus decision 74's record; which metrics are acceptance evidence, the site's flood thresholds, and whether engineered flood suppression is required stay flagged open assumptions; no contract machinery is added beyond what the lifecycle audit already needed.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 alarm-metrics surface ticket — the computed flood/standing/stale/chattering/frequency/shelving/response measures over the durable record (to be filed); the M9 IJmuiden acceptance ticket's burst assertions (to be filed); requirement `WW-ALM-004`; relies on #35, #163, #164 and decisions 17, 36, 37, 73, 74; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [1 §16, 2 §16, 5 §6.2/6.7, 6, 8, 12, 13, 14, 15, 16].

### 77. The independent-protection boundary

- **Status:** Accepted — decided: the protection layer is observed, declared, and audited — never implemented or credited — on implemented seams; the per-function hazard-analysis classification is recorded open. The pane slice (#295) lands the distinct presentation: each `?protection=<group>` URL parameter names a signal `group` whose points render in their own bordered section apart from the alarm pane — undeclared, the section stays hidden; a named group matching no points renders the empty count. Implemented basis: the protective-device precedent is decision 64's `surge-guard` — hardwired protective devices report status, and the contract honors but never re-implements them; declared field `Bool`/`Int` points carry the layer's reported states into telemetry and the alarm wiring (decision 5); `journaled` points make their transitions durable audit (decision 74); a declared signal `group` gives the pane a distinct presentation section (#86, decision 23); the simulated plant's dynamics elements act on the process independently of the controller (decision 24); and the receipted, attributed command path covers operator bypass actions where the plant exposes them (decisions 7, 18, 39).
- **Problem:** Where hazard analysis requires automatic action, the alarm-and-operator path is not itself the protective function [21] — the note's sharpest IJmuiden implication. `WW-ALM-005` requires the boundary declared: an independent protection layer's availability, activation, fault, bypass, trip, and proof-test state must be declared and rendered such that the HMI alarm path is never credited as the safeguard, with loss, bypass, or failure of the protection path visible and audited. The contract questions: which seams declare the layer's states, how presentation keeps it distinct, and where the hazard-analysis classification lives.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The protection layer is independent equipment the contract *observes and honors* — the decision-64 precedent at system scale. Its reported states — availability, activation or demand, fault, bypass, trip, and proof-test mode or result — are declared field points like any other field signal: wired into `bool-latching-alarm` or the managed kinds where they annunciate (a bypassed or faulted protection layer is itself an alarm condition), declared `journaled` so every transition is durable audit (decision 74), and grouped under a declared signal `group` so the pane presents the layer's states distinctly from ordinary process alarms. The protective *function* stays outside the DCS: in the reference scenario the independent high-high action is a plant-side element — the simulated world's dynamics or a scripted device acting on the process whether or not the controller scans — while the model carries only its status interface; that separation is what makes the demonstration honest, since the DCS cannot be its own independent layer. Operator-facing bypass authority, where a plant exposes it, is the ordinary receipted attributed path — journaled like every command. The hazard-analysis boundary — which functions require the independent layer versus which an alarm-plus-operator response may cover — is engineering data outside the contract today: an open customer assumption, with the named revisit that a client whose hazard-analysis deliverable requires the classification in-model promotes it to declared data — a per-alarm safeguard classification on the decision-70 `rationalization` block is the standing seam.
- **Alternatives considered:** The protection layer as a component kind in the control contract (rejected outright — a DCS-resident protection function would credit the alarm path's own machinery as the safeguard, the exact failure [21] warns against; the contract's part is reporting the layer's states, not performing them); silently reusing ordinary alarm points with no distinct presentation (the boundary stays invisible — nothing shows the operator that this layer is the safeguard rather than another alarm); a dedicated protection-layer device kind (device kinds resolve drivers, not semantics — the layer's states are ordinary points and its independence is physical, not a driver property); the hazard classification as a `Value` parameter now (prose-shaped data — the open question is recorded with its seam rather than guessed).
- **Consequences:** The protection boundary is structural, not notational — the layer's states enter as declared, journaled, distinctly presented points while its action lives in the plant or field outside the controller; the `WW-ALM-005` acceptance scenario exercises it through decision 75's vertical case. The per-function hazard-analysis classification and the bypass/proof-test workflow expectations stay flagged assumptions with a named revisit.
- **Affected tickets:** #273 (this record); the M9 IJmuiden vertical acceptance ticket carries the simulated independent high-high action and its status interface (to be filed); the alarm-pane distinct-presentation slice (to be filed); requirement `WW-ALM-005`; relies on #52, #86, #163 and decisions 5, 23, 24, 64, 74, 75; evidence `docs/research/alarm-management.md` (#234) sources [18, 19, 20, 21].

### 78. The cyclic field-I/O exchange contract

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#324) — the cyclic exchange contract proposed under this number on `codex/ethercat-path` lands as decision 78 on main. `IoDriver` gained the opt-in `cyclic()` seam returning `Some(&dyn CyclicIoDriver + Sync)` for drivers whose transport exchanges the whole process image once per scan; `CyclicIoDriver::exchange(tick)` is that exchange, which the executor — having detected the surface at wiring — calls once per scan after command application and before the per-point input reads. A cyclic driver's `read` serves the input image the last completed exchange latched and `write` stages the pending output image — neither touches the transport, and `UnknownPoint`/`TypeMismatch` semantics are unchanged. `DriverDiagnostics` carries the serde-optional `exchange` section (`ExchangeDiagnostics`: attempted/succeeded counters, working-counter mismatches, the last completed exchange's tick, missed deadlines), and `IoHealth.failed_exchanges` counts each failed exchange once at the boundary. The scripted `CyclicStub` in `dcs-runtime`'s executor tests pins the boundary ordering, the no-transport rule, held-image aging to `Uncertain(Stale)`, `Disconnected` escalation past the declared `exchange_miss_threshold`, output-image retention, and the one-scan actuation delay.
- **Problem:** Every registered kind — `sim`, `sim-tcp`, `sim-scripted`, `sim-bus` — answers `IoDriver::read`/`write` per point. A real fieldbus does not have that shape: EtherCAT exchanges the whole process image once per cycle, inputs arriving atomically in one frame while the staged outputs publish in the same frame — the contract the WW-FND-002 hardware direction and HQ-4's cyclic-I/O test subject demand. Per-point semantics over such a transport waste the bus and, worse, blur the failure semantics: today's scan has no hook for "the bus turned once," so a driver cannot tell the scan's read boundary from a point's read, the executor re-stamps every read with the scan tick (a held input would read fresh forever — decision 45's declared freshness budget has no exchange freshness to measure), and a dead bus can only surface as N per-point faults rather than one counted boundary failure with a `Disconnected` link.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The exchange hook is an open extension trait, `CyclicIoDriver: IoDriver`, reached through a `cyclic()` method on `IoDriver` defaulting to `None` — the opt-in surface the executor detects at wiring and stores. One `exchange(tick)` per scan runs at the read boundary: after queued commands apply — so a command-staged write publishes in the same exchange — and before the per-point input reads that serve the image it latched. One exchange publishes the output image staged since the previous exchange and latches the returned input image atomically, stamping each latched sample with the exchange's `tick` as its acquisition stamp — so `stale_after_ticks` measures exchange freshness and held samples age to `Uncertain(Stale)` under the declared budget. A value a component writes in scan `t` publishes in scan `t + 1`'s exchange — the contract's documented one-scan actuation delay. A failed exchange completes nothing — the input image holds, the staged output image is retained for the next exchange — and counts once at the boundary under `failed_exchanges`, `consecutive_failures`, and `last_error` (attributed `In`, the boundary it opens) while the driver's `diagnostics` reports link `Disconnected`; the scan never aborts on it. While consecutive misses stay under the device's declared **`exchange_miss_threshold`** parameter — the contract-settled name — `read` keeps serving the held image; at or past the threshold reads escalate to `IoError::Disconnected`. An exchange that completes short — a working-counter shortfall naming one station — latches the answering stations' data and escalates only the named station's points; an unattributable failure degrades the whole bus. `WriteGate` forwards `cyclic()` — the exchange is not a write, so a quiesced standby still latches fresh inputs while its dropped writes never stage — and `FanoutDriver` answers `Some` when any backend is cyclic, its `exchange` turning each cyclic backend's image in backend order and its `diagnostics` merging each reporting backend's exchange section (counters summed, `last_exchange_tick` the earliest reported).
- **Alternatives considered:** Per-point `read`/`write` over an implicit exchange (a fieldbus driver would cache without a contract — losing the atomic image, the acquisition stamp, and the once-per-scan failure count, and leaving freshness unmeasurable); a mandatory `exchange` method on `IoDriver` (breaks every non-cyclic kind and forces meaningless no-ops onto `sim`/`sim-tcp`/`sim-scripted`/`sim-bus` — the contract is opt-in precisely because most drivers are not cyclic); a bare `exchange` default method plus a separate `is_cyclic` capability flag (the pair can disagree — a driver claiming cyclic with a no-op default exchange would scan happily while never transporting; the extension trait makes support and implementation atomic: `cyclic()` can only return `Some` when an `exchange` impl exists); sealing the extension trait (would reserve cyclic implementations to `dcs-core`, contradicting decision 29's open device-kind seam — the EtherCAT integration must implement it from its own crate); failing the scan on a failed exchange like the output-write boundary does (a field stall would halt the controller instead of degrading — held inputs aging under declared budgets is the designed degradation); executor-side image machinery with the driver supplying only the transport (the image belongs beneath the driver boundary — which stations exist, how a shortfall attributes, and what "the field" is are all the driver's knowledge).
- **Consequences:** The contract is the shape HQ-4's "EtherCAT inside DCS" test subject needs: the controller owns one exchange per scan, image-local point access between exchanges, and a failure surface that names bus-level and station-level degradation distinctly. The `exchange_miss_threshold` name is settled for cyclic device kinds' `DeviceSpec.parameters`; a cyclic kind's miss threshold and its station attribution are device-parameter data, exactly like `sim-bus`'s register addressing. Existing kinds are untouched — `cyclic()` defaults to `None` — and persisted model formats and wire names are unchanged (the new diagnostics fields are serde-optional).
- **Affected tickets:** #324 (this record and implementation); unlocks WW-FND-002's hardware direction and HQ-4's EtherCAT field path; relies on #9 (the scan phases and timestamp authority), #83 (the I/O-health surface and `diagnostics` hook), #196 (decision 45's declared freshness budgets, now measuring exchange freshness), #113 (the fan-out the contract composes under), #46/#64 (the write gate forwarding); decisions 5, 22, 29, 30, 32, 45.

### 79. Customer plants are external consumers of a released platform

- **Status:** Accepted; the ownership and release boundary is decided. The release packaging half is implemented under #348: `docs/release-contract.md` records the supported release set, pin mechanisms, version scheme, and compatibility policy (decision 80), the supported engineering-surface boundary inside `dcs-build` (decision 81), and `crates/dcs-build/tests/consumer_release.rs` proves a path-free consumer resolves the pinned release. The independent reference-plant repository remains proposed work. Implemented basis: `dcs-build` emits the same versioned `PlantModel` that `dcs-model` validates and `dcs-controller` loads (decision 31); the generic controller image accepts an operator-supplied model path and is deliberately not baked for one plant (decision 46); model fingerprints and versioned checkpoints reject incompatible crossings (decisions 27 and 46). The current pump-station, dosing, filter, aeration, and IJmuiden sources and generated documents remain platform-owned reference and conformance fixtures inside this workspace; their path dependencies do not prove the customer boundary.
- **Problem:** A plant assembled only as a workspace example or fixture is coupled to the platform's repository layout, unpublished crates, and current source revision. That shape hides whether an integrator can own and version plant logic independently, upgrade the platform without moving plant source, or reproduce a deployment from released inputs. It also makes platform tests look like customer code even though they have different ownership: DCS maintainers own conformance fixtures, while a customer owns site topology, parameters, I/O binding, simulation evidence, operator-layout data, and deployment configuration.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The `dcs` repository owns the platform: reusable crates, generic binaries and images, model/schema contracts, integration documentation, and the smallest fixtures needed to test them. A customer plant is an independent version-control repository created from a supported template. It pins versioned DCS engineering artifacts and a generic controller image by release version or immutable digest; it must not be a member of the DCS Cargo workspace, use `path` dependencies into a DCS checkout, import private modules, or require edits to platform crates. The plant repository owns its Rust composition source, site parameters, generated model, simulation-only dynamics and scenarios, deployment manifests, and optional model-declared operator-layout configuration. Its clean CI checkout composes the model through the public `dcs-build` API, validates/lints/checks it with released tooling, runs deterministic simulation, and exercises the served operator UI. Deployment supplies the approved model to the generic controller as decision 46 already requires. Platform conformance fixtures may mirror a reduced plant case to protect interfaces, but cannot be cited as the sole acceptance evidence for `WW-ENG-003`.
- **Alternatives considered:** A top-level `plants/` directory in this monorepo (improves navigation but still permits workspace paths and atomic platform/plant changes, so it cannot prove independent ownership); a customer-specific controller binary or image compiled with the plant (couples runtime releases to each site and weakens decision 46's generic-image boundary); hand-authored JSON as the customer interface (remains supported by the model schema but loses the typed composition workflow); adopting the supplied QiTech-inspired phase plan wholesale, including YAML, a particular WebSocket stack, or React (rejected as a roadmap substitution — those are design references and future implementation choices, while the ownership failure exists under any serialization or UI technology).
- **Consequences:** Release engineering becomes product work: the supported Rust API, CLI/schema tooling, controller image, compatibility policy, and template need versioned artifacts that an external repository can resolve without the source tree. Cross-repository acceptance must test both a clean first build and a pinned compatible upgrade. Breaking API or model changes require explicit versioning and diagnostics rather than coordinated monorepo edits. Existing reference fixtures stay where they provide fast platform tests; a separate reference-plant repository becomes the customer-shaped proof and documentation entry point. This gate precedes new broad component growth, while already-started water slices and concrete reliability or field-hardware prerequisites may finish.
- **Affected tickets:** the `WW-ENG-003` external-consumer release contract (#348 landed the contract and workspace-side proof), independent reference-plant template/repository, cross-repository CI and upgrade proof, and customer quick-start tickets to be planned; the post-M10 plan refresh #304 must incorporate this gate. Relies on decisions 27, 31, 40, 46, and 49 and requirements `WW-ENG-001`, `WW-ENG-003`, and `WW-LCM-002`.

### 80. The versioned consumer release contract

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#348): `docs/release-contract.md` records the contract, and `crates/dcs-build/tests/consumer_release.rs` proves it — a scratch crate outside the workspace resolves `dcs-build`/`dcs-model` through a `git` dependency pinned to an immutable revision of this repository (a `file://` remote standing in for the published origin), composes and byte-deterministically emits a model, and passes `dcs-model validate`/`lint` and `dcs-controller --check`; an unfetchable or version-incompatible pin fails with the named `pin-unresolvable` diagnostic.
- **Problem:** Decision 79 fixed *that* a customer plant is an external consumer of a released platform but left open what a release is — which artifacts a consumer pins, how each is pinned immutably, how releases are numbered, and what compatibility a consumer can rely on across a repin. Without a recorded contract the first reference plant would improvise answers per artifact, and "a supported release" would have no testable meaning.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** One release is one version of a fixed artifact set cut from one commit: the `dcs-build` engineering crate plus its public transitive surface (`dcs-core`, `dcs-model`); the `dcs-model` CLI (including its emitted JSON Schema) and `dcs-controller --check` as the consumer's tooling; the generic `dcs-controller` and `dcs-plant-server` images; and the consumer-owned deployment-manifest shape. Pin mechanisms per artifact: crates resolve through Cargo `git` dependencies on a tag or rev, CLIs through `cargo install --git` at the tag or a binary built from it, images through a recorded sha256 digest, and the emitted schema as a recorded file under `docs/releases/<tag>/`. Versioning is the single workspace version with `v<version>` tags; at `0.x`, patch bumps are drop-in compatible while minor bumps may break the supported surface, `MODEL_VERSION`, or the checkpoint format and must say so in the release record. The compatibility policy reuses existing negotiated semantics rather than inventing new ones: `MODEL_VERSION` exact-match load, the serde-optional field convention (decision 3), `SUPPORTED_FORMAT_VERSIONS` plus fingerprint negotiation for checkpoints (decision 27), and named diagnostics — never silent acceptance — for incompatible crossings.
- **Alternatives considered:** Publishing to crates.io (the canonical Rust distribution, but premature — it adds registry/account machinery and public-permanence obligations the first pinned release does not need; `git` tag/rev pins give identical immutability and the contract's pin mechanism can add a registry source later without changing the release set); per-crate version numbers (finer-grained but the workspace ships and tests as one product — a single version keeps "the release" unambiguous); a lockfile-only convention with no release records (a consumer could not identify what a release contains or where the schema and image digests live); letting the reference plant define the manifest shape later (the deployment manifest is part of what the plant pins, so its shape belongs to the contract now — recorded as data, not yet schema-enforced).
- **Consequences:** A consumer can identify and pin a release from `docs/release-contract.md` without reading the source tree; releases carry a defined artifact enumeration and a recorded procedure a supervisor can execute; the scripted check gives the pin mechanism a regression test inside `cargo test --workspace`. Release versioning becomes a real obligation: workspace-version bumps now carry the documented patch/minor compatibility meaning, and `docs/releases/<tag>/` records accumulate per release.
- **Affected tickets:** #348 (this record and the scripted proof); the reference-plant repository, cross-repository CI, upgrade proof, and quick-start slices that consume this contract; relies on decisions 3, 27, 31, 40, 46, 79 and requirement `WW-ENG-003`.

### 81. The supported engineering surface inside `dcs-build`

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#348): `docs/release-contract.md` names the boundary, and the consumer-resolution check compiles against the supported surface only.
- **Problem:** `dcs-build` mixes two kinds of code under one crate: the composition primitives a customer plant is meant to build on (`PlantBuilder`, the typed endpoint handles, the `Spec` vocabulary, the per-kind `specs` data, and the re-exported contract types) and platform-owned reference compositions (`station`, `dosing`, `ijmuiden`, `ethercat`) that exist as conformance evidence and worked examples for this repository's own fixtures. If both are equally "the API," every refactor of a reference composition becomes a breaking release change and the customer boundary decision 79 drew stays untestable.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The supported consumer surface is the primitives layer only: `builder` (`PlantBuilder`, `SignalBuilder`), `endpoint` (`Source`/`Sink`/`InPoint`/`OutPoint`/`Dynamic`), `spec` (`Spec`, `DynamicSpec`, port/parameter declarations and range constants), `specs` (the per-kind spec data pinned to registered descriptors), and the re-exported `dcs-core`/`dcs-model` vocabulary — plus `dcs-model`'s document API (`PlantModel`, `MODEL_VERSION`, `json_schema`, lint/diff types). The `station`, `dosing`, `ijmuiden`, and `ethercat` modules remain public and usable from a release — consumers may read them as worked references — but they are conformance/example code: their signatures and behavior are outside the release's compatibility policy, and a consumer composes its own plant from the primitives rather than pinning semantics against these helpers.
- **Alternatives considered:** Splitting the reference compositions into a separate `dcs-build-examples` crate (cleaner boundary mechanically, but a new crate for what are convention examples adds publishing surface without changing what the contract covers — documentation plus the compatibility-policy exclusion draws the same line); marking the modules `#[doc(hidden)]` or private (they are legitimately useful as readable references and tests; hiding them makes the boundary look like secrecy rather than policy); semver-covering the whole crate (makes the reference station's evolution a consumer-facing contract — exactly the coupling decision 79 rejects).
- **Consequences:** The compatibility policy has a testable scope — the consumer check compiles the supported surface, and changes to the helper modules cannot silently break a consumer's pinned expectations. Customer plants that want station-like composition copy the pattern into their own repository, which is where site composition belongs under decision 79. If a helper graduates into a reusable typical (the `WW-ENG-002` template direction), promoting it into the supported surface is an explicit contract change, not an accident.
- **Affected tickets:** #348 (this record); the reference-plant repository ticket consumes the boundary; `WW-ENG-002`'s template/typical direction may later nominate a helper for the supported surface; relies on decisions 31, 79, 80.

### 82. One schema-driven interface per block kind

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented; the contract slice (#363), the declared command/event vocabulary (#374), the named-command dispatch and kind-emitted event runtime (#377), the served schema and live resource state (#375), the consumer-side served-surface proof (#384), and the generic rendering (#379) are landed. `dcs-core`'s `BlockInterface` (`crates/dcs-core/src/interface.rs`) carries the five resource collections keyed by `kind`, and `BlockInterface::from_descriptor` is the single adaptation: ports split across `measurements`/`state` by the `Status` role, parameters become tunable `configuration`, `In` ports and parameters adapt the generic command variants into `commands`, and the journaled transitions adapt into `events`. `Component` carries the opt-in `invoke_command`/`drain_events` hooks with refuse-all/emit-nothing defaults, `Command::Invoke` validates against the descriptor's declared `CommandDecl`s at submission and dispatches at the scan boundary through the existing bounded receipted path, and kind-emitted events drain after each `step` — a failing step's emissions included — journaling `JournalEvent::EventEmitted` at the producing tick where the declaration's `EventRetention` keeps them durable; `sequencer` proves the path with `advance`/`reset` and `step_completed`. `dcs-monitor` serves the instance-level form: `GET /schema` returns each served instance's interface — the bound-point-annotated derivation plus the signal index's `unit` — and `GET /resources` joins the same publication's live values (samples with quality, current parameters, per-command availability or its named refusal, and the retained journal tail attributed per instance beside its routed `History`/`Latest` emission records — the bounded event-history ring and latest-emission view the declared `EventRetention` routes them to, each entry's `retention` mark telling the diagnostic streams from the durable record) — both from the published read model, never under the executor lock. `dcs-blocks/tests/spec_drift.rs` is the drift authority — its sweep pins every registered `KIND`'s `dcs-build` spec, registry-constructed `describe()`, derived interface, and complete served-schema document together, and `dcs-controller`'s registry test pins registration to the same kind list. The reference-plant proof (#384) is the clean-CI `surface` stage: the consumer checkout drives the released tooling's monitor and asserts the served registry covers every declared component, a kind-declared `invoke` answers and settles a structured receipt, and a kind-emitted event reaches `GET /journal` and the per-instance `GET /resources` view — the exercise `sequencer` carrying the vocabulary. The page's generic surface (#379, `crates/dcs-monitor/src/page.html`) joins `/schema` and `/resources` per component — measurements and state as labeled readouts with unit and quality and an explicit unwired/no-sample rendering, configuration rows driving the existing receipted `set_parameter` path, the command table with per-argument typed controls honoring served availability and its named refusal plus the settled receipt, and the component-attributed recent-events list — and a kind without dedicated markup opens that surface by default, while a peer predating the two endpoints renders absent sections rather than a page failure. `dcs-ctl invoke <component> <command> [name=value]...` reaches the declared-command path without a browser, parsing arguments per the served declaration's `ValueKind`, submitting `Command::Invoke` through the receipted path with the existing actor attribution, and exiting nonzero on a rejected receipt. The proof set is `dcs-monitor/tests/interface.rs` (the five renderers present and a no-dedicated-markup kind fully operable), the `invoke` cases in `dcs-monitor/tests/ctl.rs` (applied receipt, named refusal, malformed-argument usage), and `dcs-build/tests/pump_station.rs` serving both duty/standby documents — the platform fixture and the consumer tree's emitted `reference-plant/model/plant.json` — asserting every component's five categories with parallel live collections and a receipted generic command attributed in its events.
- **Problem:** Today a generic consumer must reconstruct a block's interface from several places: ports and parameters in `ComponentDescriptor`, live points and current parameter values in the snapshot, writable marks in `SignalIndex`, generic `write_value`/`set_parameter` command variants, and inferred journal transitions.
