That is enough for today's generic faceplates, but it does not let a block declare `start`, `stop`, or `home` as named actions with typed arguments and availability; it cannot declare a typed event emitted by the block; and it blurs measured values, durable state, and writable configuration. The QiTech framework demonstrates the useful shape at commit `9b953f2c23261f81fe38b3ebd8fa51e0669e7a6f`: its machine schema has separate maps for config properties, state properties, measurements, commands, and events, and its builders reject undeclared paths, type mismatches, and duplicate registrations. `docs/research/qitech-framework.md` records the source facts and limits.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Add a versioned serde `BlockInterface` in the shared contract layer, keyed by the existing block `kind`, with five explicit resource collections: `measurements` (read-only sampled values with value kind, unit/quality metadata, and stable identity), `configuration` (writable or engineering-fixed values with kind, bounds/allowed values, current/default semantics, and capability), `state` (read-only runtime state with kind and persistence semantics), `commands` (named actions with a typed request schema, current availability or refusal reason, and the existing receipt outcome), and `events` (stable event kind plus typed payload schema and retention class). Existing ports, `ParameterDescriptor`s, writable points, and journal records are adapted into this vocabulary before any duplicate representation is added; compatibility fields are serde-additive while the migration runs. `dcs-build` specs, registry construction, runtime registration, and the served schema share one declaration or are pinned by a drift test. A generic UI renders from the schema and live resource values; kind-specific presentation may enhance it but never becomes the only usable control surface.
- **Alternatives considered:** Keep extending `PortRole` and naming conventions (cannot express named action payloads or typed events and leaves category inference in every consumer); adopt QiTech YAML or its Rust types directly (unnecessary format coupling and LGPL-3.0 implementation reuse when the product needs only the architectural separation); put UI widgets into the block schema (couples the authoritative contract to one presentation technology — semantic roles and constraints are sufficient); expose every action as a writable Boolean point (loses action availability, typed arguments, one-shot semantics, and an explicit receipt identity).
- **Consequences:** A block becomes a self-describing product unit: engineering, validation, simulation, CLI, UI, and external integrations agree on the same categories and stable resource identities. The model and snapshot grow additively; named command and event execution require new shared contract types but continue to settle at the deterministic scan boundary and journal through the existing receipt record. Every registered kind needs schema coverage and drift verification. This tranche precedes new broad component growth because each new kind should be born on the final interface rather than migrated later.
- **Affected tickets:** #363 (the shared `BlockInterface` types, the descriptor/spec compatibility mapping, and the drift authority); #374 (declared named commands and typed emitted events on the interface); #377 (named-command dispatch and kind-emitted event production — `invoke_command`/`drain_events`); #375 (the served block-interface registry and live resource state — `GET /schema`, `GET /resources`, `MonitorClient` accessors, and the registry-coverage drift pin); #384 (the reference-plant served-surface proof — the clean-CI `surface` stage asserting registry coverage, declared-command receipts, and emitted-event visibility from the consumer checkout); #379 (generic faceplate/event rendering — the page's per-component five-category disclosure, `dcs-ctl invoke`, and the pump-station served-surface proofs over the platform fixture and the consumer tree's emitted `plant.json`). Requirement `WW-FND-003`; relies on decisions 3, 7, 16, 17, 20, 31, 34, 40 and the release boundary in decisions 79–81.

### 83. The controller owns execution; UI delivery is a bounded consumer

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented. The bounded command-ingress half (#365) bounds the executor's pending-command queue through `Executor::with_command_queue_capacity` (default `DEFAULT_COMMAND_QUEUE_CAPACITY`), answers a validated submission past the bound with a named `queue_full` rejection receipt without queueing, and rides admission metrics (attempts, full-queue rejections, depth, high-water) on the snapshot's `command_queue` section; checkpoint capture/restore preserves pending entries verbatim, and an adopted queue at or over the bound admits nothing new until a scan drains it. The read-side publication half (#364) publishes one immutable read model per completed scan into the bounded store outside the executor lock — every read endpoint serves store copies, the receipt mirror refreshes between scans, and the snapshot's `publication` section reports the store's overload counters. The non-interference load/failure proof (#376) is `crates/dcs-monitor/tests/noninterference.rs`: a parameterized matrix replays identical deterministic scripts — receipted commands, forces, a checkpointed monitor restart — against an identically seeded no-consumer reference across zero clients, normal polling, a stalled reader holding its response connection, disconnect churn, malformed and flooded traffic within the declared bounds, a saturated command queue, and monitor and consumer restarts mid-run, asserting byte-identical field writes, checkpoint bytes, the receipt log, and the durable journal file, plus named-gap and freshness coverage for bounded eviction. The consumer-side end-to-end proof (#378) is the reference plant's `consumers` clean-CI stage: the scripted simulation's driven run replays under each consumer schedule — no UI, polling, a stalled reader, churn, malformed/flooded traffic within the declared limits, and a killed-and-restarted UI process — requiring identical output and receipt digests across schedules and repeated passes, from released tooling only. The schema/resource views landed under #375 and joined the matrix's read-surface set.
- **Problem:** A browser, historian, or network client is outside the control loop's trust and timing boundary. If snapshot construction, serialization, socket backpressure, a stalled reader, or UI lifecycle holds the executor lock, monitoring can add scan jitter or become a liveness dependency. Conversely, making commands fire-and-forget to avoid blocking would destroy the accepted/rejected receipt contract. The required asymmetry is deliberate: telemetry and events may be sampled, coalesced, or report an explicit gap per consumer; mutations must be admitted, bounded, validated, applied at the scan boundary, and answered.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** The paced controller publishes immutable, already-materialized read models after a completed scan into bounded latest-value/history/event storage owned outside the executor. HTTP or later transports serialize and deliver those copies without holding the executor or scan lock; a slow consumer never creates backpressure into execution and instead receives the newest snapshot plus monotonic sequence/freshness metadata or a named gap. UI presence is not a controller health input: zero clients, disconnects, malformed requests, and UI restart leave scans, outputs, checkpoints, and redundancy behavior unchanged. The mutation side is a separate bounded ingress: parse and schema validation happen before admission, a full queue gets a named rejection, accepted items enter the existing deterministic scan-boundary command path, and every admitted command receives the existing structured receipt. Promotion/demotion and peer checkpoint traffic remain controller-to-controller control-plane operations and must not be routed through a disposable browser channel.
- **Alternatives considered:** Keep the executor behind the monitor mutex and rely on small payloads (an implementation accident, not an enforceable timing boundary); let every client subscribe directly to runtime internals (multiplies authorities and backpressure paths); drop commands or answer optimistically (violates decision 7's audit and determinism); terminate the controller when a UI or telemetry session disappears (the QiTech runtime intentionally terminates on loss of its sole controller session, but that controller is a persistence authority, not a disposable UI — DCS explicitly rejects UI/session loss as a plant shutdown trigger); put a mandatory broker in the scan path (makes another service part of plant liveness).
- **Consequences:** The UI can be replaced, upgraded, or absent without touching plant execution, and telemetry transports can evolve independently. Consumers must handle sequence gaps and freshness honestly. Snapshot publication and command ingress gain explicit memory/queue bounds and overload metrics. Tests must inject stalled sockets, disconnect/reconnect cycles, malformed/flooded reads, and full command queues while comparing the authoritative run with a no-consumer reference.
- **Affected tickets:** #364 (immutable published read model and bounded rings), #365 (bounded command ingress with admission receipts), #376 (non-interference load/failure proof), #378 (reference-plant consumer-boundary proof); #375 added the served schema/resource views to the matrix's read-surface set. Requirement `WW-FND-004`; relies on decisions 4, 7–9, 12, 15, 17, 22, 34, 36 and 82.

### 84. Declared commands and emitted events across the redundant pair

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#381). The contract slice decision 82 pins on the single-controller path — `Command::Invoke` admission and dispatch, `drain_events`, and `JournalEvent::EventEmitted` — now has its redundancy half pinned and tested at both layers: `dcs-runtime` unit coverage (`a_tracking_standby_emits_the_same_declared_events`, `a_pending_invoke_carried_at_promotion_settles_exactly_once`, `a_promoted_peers_emitted_events_continue_the_sequence`, and the `final_sync` tests in `crates/dcs-runtime/src/peer.rs`) and the driven-pair proving run in `crates/dcs-controller/tests/command_event_redundancy.rs`, which extends the hot-swap harness with a `sequencer` proving kind wired to internal points and compares the promoted peer's journal and receipts against an uninterrupted reference controller.
- **Problem:** A tracking standby executes the same deterministic scans from adopted state (decisions 10, 15), so it re-derives the same declared events the active emits. Whether those emissions journal on the standby — and what happens to a command the active admitted but had not applied when promotion arrives — was left open by decisions 82 and 15. Suppressing standby emission would make the tracking peer's journal a subset of the run's, losing the pre-switchover audit a monitoring consumer or post-incident review needs, and would leave the promoted peer's journal missing the events its own scans produced. Dropping or duplicating an in-flight invocation at promotion would break decision 7's one-command-one-outcome audit exactly where the audit matters most.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Emit-identical. A tracking standby emits and journals the same declared events as the active: the emissions are deterministic functions of adopted state and scan order, so identical per-peer streams — including the standby's — are the run's honest record, and a promoted peer's journal is indistinguishable from an uninterrupted run's for the same tick count. Event-sequence continuity across promotion is then a consequence of the existing checkpoint contract: components that emit sequence-bearing events checkpoint the state that produces them (`Sequencer` does; the executor's `apply` clears only the abandoned line's drained buffer), so the adopted run resumes emission where the checkpoint left it rather than restarting or duplicating. Commands follow the receipt log that already rides the checkpoint (decision 11): an invocation still `Accepted` at capture re-queues on the adopting peer and settles exactly once on the promoted active — one receipt, one journaled `CommandSettled` — and an internal-point `WriteValue` it replays keeps the admitting line's `apply_tick` as the image sample's stamp, so consumers that age a standing request (the managed alarm's shelve countdown) measure the operator-visible window rather than the handover lag (#799). A command submitted to a peer not reporting settled `active` — `standby`, or the `promoting`/`demoting` transitions — is refused at the role boundary with the named `NotActive` rejection receipt, journaled on the refusing peer, and never enters its executor log. Because an admission can land between the standby's last regular tracking pull and the promotion request, `POST /promote` first runs `Peer::final_sync`: one best-effort, non-regressing pull against the configured tracking source that carries such in-flight receipts across the boundary; a failed or older-tick pull is skipped, and whatever it does the promotability verdict the `promote` check reads stays the tracking cadence's — the pull carries state, it does not manufacture or revoke convergence.
- **Alternatives considered:** Suppress standby emission until promotion (a silent tracking journal — loses the pre-switch audit, and a promoted peer's early-tick record diverges from a fresh active's); emit-but-don't-journal on the standby (a phantom stream a consumer could read but the durable record lacks — strictly worse than either honest answer); have the active push its pending queue at demotion instead of the standby pulling at promotion (adds a second synchronization path and a coupling from demote to a possibly-down peer, while the promote-side pull reuses the existing tracking transport); make `final_sync` mandatory for promotion (turns a momentarily unreachable active into a promotion refusal — availability the heartbeat/failover machinery is meant to provide).
- **Consequences:** `GET /journal` and `GET /receipts` answer the run's one command-and-event audit on whichever peer is asked, before and after a switchover. Event-producing components must checkpoint every input to their emission sequence — a new kind that emits from non-checkpointed state would break the promoted-run indistinguishability, so `capture_state`/`restore_state` coverage is part of an emitting kind's contract. `final_sync` is deliberately weak: it never rewinds, never counts heartbeat misses, never joins the staged-image divergence comparison (the staged what-if belongs to the tracking line the applied checkpoint abandons — a same-tick check there would flag only the inherent one-tick lag of a field-carried write a quiesced peer cannot issue), and restores the standing `sync`/`converged` report whatever the pull did — so it cannot expand or shrink the conditions under which promotion succeeds; it only narrows the admitted-but-uncarried window to the moment between its pull and the gate lift. The staged image it sets aside for that check is handed back rather than consumed (#541): a boundary the standing verdict refuses leaves the peer tracking, and on a diverged peer the staged image is the only evidence the next same-tick apply can clear the verdict on — only a transfer that itself crossed a boundary (a stream regression's new generation, a rolling revision's new model) retires it outright.
- **Affected tickets:** #381 (this record and the coverage); builds on decisions 7, 10, 11, 15, 82 and the #342 receipt-carry and #377 invoke/event runtime. Requirement `WW-FND-003`.

### 85. Field loss degrades the scan; it never ends the run

- **Status:** Accepted and implemented (#501). The remote field boundary now behaves the way the read boundary already did: `Executor::write_outputs` counts and attributes every failed output write into `io_health` and continues through the remaining points, so `Executor::scan` completes and returns the tick with degraded telemetry instead of aborting in `ScanError`. The scan-cycle plant step degrades the same way at the controller shell — a field-side `StepError::Backend` or transport failure is logged and the cycle continues, only a refused step (`InvalidRequest`) staying fatal. `RemoteDriver` lazily re-attaches after a failed exchange — the `CyclicBusDriver` pattern, bounded to one connect attempt per `REATTACH_INTERVAL` — reporting `Disconnected` per access while the plant is down and `LinkState::Disconnected` plus the retained `last_error` on its diagnostics surface. A recorded writer claim re-arms on re-attach through the new conditional `ensure_writer` grant, which the field answers `fenced` while a *different* owner stands — a restart's empty arbitration re-arms for the recorded owner, but a superseded peer re-attaching cannot preempt the attachment that claimed during the outage. `Peer::with_field_release` installs the demotion counterpart: `demote` closes the gate and runs the release, so a demoted attachment forgets the token it would otherwise re-assert on a re-attach. `PlantServer::shutdown` now drops the listener — the port refuses new connections and a restarted plant rebinds it, matching the `docker stop`/`docker start` shape the QA lane reproduces. A later QA pass (#528) closed the restart window the in-memory claim could not cover: the unclaimed field now fails closed — a fresh or restarted server refuses `write`/`step` with `PlantError::Unclaimed`, named separately from `fenced` so a probe can tell the closed window from a standing owner — until a claim lands, so no attachment mutates the field between the restart and the owner's re-arm. `release_writer` complements it: the deliberate hand-back a conditional claim (`dcs-plant-ctl`'s mutation shape) performs so a tool's claim cannot outlive its connection and fence the owner's re-arm. The proving runs: `crates/dcs-sim-net/tests/remote.rs` (the restart/reattach/re-arm/fence cycle, the cannot-preempt case, and the released-claim case) and `crates/dcs-controller/tests/link_loss.rs` (the process-level survive contract: monitor answering through the outage, degraded counts and qualities, role stability, and re-claimed recovery).
- **Problem:** QA finding `active-controller-exits-on-plant-loss` (run `qax-20260916-005`): reads already degraded to held-bad samples, but a failed output write returned `ScanError` and the scan loop treated it as fatal — the active controller exited within one scan of `docker stop` on the plant, taking the monitor (and all observability) down with it. The standby kept scanning gate-closed but could not promote — `not_converged` plus a claim against a dead plant — so a plant outage became a total station outage requiring manual restart, and a restarted controller could re-exit if a foreign claim still stood. The defect also fixed itself asymmetrically in the wrong direction: the peer that *must* keep serving telemetry died while the quiesced one lived.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** The survive half of the issue's offered contract. The controller runtime treats every field-boundary failure as degradation: reads hold the last or neutral value marked `bad:communication_fault`, writes count `failed_writes` and continue, the cyclic exchange already counted `failed_exchanges`, and the plant step's field failure is a logged degraded cycle — the run ends only for genuine run defects (a refused request), never for field silence. Recovery is the driver's, not the operator's: the attachment re-connects lazily and re-asserts only the claim it legitimately recorded — `ensure_writer` for re-arm (conditional, never preempting), `claim_writer` for promotion (unconditional, preempting), and `release_claim` at demotion so a stale token cannot race the new owner across a plant restart. Telemetry stays honest throughout: per-point quality marks the outage, `io_health` accumulates its failures rather than resetting on recovery, and the driver diagnostics name the link state — the monitor, a replaceable consumer, simply keeps serving it.
- **Alternatives considered:** The journal-and-die half the issue allowed (exit journaled, monitorable, resumable by the peer) — rejected: the monitor dying with the process removes observability exactly during a field outage, and the peer-resume path still needed the same claim-recovery machinery for the restarted plant; keeping the active alive is strictly more available and no more complex. Letting the scan abort but restarting the process in place (restart policy, supervisor retry) — rejected: it reintroduces the boot-time claim race the release hook exists to close, and a crashing loop is indistinguishable from healthy at the field. Clearing the recorded claim on any fenced answer *and* requiring explicit re-claim tooling — rejected: it would leave the legitimate owner fenced out of its own field after every restart. An unconditional re-claim on re-attach — rejected: a superseded peer's re-attach would steal the field back from the peer that promoted during the outage, breaking exactly-one-writer.
- **Consequences:** `ScanError` left the public surface under #613: no scan phase ever produced it, so `Executor::scan`/`Executor::run`, `Peer::scan`, and the monitor's paced/driven scan paths now return `Tick` directly and callers no longer match an impossible error — the compatibility retention this record originally kept ended once the consumer release set (dcs-build + dcs-core + dcs-model) proved not to expose `dcs-runtime`. Field-facing backends that want demotion-safe claim re-arm must expose a `DeviceBackend.release` hook — `sim-tcp` does (`RemoteDriver::release_claim`); `sim-bus` and `sim-cyclic` leave it unset because their claim dies with the connection. `POST /scan` on a driven monitor now answers `200` with degraded snapshots through a plant outage rather than `500`/silence, and the paced loop keeps its period. Failover semantics are unchanged: field loss is not a heartbeat miss, promotion still claims before the gate lifts, and a fenced attachment's mutations stay refused. The QA lane re-verifies the finding against `scenario_plant_link_loss` — stop the plant, observe degraded-but-serving telemetry and stable roles, restart it, observe re-armed fencing and `Good` reads with the outage's counters retained.
- **Affected tickets:** #501 (the QA defect fix and this record), #528 (the fail-closed unclaimed field and `release_writer`), #613 (the `ScanError` removal); builds on decisions 5, 13, 22, 28 and the `sim-bus` lazy-reattach precedent.

### 86. Dead active holding the claim with an unconverged standby — restart-as-active is the recovery

- **Status:** Decided (#562) — the recorded recovery path is restart-as-active; no force-promote variant and no operator-directed claim-release seam enter the contract. No runtime changes. Implemented basis the decision rests on: the field claim stands until preempted — `claim_writer`'s grant is unconditional and a holder's disconnect never releases it, a dead owner's silence being exactly what the claim fences (decision 28; `RemoteDriver::claim_writer`, `crates/dcs-sim-net/src/client.rs`); promotion preempts the claim but only from `StandbySync::Tracking` or `Reinitialized`, every other verdict answering `SwitchError::NotConverged` carrying the reported state (decisions 15, 26; `Peer::promote`/`self_promote`, `crates/dcs-runtime/src/peer.rs`); and a launched active runs the conditional startup grant before its gate lifts — `Peer::activate`, preempting a dead owner's standing claim but refusing `FieldClaimFailed` with the gate still closed while a *live* different-owner incumbent stands (decision 89's #797 amendment of the startup half — the recovery this record names targets the dead-owner case, which the grant still preempts).
- **Problem:** The field-claim settlement closed every demonstrated wedge except one the recorded contract leaves recoverable only by process-level intervention: an active dies holding the plant's single-writer claim while its standby is not converged. The claim stands until preempted — never on disconnect. The standby's promotion would preempt it, but `promote` requires `Tracking` or `Reinitialized`, and a dead peer produces no more checkpoints: a `Diverged` verdict clears only on a positive same-tick comparison against a served checkpoint (decision 26's #541 rule), a `Degraded` peer realigns only on a successful apply, and an `Unsynchronized` peer holds only its own initial state — so all three sit permanently unpromotable, `not_converged` forever, still serving telemetry and journaling but never writing. The only recovery the contract offers is launching a fresh active, which works because `Peer::activate` preempts a dead owner's standing claim at startup — but is otherwise a cold start losing the carried setpoints, run hours, alarm latches, and tuning `WW-LCM-001`'s continuity clause names, unless a reachable `--state-file` resumes the run (decision 35). Whether the operator should get a bounded force-promote, an operator-directed field-claim release, both, or neither needed a recorded answer.
- **Chosen approach (decided):** Neither — restart-as-active stands as the deliberate recovery path, and both forced seams are declined. The named procedure: relaunch the controller as a fresh active on the same model (no `--standby`). `Peer::activate` runs the conditional startup grant before the gate lifts, so the dead owner's standing claim — its holder set empty once the dead process's attachments drop — is preempted at startup, no release step exists or is needed, and a `FieldClaimFailed` refusal names the cases the grant cannot take: the field unreachable, or a *live* different-owner incumbent still holding (decision 89 — the restart must then rejoin as `--standby`). A `--state-file` that survived the dead process resumes the run at its last persisted cycle — the checkpointed setpoints, run hours, alarm latches, and tuning all riding decision 11's carry — with `--journal-file` keeping the audit continuous across the run boundary (decision 36); without reachable persistence the restart is the honest cold start, and the loss is a deployment-coverage question — was persistence configured, and did it survive the host — not a contract gap. The marooned standby needs no promotion to recover: when the fresh active answers where the standby's tracking source resolves (the configured `--standby`/`--peer` address or the address decision 15's #514 resolution learned), the ordinary pulls resume and the peer reconverges to `tracking` on its own cadence — the restart restores the whole pair, not just a writer.

  Both forced seams are declined against the settled contract. A bounded force-promote would have to promote precisely the states the contract cannot vouch for: `Unsynchronized` holds only this instance's initial state — force-promoting it is a cold start wearing a standby's identity, strictly less honest than the restart that names itself; `Diverged` is *proved* divergence — the staged image the peer computed does not match the field, exactly the evidence decision 26 exists to act on; and `Degraded` continues on last-known state of unbounded staleness with no checkpoint left to prove it by. An operator override cannot manufacture the missing evidence — it can only journal that the evidence was overridden — and the `not_converged` gate's purpose is that no peer ever writes what the run would not, a rule that does not soften because the competing writer is dead: a diverged standby's writes are wrong outputs, not merely contested ones. An operator-directed claim-release adds no capability: the wire's `release_writer` drops only the releasing connection's own hold by design — no attachment may release another's claim, the dead owner's silence being the fenced case — and even an administrative release-by-token would only move the field to `unclaimed` (fail-closed under decision 85's #528 rule), which unblocks nothing `claim_writer`'s unconditional preemption does not already handle. It would matter only as the precondition half of the force path, which is declined.

  The audit and fencing obligations any forced path carries — recorded so a reopening ticket lands a specified seam rather than inventing one: it must take the field through the unconditional `claim_writer` before the gate lifts, never `ensure_writer` — decision 85's rule, conditional re-arm only for a recorded owner — so a still-alive old active is fenced and demotes in place (decision 28's #508 semantics) rather than dual-writing; it must journal the forced transition attributed to the declaring operator, carrying the overridden verdict — the `Diverged` mismatches or the never-converged state — into the durable record instead of silently dropping it (the routine `promote`/`demote` path is deliberately unattributed today per decision 39, so a forced switch extends attribution where the ordinary path does not); it must exist on the operator path only — `self_promote` never forces, automatic failover never writing unproven state; and it must report honestly non-bumpless — the promoted peer's role report carrying that it runs adopted-but-unproven state, not `tracking` parity.

  A `Diverged` or otherwise unconverged standby under this answer is a named, monitorable, quiesced peer — never a writer: `GET /role` keeps serving its `diverged`/`degraded`/`unsynchronized` verdict, the pair view keeps reporting the convergence-grace redundancy fault (#523's pairHealth rule, decision 19), and its telemetry, journal, and divergence evidence stay served until the process reconverges on the new active's stream or is deliberately restarted. It is a redundancy fault to display, never a plant fault to clear. What reopens the question: evidence that restart-as-active is measurably worse than the standby's adopted state — a QA-lane scenario or a customer case where no reachable `--state-file` existed, the cold-start outputs moved the process somewhere the standby's last-adopted state would not have, and an owner names recovery-to-carried-state an acceptance obligation. The evidence a bounded force-promote needs is exactly the evidence that stale-but-carried beats initial.
- **Alternatives considered:** A bounded force-promote (declined above — it promotes unproven state: `Unsynchronized` is initial state, `Diverged` is proved-different outputs, `Degraded` is unbounded staleness; the gate the override bypasses is the contract's whole point); an operator-directed claim release (declined — `release_writer` can drop only the caller's own hold, and a release-by-token moves the field to `unclaimed`, unblocking nothing `claim_writer` does not already preempt); both (each declined on its own terms); a staleness-bounded promotion — "was `Tracking` within N misses" (rejected: the last applied verdict expires with the heartbeat budget because staleness past it is unbounded — a freshness guarantee the dead checkpoint stream cannot supply, and re-arming it would resurrect exactly the over-budget promotion decision 28 refuses); leaving the wedge unrecorded (a marooned standby was already a named fault, but *why* restart rather than force is the answer was implicit).
- **Consequences:** The convergence gate becomes absolute: every field writer is either a proven tracker or a fresh operator-launched authority, and `not_converged` never has an override — decisions 15, 26, and 28's semantics stand unchanged. The operational duty the record shifts to deployment is persistence coverage: a reachable `--state-file`/`--journal-file` is the difference between a warm resume at the last persisted cycle and the recorded cold start — the reference-plant manifest already carries the fields (`docs/customer-quickstart.md`). The unconverged peer remains useful — serving, journaling, reconverging — while never writing. If the recorded evidence lands, the implementing ticket carries the stated bar — claim order, attribution, the carried verdict, an operator-path-only surface — rather than reopening the gate by default.
- **Affected tickets:** #562 (this record); the implementing seam is deferred until the reopening evidence lands; requirement `WW-LCM-001`; decided against the machinery decisions 15 (#514's tracking-source resolution), 19 (#513's dual-active fault and #523's convergence-grace fault), 26 (#541's positive-evidence clearing), 28 (#65's claim fencing, #508's demote-in-place, #515's `claimed_shared`), 35 and 36 (the restart-side persistence), 39 (the unattributed switch path), 84 (`final_sync`'s deliberate weakness), and 85 (#501/#528's claim rule) record.

### 87. Unowned tracked lines: the orphaned standby is a named state, and the ex-owner re-arms a released claim

- **Status:** Implemented — #778 against QA finding `mutual-standby-tracking-deadlock`. The checkpoint contract gains an ownership stamp: `Checkpoint::source_owns_field` is `Some(true)` when the serving run holds the field claim, `Some(false)` when it serves checkpoints without one, and absent from bare-executor checkpoints that make no peer assertion; `Peer::checkpoint` stamps it from `Peer::owns_field`, so the stamp rides the existing serde contract with no new endpoint. A puller that applies a `Some(false)` checkpoint — converged state, but a serving run that writes nothing — reports the new `StandbySync::Orphaned { aligned }` instead of `Tracking`: the transition journals `JournalEvent::FieldOrphaned`, the pair view names it the `standby_orphaned` redundancy fault (vocabulary version 2), and the miss accounting counts it toward the failover budget so `--auto-promote` treats a permanently unowned line like a dead one. `Orphaned` remains promotable — `promote`/`self_promote` accept it beside `Tracking` and `Reinitialized`, the convergence proof being identical — so the escaped wedge resolves through the ordinary promotion claim. The second half is the demoted ex-owner's duty: a run that once held the claim (`was_owner`, set when the gate lifts and kept across the demotion — the ex-owner is precisely who may re-arm) probes `ensure_writer` once per orphaned pull — the conditional re-arm that claims only while the field is unclaimed or already names the peer's token, never preempting a different standing owner (`RemoteDriver::ensure_writer`; `FanoutDriver::ensure_field_writer` reaches it through the `DeviceBackend::ensure` seam, absent on backends whose claims die with their connections). The `dcs-controller/tests/mutual_standby.rs` regressions script both reproductions on the sim rig: the foreign `claim_writer`/`release_writer` path ends with the ex-owner's claim re-armed and the pair promotable, and the `demote → promote → restart-as-standby` path reports `orphaned` on both peers until the ex-owner's promotion preempts the dead token. Amended by #800 (QA finding `demote-during-interlock-release-leaves-field-energized-and-wedges-pair`): the orphaned apply also supersedes a *standing* `Diverged` verdict — a demote inside a multi-scan release window can land a peer `diverged` on the last `Some(true)` checkpoint the demoting owner served (staged released outputs vs the field's frozen energized ones) just before the ownerless stream began, and a `Diverged` that survived the stamp then wedged permanently: orphan applies discard the staged evidence, so no comparison could ever clear it and `promote` refused forever. The stamp now adjudicates it: the mismatch is the ownerless condition itself, so `Diverged` reports `Orphaned` like every other promotable state — the promotion the verdict blocked is exactly the write that converges the field. Amended under #831 (decision 91): `Orphaned` stays promotable, but its claim is now the *conditional* grant — the tracking evidence cannot tell a dead owner from a live incumbent the run lost track of, so a live different-owner *controller's* unyielded claim refuses the promotion instead of being preempted by a stale island — and the orphan cycle also probes the line's propagated `line_owner` to re-resolve the pulls onto the real owner, so a demoted pair cannot wedge on each other forever.
- **Problem:** The failover heartbeat — decision 28's "a produced checkpoint means the source is alive" — cannot see the wedge two protocol-legal paths leave behind: every peer alive, every pull succeeding, no peer owning the field. Path one: a foreign `claim_writer` preempts the active's claim; the first fenced write demotes the ex-owner in place (decision 28's #508 semantics), and when the foreign claim is released the demoted peer has no rule that sends it back for the field — it tracks its still-standby successor forever. Path two: the pure-ops `demote → promote → restart` — the promoted peer relaunches under its `--standby` flag and tracks the demoted peer while the demoted peer tracks it back, the field fenced to the dead owner's token by the claim's deliberate never-release-on-disconnect rule (decision 86). In both, each peer treats the other as its source indefinitely: pulls apply cleanly, ticks advance on stale field data, `/role` reads `standby`/`tracking` on both sides, and the plant freezes with no journal entry, fault, or alarm — a silent total control outage on a hot-standby system.
- **Chosen approach:** The stamp, not a new channel: the checkpoint already carries the run's identity (`generation`, #622), so it now also carries the run's field ownership, and the puller reads the wedge directly from the line it already trusts. Reporting gets a named verdict rather than a re-used one — `orphaned` is not `tracking` (nothing owns what it tracks), not `degraded` (the pull succeeded), and not `diverged` (no proved output mismatch) — so `/role`, the journal, and the pair fault surface each carry the outage in their existing vocabularies. Recovery splits the two halves the wedge is made of. Detection is automatic everywhere: every orphaned pull counts a miss, so an `--auto-promote` peer self-promotes on the same budget a dead source would, and the manual `promote` accepts the verdict like `Tracking` — the convergence evidence stands, and the missing piece is precisely what promotion's unconditional claim supplies. Re-arming is conditional and ex-owner-only: `ensure_writer` fires only from a peer that actually held the claim, only while the tracked line stays orphaned, and only takes the field when it is unclaimed or already the peer's — the dead-token case of path two stays fenced for the promotion that must preempt it, while the released field of path one re-arms for the recorded owner instead of standing open to the next foreign grab. A peer that never owned the field never probes — a fresh standby re-arming a claim it never held would be an unproven writer.
- **Alternatives considered:** Detecting the loop on the serving side — "a peer whose own tracking source is its puller refuses to serve" (the pull direction is already the announce channel, but the wedge also forms without mutual announcement — a demoted peer's source resolves through #514's adoption, not the successor's `?peer=` — and a refusal breaks the clean apply that keeps both sides promotable, trading a silent wedge for a diverged one); treating `Some(false)` checkpoints as failed transfers (conflates "alive but unowned" with "unreachable" — the miss counter alone cannot distinguish them for the re-arm rule, and `Degraded` overstates a clean apply); an unconditional re-claim on every orphaned pull from any peer (a never-owner standby grabbing the field is an unproven writer — the exact class decision 86 keeps out — and an unconditional claim would preempt a standing owner, reopening the dual-writer hole); a new operator endpoint or journal nudge to wake the ex-owner (human in the loop for a defect whose whole cost is silence; the conditional probe is the same safe claim shape the field already arbitrates); leaving the pair's mutual-tracking loop to a watchdog outside the role machine (another deployed component, and the evidence — applied checkpoints from a non-owning run — already lives in the puller's sync verdict).
- **Consequences:** Mutual healthy tracking with no field owner becomes unrepresentable: the pair either converges to one active or reports `orphaned` — journaled, pair-faulted, and promotable. The checkpoint contract grows one optional field — older peers ignore it (serde-tolerant like every contract extension), and a bare `Executor` checkpoint stays honest by making no claim. The failover budget gains a second counted miss shape, so detection time is the configured budget in scan periods, same as for a dead source. `ensure_writer` becomes a load-bearing part of the device seam: field kinds that cannot answer "unclaimed or mine?" conditionally keep `DeviceBackend::ensure` unset and get orphan *detection* without re-arm — promotion still escapes the wedge, the claim standing until then by decision 86's rule.
- **Affected tickets:** #778 (implemented — QA finding `mutual-standby-tracking-deadlock`), #800 (the diverged-supersede amendment — QA finding `demote-during-interlock-release-leaves-field-energized-and-wedges-pair`; regressions `dcs-controller/tests/demote_release.rs` and the peer unit test `an_orphaned_apply_supersedes_a_standing_diverged_verdict`); relies on and amends decision 28's heartbeat contract (#65, #508, #515), decision 15's tracking-source resolution and convergence gate (#514), decision 26's verdict vocabulary (#541), decision 19's pair faults (#513, #523), decision 84's `final_sync`, and decisions 85/86's claim-order and never-release rules (#501, #528, #562); the regression is `dcs-controller/tests/mutual_standby.rs`, with unit coverage in `dcs-runtime`'s peer tests and the fault contract in `dcs-monitor`'s pair and HTTP tests.

### 88. Manual takeover persists the declared protections — the station's protection set gates both command legs

- **Status:** Decided and implemented (#782, consolidated with #780). The duty/standby station's per-pump command path — `motor.cmd = ((group_cmd and in-auto) or (hand and mode)) and guards` — carried out-of-service as its only guard, so a hand-held pump kept its field command through a station power trip, ran the well unbounded below the dry-run cutoff, and annunciated `none-available` while it ran: the QA finding `hand-mode-bypasses-station-interlocks` (run `qax-20260921-002`). The recorded contract is the composition below, landed identically in the platform fixture (`crates/dcs-build/src/station.rs`) and the consumer tree (`reference-plant/src/station.rs`) against the pinned release surface — no kind changes, no release dependency. #809 completes the annunciation half: the per-pump thermal/moisture alarms take the same quality-aware cause-guard wiring the power-fail alarm got — QA finding `degraded-protection-contact-trips-pump-silently` (run `qax-20260922-002`) caught a Bad-quality contact tripping the command while its cause alarm stayed clean.
- **Problem:** The reference station aggregated `avail_i` from in-auto, in-service, power-ok, thermal-ok, and moisture-ok, but the manual leg carried none of them — the operator's `hand and mode` OR'd onto the auto leg and passed only the out-of-service guard. The harm ran deeper than a missing gate: `avail_i` reads non-`Good` inputs as unavailable (decision 41's fail-safe rule), so a *degraded* power-fail contact dropped availability on quality while the value-only hand path and the value-only `power-fail` alarm stayed clean — the station declared no pump available while the causal alarm stood false and a motor stayed commanded. Which protections persist into manual was a listed open assumption (WW-OPS-001, WW-CTL-002); the QA evidence argued the only defensible reading.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Manual takeover is an operator demand bounded by the full declared protection set — the protections persist, the demand does not override them. The machinery is the fail-safe `interlock` kind used as a trip aggregator: each pump gains a `protect` interlock whose `trip_N` inputs bind the raw protection contacts — the station `power-fail` point, the pump's `thermal` and `moisture` contacts, and the chain's `below_cutoff` — and whose `permissive` binds in-service; its `in` carries the failover-selected level, so an untrusted level cannot prove the dry-run trip is clear and trips too. The kind's quality rule does the work no `bool-gate` can: a non-`Good` input cannot vouch for pass-through, so a `Bad` or disconnected contact trips exactly as an asserted one does — one rule covering value and quality, the same reading `avail_i` applies. `protect.tripped` inverted (`digital-input`) yields `protections-ok`, which replaces in-service alone as the final command guard in both modes. The hand leg additionally carries a `timer` holdout on `protections-ok` at `min_off_ticks` — the restart holdout the automatic path already gets from the group's staging bounds — so a held demand re-asserts only after the protections have stood, and a tripping protection cannot be flickered by a bouncing contact. `protect-tripped`/`protections-ok` are journaled protection-layer status.
  The annunciation half aligns on the same cause: a station `power-guard` interlock evaluates the conditioned `power-ok` signal — false *or* non-`Good` trips — and its `tripped` output drives the `power-fail` alarm's condition input, so the causal alarm asserts on the asserted contact and on its degraded or disconnected quality alike, matching what availability trips on. The per-pump contacts take the same guard treatment (#809): one single-trip `interlock` per contact — held `Good` `in` and `permissive`, the raw contact as its `trip_1` — whose `tripped` drives that contact's cause alarm, so a degraded thermal or moisture contact annunciates on the same reading that trips the pump rather than leaving a clean alarm over a stopped motor. `none-available` gains a declared `suppress` wired to any pump held in manual: with the operator holding a pump outside the group's roster, an empty roster is designed state, not a fault — the alarm's `alarm` output still reports the carrier's truth and `suppressed` names the withholding, decision 73's designed-suppression pattern, while the cause alarms (power-fail, thermal, moisture) annunciate unimpeded. The carrier asserts on availability alone, so its alarm's declared consequence names the empty roster — "No pump is available; the station cannot pump" — rather than claiming a standing demand (#825's annunciation-narrative finding).
- **Alternatives considered:** Gating the hand leg with the `avail` aggregate's health legs through plain `bool-gate` wiring (rejected — `bool-gate` propagates quality but folds on value, so a `Bad` power contact reading false would still pass the hand path, recreating the exact asymmetry the finding names); extending `digital-input` or a new Bool kind with an `on_bad` quality-to-value rule (rejected — a new kind or parameter widens the supported surface for one composition need, and the consumer pins the release; the `interlock` composition expresses the contract with the kinds already released); the issue's fallback contract — keep the bypass, gate `none-available` on hand-running pumps (rejected — the finding's evidence shows real dry-run harm in-model, and WW-OPS-001's research-backed sentence reads "persists declared protections while the operator holds the demand," not "persists annunciation hygiene"); latching the manual trip until an operator reset (rejected — auto-resetting with the `min_off_ticks` holdout matches the automatic path's discipline; a manual-reset lockout is a station-policy question that stays on the open-assumption list).
- **Consequences:** Every motor command — auto or hand — releases within the carrier-hop bound of a protection trip, value-true or quality-degraded; a hand pump cannot draw the well below the dry-run cutoff or run through a power trip while `none-available` stands; and the `power-fail` alarm can no longer sit clean while its input trips the station. The consumer tree's per-pump internal block widens to the platform fixture's 32-point stride — signal names are unchanged, so deployed consumers addressing points by name are unaffected. `power-tripped` lands one scan after the contact lands (the carrier hop), so the durable record orders the cause alarm's assertion beside — not before — the availability collapse it explains. The open-assumption list keeps the lockout-policy question; the protections-persist-in-manual question this resolves.
- **Affected tickets:** #782 (this record and the composition change), #780 (the degraded-input cause-alarm case consolidated into it), #809 (the per-pump cause-guard completion — QA finding `degraded-protection-contact-trips-pump-silently`); requirements `WW-OPS-001`, `WW-CTL-002`, `WW-ALM-002`; builds on decisions 41 (the `avail_i` aggregate and its fail-safe quality rule), 44, 73 (designed suppression), and 74 (journaled protection status).

### 89. A restarted active must not preempt a live incumbent — the startup claim is conditional

- **Status:** Implemented — #797 against QA finding `stale-checkpoint-takeover-reverts-incumbent-commands` (run `qax-20260921-004`, rig `lenovo`). The launched active's startup claim is no longer the promotion path's unconditional `claim_writer`. `Peer::activate` now runs a conditional startup grant when the `with_field_startup_claim` hook is installed — the controller installs `Driver::claim_writer_unless_held`, which the sim-net wire carries as `PlantRequest::ClaimWriterUnlessHeld`. The plant server's writer claim already tracked the live connections holding it; the conditional grant refuses (`PlantError::Fenced`) only while a *different* owner's claim has a live holder — the claim's never-release rule leaves a dead owner's token standing with an empty holder set, and that claim the grant still preempts, so decision 86's restart-as-active recovery is unchanged for a crashed owner. A refusal surfaces as `SwitchError::FieldClaimFailed` through the ordinary startup-failure path — the gate never opens, the process exits nonzero, and the message names the remedy: rejoin as a standby, whose tracking pulls adopt the incumbent's state rather than reverting it. Explicit `promote`/`self_promote` keep the unconditional claim — a promotion is the deliberate takeover the claim exists to arbitrate, and the fencing demotion that follows it is the designed path. Backends that cannot distinguish a live holder from a standing one (`sim-bus`, `sim-cyclic` — their claims die with the connection) leave `DeviceBackend::startup_claim` unset and `FanoutDriver::claim_field_writer_unless_held` falls back to their ordinary claim hook, so their startup behavior is unchanged; a same-token live holder still answers `ClaimedShared`, warned and granted exactly as `claim_writer` reports it. #831 (decision 91) refines "live holder" once more: the claim now records whether its claimant is a controller (`ClaimWriter`/`EnsureWriter`'s `controller` flag, absent-flag payloads reading `true`), and the conditional grant refuses only a live unyielded *controller* claim — a field tool's hold is never an incumbent, so the rogue `claim_writer` the orphan-promote recovery preempts cannot wedge it.
- **Problem:** The QA reproduction: pair A-active/B-tracking; `POST /promote` on B (B claims the field, A auto-demotes); A killed inside the handover window before adopting B's post-promotion checkpoint, so its `--state-file` froze pre-command; B applied `write_value` to the managed-alarm acknowledgment and pump out-of-service points, both receipts settling `applied`; `docker start` A — it resumed the frozen file and its unconditional startup claim preempted B's live claim. A served the reverted state; B's next scan fenced, demoted, and its tracking pull adopted A's stale checkpoint — the revert propagated to *both* peers while B's receipts still claimed `applied`, and the subsequently demanded pump was physically commanded out-of-service. The claim seam could not tell a live incumbent from a dead one, so the restart-as-active recovery path doubled as a silent state-rollback seam — and the adoption mechanics then carried the rollback to the surviving peer, leaving the receipt log (the line's one audit) asserting applications the live image contradicted.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** Make the *startup* claim conditional on the incumbent's liveness — the minimal distinction the defect exploits: a restarted run cannot prove its resumed state is current with a live incumbent's, so it may preempt only a dead owner's standing claim, never a live one's. The conditional op lives on the wire next to the unconditional one (`ClaimWriterUnlessHeld` beside `ClaimWriter`), refusing through the existing `fenced` vocabulary rather than a new error shape, and the peer seam exposes it as a separate hook so promotion's deliberate preemption is untouched. The refusal is a named startup failure — the same `FieldClaimFailed` a claim the field cannot answer produces — not a silent fence, because the operator-facing remedy is a launch flag (`--standby`), not a retry. The deferred-activation order decision 85 established is load-bearing here: the claim runs after every fallible local startup step, so a starter that cannot serve never lands a claim the field must fence.
- **Alternatives considered:** Fencing the restart silently — leave the process running gate-closed, role `standby` (rejected: a launched active that quietly cannot write is the unowned-line ambiguity decision 87 exists to name, and the refusal message is what turns a stale takeover into the deliberate rejoin the pair contract already supports); reconciling the stale checkpoint against the incumbent before claiming — a state merge or a freshness comparison (rejected: no rendezvous exists at claim time — the claim is the first field contact, and comparing checkpoint ticks proves nothing across generations; liveness is the only question the field can answer honestly); carrying a "this checkpoint supersedes" stamp so the incumbent ignores a reverted stream (rejected: the revert already happened on the restart's own image and field writes before any peer consumed it — the injury is at the claim, not the stream); journaling the revert as a named audit event while still taking the field (rejected: the issue's minimum bar — an audited revert still reverts protection commands the operator applied, and auditing a wrong actuation is not a mitigation); refusing on *any* standing different-owner claim, dead or live (rejected: it would kill decision 86's recorded recovery — a crashed owner's claim stands precisely so nothing else writes, and the restart that owns the model must still preempt it).
- **Consequences:** Exactly-one-writer gains a startup edge: a controller can no longer seize a field a live peer legitimately owns, so a stale checkpoint cannot silently roll back receipted commands on either peer — the reproduction's propagation path (fenced incumbent → demote → adopt the stale stream) never starts because the incumbent is never fenced. The receipt contract is strengthened, not changed: `applied` remains the durable verdict, now unreachable by this revert. The remedy for a refused restart is the documented `--standby` rejoin — the refused run's remedy is named in its own error — after which tracking adopts the incumbent's state, commands included. Two honest boundaries stand: the conditional grant cannot see *network* liveness — a partitioned-but-alive incumbent still reads as a dead owner to a restart on the far side, the same limitation the heartbeat budget already lives with — and backends without a live-holder query keep the unconditional startup claim, so the protection is sim-net-exact today and each future field kind inherits the seam to implement it. The `dcs-controller/tests/failover.rs` suite carries the regression: `a_stale_restart_is_refused_over_the_live_incumbent` scripts the QA reproduction end-to-end (frozen state file, applied protection commands, refused relaunch, truthful receipts, `--standby` reconvergence adopting the applied values), `a_misordered_promotion_degrades_the_superseded_active` and `a_restarted_superseded_active_is_refused_while_the_incumbent_holds` cover the refusal on both misordered and documented switchover paths, and `a_cold_restarted_source_resyncs_the_tracking_peer_without_rewinding` proves the dead-owner preempt still works — the restart-as-active recovery decision 86 names.
- **Affected tickets:** #797 (implemented — QA finding `stale-checkpoint-takeover-reverts-incumbent-commands`); amends the startup half of decision 86 (#562 — restart-as-active stays the recovery for a *dead* owner's standing claim); builds on decision 28's claim fencing (#65, #508, #515), decision 85's claim ordering and fail-closed field (#501, #528), decision 87's `ensure_writer` conditional-claim precedent (#778), and decision 11's checkpoint carry; the sim-net regression lives in `dcs-controller/tests/failover.rs`, with the wire op covered by `dcs-sim-net`'s protocol serde tests and the activation path by `dcs-runtime`'s peer tests.

### 90. The unclaimed field is a named served signal — `field_claim` on the role report

- **Status:** Implemented — the served vocabulary landed under #853 (`dcs-core` gains `FieldClaim` (`held`/`unclaimed`) and `RoleReport` gains the serde-optional `field_claim`) and the production half under #857: `Peer::with_field_probe` installs a read-only claim observation the peer runs once per scan, carried to the field as `dcs-sim-net`'s `PlantRequest::ProbeWriter` — `held` while an owner stands (the attachment's own hold or a fenced standing owner's, which the report need not distinguish), `unclaimed` while none does — and surfaced through `RemoteDriver::probe_writer` and `FanoutDriver::probe_field_claim` into `Driver::probe_field_claim`. Each answered observation replaces the cached `field_claim`; a failed probe is no observation, so the last answer stands and a never-answered probe reports `None`. The observation moves nothing else — role, `sync`, gate state, promotion eligibility, and the journal are untouched, and `promote` remains the unclaimed field's documented recovery. The surfacing half landed under #901: `PairFaultKind::FieldUnclaimed` (`field_unclaimed`) joined the versioned pair-fault vocabulary at `PAIR_FAULT_KINDS_VERSION = 3`, `PairClient::health` emits it while any reporting peer's `field_claim` stands `unclaimed` (clearing on the first poll after a holder claims), and the page's `pairHealth` names the same verdict while its peer row badges `unclaimed` reports — distinctly from the `orphaned`/`degraded` sync verdicts, which answer the tracked line's different question.
- **Problem:** The field's write-ownership arbitration has a state no served vocabulary names: *unclaimed* — no attachment holds the claim, the fail-closed window a fresh or restarted plant serves until a claim lands (decision 85's #528 rule) and the state a released claim leaves behind (decision 87's path one). The role report's existing vocabulary answers different questions: `role` says what the *run* does and `sync` says how far the *tracked line* converged — `StandbySync::Orphaned` names a tracked line whose serving run owns nothing, proven off the checkpoint's `source_owns_field` stamp — but nothing says whether the *field's own arbitration* holds a writer. The two observably differ: a dead owner's standing claim keeps the field held while both peers report `orphaned` (decision 87's path two), and a restarted field stands unclaimed while the tracked line may still report `tracking`. An operator's remedy for "the field stands unclaimed" is `promote` on a converged peer — the unconditional claim a promotion already runs — but no served signal names the state, so the visibility chain (role report → pair health → page) has nothing to carry. `WW-OPS-003` asks operators to see field-communication problems distinctly; `WW-LCM-001` asks the continuity surface to show which peer can take over.
- **Chosen approach (implemented):** `RoleReport` gains `field_claim: Option<FieldClaim>` under the served contract's optional-field convention — serde-defaulted and skipped when `None`, so a payload written before the field existed loads unchanged and re-serves without the key, `MODEL_VERSION` and the checkpoint format untouched (the report is served, never persisted). `FieldClaim` is the closed snake_case vocabulary the field's arbitration answers: `held` — an owner holds the claim, whether the reporting run's own grant or a standing owner a claim probe's `fenced` answer named — and `unclaimed` — no attachment holds it. `unclaimed` is the operator signal "field unclaimed: needs promote": on a promotable peer — a non-field-owning run whose `sync` reports a converged state — the documented remedy is `promote`, whose unconditional claim takes the field and lifts the gate; on a field-owning peer it reports the run's own claim lost, whose remedy is the driver's conditional re-arm, not promotion. `None` reports no observation — a driver surface without claim arbitration, a run that has not asked, or a report whose producer predates the vocabulary — and is deliberately distinct from `held`, so a consumer never reads "uninstrumented" as "an owner stands". The production half (#857) is a dedicated read-only probe rather than a mapping of answers the mutation hooks already see: `ProbeWriter` asks the claim surface for the verdict a mutation from this attachment would meet — without asserting, joining, or releasing — because a fenced write arrives too late to report an unclaimed field and the claim hooks run only at transitions. The pair-health/page presentation — landed under #901 — turns a reporting peer's `unclaimed` into the named `field_unclaimed` fault whose prose carries the needs-promote remedy.
- **Alternatives considered:** A `StandbySync` variant (rejected — `sync` is the tracked line's convergence, the promotability evidence `promote` gates on; the claim state is field-side arbitration observable on any role, active included, and folding it into `sync` would weld an unrelated observation onto the convergence verdict); an `IoHealth`/`DriverDiagnostics` section (rejected — the signal names a redundancy remedy, needs-promote, not driver-boundary health; `io_health` answers the producing run's own driver experience identically on either peer, while the promotability question is exactly what the role report exists for); a `PairFaultKind` entry only (rejected — pair health derives its verdicts from the peers' role reports, so without a served per-peer claim observation the view has nothing to read; a `field_unclaimed` fault kind remains the proposed surfacing half once production lands — it landed under #901); and an `Option<bool>` like the checkpoint's `source_owns_field` (rejected — a bare bool cannot grow the vocabulary and reads ambiguously on the wire; the named enum keeps `held`/`unclaimed` self-describing and leaves room for later arbitration states).
- **Consequences:** The served role contract grows additively: `GET /role` and the `POST /promote`/`POST /demote` answers carry `field_claim` only once a producer reports an observation, and consumers predating the field ignore it — serde-tolerant like every contract extension under decision 3's convention. The drift pin updates only where the wire type grows: `RoleReport`'s serde tests pin the legacy payload byte-identical and the grown shape spelling `field_claim` snake_case beside `role`/`sync`. `held` versus `None` separates "the field confirmed an owner" from "no claim question was answered" — the distinction the surfacing chain needs to keep uninstrumented drivers from reading as healthy. The unclaimed-field visibility chain's surfacing ticket — #901 — landed against this vocabulary rather than reopening the wire.
- **Affected tickets:** #853 (this record and the contract), #857 (the production half — the per-scan read-only probe, the peer's cached `field_claim`, and the driven-pair acceptance in `dcs-controller/tests/unclaimed_field.rs`), #901 (the surfacing half — the `field_unclaimed` pair-fault kind at vocabulary 3 and its page render); requirements `WW-OPS-003` and `WW-LCM-001`; relies on decision 3's optional-field convention, decision 19's role-report surface (#46, #63), decision 85's fail-closed unclaimed field (#528), and decision 87's orphan vocabulary and conditional-claim probes (#778).

### 91. A demoted peer must not island on a sibling standby — owner-preferred demote hints, propagated line ownership, and the conditional orphan claim

- **Status:** Implemented — #831 against QA finding `demoted-peer-adopts-standby-line-into-promotable-stale-island` (run `qax-20260922-008`, rig `lenovo`). Three contract pieces change on existing seams. First, the announced follow-peer slot becomes a bounded set (`MAX_ANNOUNCED`, newest-first): every `?peer=` announcer stays a demotion candidate instead of the last pull silently evicting the rest, and `verify_demote_hint` verifies each candidate — preferring one that serves the line *as its field owner* (`source_owns_field: true`), so a sibling standby whose checkpoint merely replays the demoted run's own line back can only ever be the provisional fallback, never the verified adoption over a real successor. Second, `Checkpoint::line_owner` — serve-time decoration like `line_proof`, never run state: a serving field owner stamps its own monitor address and a non-owning serving run propagates the owner the checkpoints it pulls carried, so a peer several tracking hops out still learns where the line's ownership lives. A tracking peer records the stamp per pull, and while its verdict is `Orphaned` the `Monitor::resolve_tracking_source` probe runs each track cycle — pulling one bounded checkpoint from the named owner, then the tracked source, then the announced set — accepting only a candidate whose document verifies as this line *and* claims the field (`verify_owner_checkpoint`; on a keyed run the `line_proof` too), then re-targeting the pulls through the `resolved` slot, which outranks configured, adopted, and announced sources alike. Third, an orphaned promotion's claim is the conditional grant: `Peer::with_field_orphan_claim` installs `claim_writer_unless_held`, which `promote`/`self_promote` run while `Orphaned` in place of the unconditional claim — granted over a dead, deliberately yielded, or tool-held claim, refused `FieldClaimFailed` with the gate still closed while a live *controller's* different-owner unyielded claim stands. The claim's `controller` marker — recorded on `ClaimWriter`/`EnsureWriter` grants, upgraded whenever a controller attachment joins the standing owner, and carried by every controller attachment (`RemoteDriver::as_controller`, wired at the `sim-tcp` backend and the `--remote` driver) — is what tells the island's live incumbent from the rogue `claim_writer` a peer's documented recovery exists to preempt: a field tool's hold is never an incumbent. Demotion keeps the field fenced through the handover: `ReleaseWriter{keep_claim:true}` drops the connection's hold but leaves the claim standing marked `yielded` — fencing the field, preemptable by a successor's conditional claim, distinguishable from a live incumbent — while the ex-owner's orphan-cycle re-arm probe runs `EnsureWriter{rebind:false}`, raising or confirming the claim for its token without the probing attachment ever counting as a live holder.
- **Problem:** With two standbys sharing one simulated field, a single announced-source slot made the last announcer the demotion's tracking source; `verify_demote_hint` proved only that the hint continued the run's line, which a subordinate standby trivially satisfies; the adopted source was pinned, so after a third peer took the field the demoted pair could never re-resolve — they orphaned-tracked each other indefinitely; and `StandbySync::Orphaned` was promotable on tracking evidence that cannot tell "the tracked line's owner is dead" from "the run lost track of a live incumbent", so an islanded peer's `POST /promote` returned 200, preempted the real owner's claim, and silently rolled back the incumbent's applied commands and energized outputs — the stale-checkpoint-takeover class reachable with no restart and no crash, the default outcome of a normal switchover on the three-controller topology.
- **Chosen approach:** Let the line name its owner rather than guess from tracking evidence. A standby's checkpoint can prove continuation but never ownership, so the demote verification treats ownership as a ranked property — verified owner candidates beat merely-tracking ones — while still adopting a tracker provisionally when no owner exists yet (the demote→promote gap the mutual-standby contract depends on). Re-resolution rides the checkpoints the peers already exchange: `line_owner` propagation gives even a multi-hop island the owner's address, and the probe re-verifies rather than trusting the stamp — the announce and adopted slots can only say who *tracks* the line, so only a pulled `source_owns_field: true` document on the line's generation earns the `resolved` slot. Promotion from the orphaned verdict asks the field's own arbitration the question tracking cannot answer: `claim_writer_unless_held` — the same conditional grant decision 89 built for the startup claim — preempts a dead or yielded claim but never a live different-owner's unyielded one, so the wedge escape decision 87 documented still works while the stale-island takeover does not. Two marks refine "live holder" into "live incumbent" — each closing a wedge the raw holder set cannot arbitrate. The yielded mark is what makes deliberate demotion distinguishable from a live incumbent: without it a demoted owner's lingering live holders (a field tool's attachments) would read as an incumbent and wedge legitimate orphan promotions, and a fully released claim would open an unclaimed window a foreign grab could race. The `controller` marker is what makes a rogue tool's claim distinguishable from a controller incumbent's: the claim-holder liveness the field can see cannot tell `claim_writer` on a mutation tool's attachment — the rogue hold the documented orphan-promote recovery exists to preempt — from a live controller serving the line, so the claim records the claimant's kind at grant time and the conditional refusal binds only to a live unyielded *controller* claim, while a controller attachment joining the standing owner upgrades the marker so a claim a controller stands behind always reads as one.
- **Alternatives considered:** Refusing demotion when no announced candidate owns the field (rejected — the documented demote→promote gap has no owner yet by definition, and the existing wildcard/spoofed-announce contract tests rely on adopting a not-yet-promoted standby); resolving islands purely by tracking topology — refuse adoption when the candidate's checkpoint reports `source_owns_field: false` (rejected alone — legalizes nothing the mutual-standby wedge needs, and the island would still form whenever no owner had announced); an unbounded re-resolution on any `?peer=` hint (rejected — the resolved slot is earned by a verified owner checkpoint, never by an announce, keeping the unauthenticated hint channel unable to redirect pulls); a new wire op or endpoint for ownership queries (rejected — `line_owner` rides the document every pull already fetches, and `claim_writer_unless_held` already answers the live-incumbent question at the only authority that knows it — the field itself); unconditional orphan promotion plus a post-hoc divergence gate (rejected — the preempt happens at the claim, before any comparison could run; the conditional claim is the only point where the field's arbitration can refuse).
- **Consequences:** A three-controller switchover can no longer produce a permanent stale island: the demoted pair either re-resolves onto the real owner — the fixture's observed path, reconverging `tracking` within a few scan cycles — or its promotion is refused `field_claim_failed` while a live incumbent's claim stands, never preempting it. The `Checkpoint` wire shape grows one optional field (`line_owner`, serde-tolerant like every contract extension; pre-stamping peers simply propagate nothing). The sim-net protocol gains three backward-compatible fields — `ReleaseWriter.keep_claim` and `EnsureWriter.rebind`, both defaulting to the old behavior, and `controller` on `ClaimWriter`/`EnsureWriter`, whose absent-flag decode reads `true` so a pre-flag claim keeps refusing conditional takeovers (the conservative verdict — a mislabeled tool claim only ever refuses a takeover a deliberate unconditional claim still runs) — and the demotion's keep-claim release closes the unclaimed window a full release would have opened while still letting a successor's conditional claim through. Peers built without the orphan-claim hook keep the unconditional claim — field kinds that cannot arbitrate live holders lose the refusal but keep the re-resolution. The acceptance fixture is `crates/dcs-controller/tests/stale_island.rs` (the full three-controller reproduction: announced pair, sibling adoption, islanded `POST /promote` refused, pair reconverged on the owner); unit coverage lives in `dcs-monitor`'s `verify_owner_checkpoint_rejects_a_standby_line_source`, `dcs-monitor/tests/tracking.rs`'s `an_announced_standby_line_loses_the_demote_hint_to_the_field_owner`, and `dcs-runtime`'s orphan-claim gate tests.
- **Affected tickets:** #831 (implemented — QA finding `demoted-peer-adopts-standby-line-into-promotable-stale-island`); amends decision 12's announce contract (#514, #684, #807 — the announced slot is now a bounded set and demote verification is owner-preferred), decision 15's demote resolution (#514), decision 28's fencing (#65, #508, #515 — the claim gains the yielded mark and the unbound probe), decision 87's orphaned-verdict contract (#778, #800 — orphan promotion is now conditionally claimed and the orphan cycle re-resolves), and decision 89's conditional grant (#797 — the same grant now also arms orphan promotion); relies on decision 84's `final_sync` and decision 85/86's claim-order and never-release rules (#501, #528, #562).

### 92. The involuntary demotion verifies announced hints lazily — a recorded `?peer=` announce is a candidate, never a pull target

- **Status:** Implemented — #873 against QA finding `involuntary-demote-unverified-announced-hint` (run `qax-20260923-005`, rig `lenovo`). `Monitor::tracking_source` keeps answering the recorded resolution — `resolved`, then the configured slots, then the pinned `adopted`, then the recorded `announced` front, which since #872 resolves only under the keyed contract — for diagnostics and the demote guard's "any candidate exists" test, but every cycle that actually fetches a checkpoint resolves through `Monitor::verified_tracking_source`, whose `pull_source` half deliberately omits the announced slot: the serving side cannot tell the puller's monitor port from any other same-source port, so a recorded hint is unproven by construction and no pull ever follows one verbatim. When no proven source stands and the peer owns no field on a keyed run — the announced-source contract is keyed-only outright (decision 12), so on an unkeyed run a bare hint is no tracking source at all and earns not even a verify pull — `adopt_announced_source` runs the demote verify's checks lazily over the recorded set — `probe_announced_hints`, the same prove-half `POST /demote`'s `verify_demote_hint` uses: one bounded pull per hint under `CHECKPOINT_PULL_TIMEOUT`, newest first, a verified field-owner winning outright, a merely-tracking candidate held as the provisional fallback, and every candidate answering this run's command audit. A passing hint pins into `adopted` and journals `TrackingSourceAdopted` exactly like the request path. When no hint proves a continuation, the same pass still gives the recorded set the orphan-resolution probe's owner check (`resolve_tracking_source`): a successor that already claimed the field serves a field-owning document the demote verify refuses unproven — the `source_owns_field` stamp is this run's own public one — but `verify_owner_checkpoint` proves it serves this line's field, so a promoted legitimate successor resolves where a dead or foreign endpoint cannot. A set failing both passes leaves the peer sourceless — the same answer `POST /demote` gives an unproven hint — rather than following one. The failed set is remembered in `announced_verify`, so a later cycle re-probes only a changed set or the same one once `ANNOUNCED_VERIFY_RETRY` (4s) has elapsed: a dead recorded hint falls back inside one bounded pass and earns a fresh probe inside a bounded window instead of either wedging the scan on repeated dead-end pulls or stranding the peer on it forever. The promotion path's final-sync fetch resolves through `pull_source` alone — the document whose receipt log is about to carry commands into the promoted run never comes from an unproven hint.
- **Problem:** `POST /demote` proved each announced hint before following it — verify, then pin — but the involuntary path consumed the same unproven record verbatim: a field claim's mid-run loss (`Peer::demote` on a fenced write) demotes the owner in place with no request boundary ever running the verify, so the demoted peer's `tracking_source` fell through to the `announced` front — a last-writer-wins slot validated only for source-IP equality at record time. On the QA rig a foreign monitor's routine `?peer=` pulls recorded its address on a launched active; when an external `claim_writer` preempted the field claim, the demoted peer's pulls targeted the interloper — 80 checkpoint pulls to the probe, zero to the legitimate peer — and after the probe died the recorded announce never expired and an active peer announces nothing, so no new hint displaced the dead address. Recovery needed operator action (demote the successor to force announces, then promote the demoted peer back).
- **Chosen approach:** Run the same verification, lazily, at the point a pull would consume the hint. The involuntary demotion crosses no request boundary, so the proof cannot live on one — the first sourceless tracking cycle after the demotion is the earliest point the run both knows it needs a successor and can spend bounded pulls, and the demote verify's checks are already the contract for "this endpoint serves this run's continuation". Reusing `probe_announced_hints` keeps the two paths symmetric by construction: the keyed-only gate, owner-preferred ranking, the fresh-nonce `line_proof` attestation, the replay/forgery refusals, and the command audit all apply identically, and the adoption journals and pins the same way — so a foreign announcer can never strand the demoted peer on it, the legitimate successor's hint wins on its own proof, and the operator-forced reconvergence the reproduction needed becomes automatic.
